The proposed methodology includes a convolutional neural network (CNN) encoder (or feature extractor) trained to learn domain-invariant representations by optimizing a combined objective: a cross-entropy loss on labeled simulation data and a Deep CORAL (covariance-alignment) loss between simulation data and unlabeled target-domain industrial data. Downstream classifiers trained on the domain-invariant target features were evaluated on an independent test set of 20 benchmark loops from industrial stiction data benchmark. The proposed methodology successfully diagnosed 18 out of the 20 loops and achieved100% recall across all 13 stiction cases, an accuracy of 90.00% and an F1-score of 92.86%. Compared to standard baseline approach (hand-crafted features-based method), the proposed methodology significantly mitigates domain shift, providing robust, highly reliable stiction detection for real-world industrial control loops.

</details>


### [52] [Twins: Learn to Predict Unified Representations with Focal Loss](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22531)
*Kaixiong Gong,Xin Cai,Bin Lin,Hao Wang,Yunlong Lin,Mingzhe Zheng,Bohao Li,Jian-Wei Zhang,Miles Yang,Zhao Zhong,Liefeng Bo,Xiangyu Yue*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Twins proposes a unified continuous token space for multimodal models by concatenating ViT and VAE features channel-wise on the same token grid, and addresses optimization imbalance between these components using a focal regression objective for flow matching.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing unified multimodal models suffer from mismatched latent spaces: discrete methods use a shared codebook but continuous pipelines rely on disparate representations (semantic ViT features for understanding vs. low-level VAE latents for synthesis), leading to optimization imbalance and degraded performance.

Method: Twins creates a unified continuous token space via channel-wise concatenation of ViT and VAE features on the same token grid (preserving sequence length and attention cost). It identifies three sources of optimization imbalance (frequency bias, intrinsic dimensionality, condition-aligned vs condition-independent uncertainty) and adapts a focal regression objective for flow matching that upweights large-error VAE dimensions.

Result: On ImageNet, Twins achieves up to 10.57 gFID improvement over naive MSE loss without classifier-free guidance. It performs competitively on multimodal understanding benchmarks and improves reconstruction fidelity, narrowing the gap between understanding- and generation-oriented representations.

Conclusion: The proposed unified token space with focal regression effectively balances optimization across ViT and VAE components, enabling a single continuous representation to support both multimodal understanding and image generation tasks.

Abstract: Unified multimodal models seek a shared visual token space that supports both multimodal understanding and image generation. Discrete methods unify the interface via a shared codebook, whereas continuous pipelines often rely on two disparate representations -- semantic features (e.g., ViT) for understanding and low-level latents (e.g., VAE) for synthesis -- resulting in mismatched latent spaces. We propose Twins, a unified continuous token space formed by channel-wise concatenating ViT and VAE features on the same token grid, so the sequence length is unchanged and attention cost does not increase. However, jointly modeling Twins in a Diffusion Transformer exposes a severe optimization imbalance: the model fits the ViT component well but struggles to match the VAE latent distribution. We trace this imbalance to three sources of heterogeneity: frequency bias, intrinsic dimensionality, and condition-aligned vs condition-independent uncertainty. To address it, we adapt a focal regression objective for flow matching that upweights large-error VAE dimensions, better balancing optimization across the ViT and VAE components. On ImageNet, this yields up to 10.57 gFID gain over naive MSE loss without classifier-free guidance. Twins also performs competitively on multimodal understanding benchmarks and improves reconstruction fidelity, narrowing the gap between understanding- and generation-oriented representations.

</details>


### [53] [SM4RT: Learning Structured Motion Geometry for 4D Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22534)
*Shing Ho J. Lin,Wenzhao Zheng,Dong Zhuo,Yuqi Wu,Jie Zhou,Jiwen Lu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SM4RT is a Geometry Foundation Model that extends monocular 3D reconstruction to 4D dynamic understanding by modeling scene motion as structured rigid-body transformations (6D twists in SE(3)) rather than independent point-wise displacements, using sparse motion bases with shared per-pixel assignment weights.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing motion perception methods treat motion as independent point-wise displacements (e.g., sparse tracking, dense flow), ignoring the physical reality that real-world objects obey rigid-body kinematics governed by SE(3), where points on the same object move collectively rather than in isolation.

Method: SM4RT introduces a Structure-of-Motion representation that decomposes scene dynamics into a compact set of motion bases (temporal sequences of 6D twists in SE(3)), with sparse time-shared per-pixel assignment weights to recover dense scene motion. It employs a parallel motion geometry encoder and decoder for joint inference of 3D geometry, world-coordinate motion, and scene kinematic structure from monocular RGB video in a single forward pass.

Result: SM4RT achieves strong motion reconstruction performance while preserving the geometric structure of scene motion.

Conclusion: By explicitly modeling motion with rigid-body kinematic structure (SE(3) transformations) rather than unstructured point-wise displacements, SM4RT advances monocular 4D dynamic understanding while maintaining geometric consistency of physical motion.

Abstract: Geometry Foundation Models (GFMs) have substantially advanced monocular 3D reconstruction, yet extending this capability to 4D dynamic understanding remains a fundamental challenge. Most existing motion perception methods (e.g., sparse tracking, dense point-wise flow) treat motion as independent point-wise displacements, ignoring the structured nature of physical motion. However, real-world objects usually obey rigid-body kinematics, and points thus usually move collectively, not in isolation. Motion itself possesses geometric structure: physical objects undergo a set of rigid-body transformations governed by SE(3), rather than unstructured point-wise displacements. Building on this insight, we propose SM4RT, a Structured Motion 4D Reconstruction Transformer for end-to-end 3D reconstruction and structured motion perception. SM4RT introduces Structure-of-Motion to represent scene dynamics, where scene motion is decomposed into a compact set of motion bases, each represented as a temporal sequence of 6D twists in SE(3). Dense scene motion is then recovered by sparse, time-shared per-pixel assignment weights over these bases, ensuring points on the same object share a common rigid-body motion trajectory. SM4RT introduces a parallel motion geometry encoder and decoder that jointly infer 3D geometry, world-coordinate motion, and scene kinematic structure in a single forward pass from monocular RGB video. SM4RT achieves strong motion reconstruction performance while preserving the geometric structure of scene motion.

</details>


### [54] [An Explainable FFT-Based Spatial-Frequency Fusion Framework for Deepfake Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.17441)
*Pamela Kirui,Cho Hyuk,Qingzhong Liu,Haodi Jiang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MSCA-FFT is a deepfake detection framework combining a fine-tuned Xception spatial branch with an FFT-based frequency branch, using cross-attention fusion and transformer encoders. It outperforms DCT-based and baseline models, with spectral and explainability evidence highlighting manipulation-sensitive facial regions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deepfake detection is critical for combating misinformation and identity fraud. Prior work shows spatial-frequency fusion improves detection, but existing DCT-based pipelines require inverse reconstruction to images. This paper aims to design a more effective fusion framework using FFT-based frequency analysis with cross-attention, avoiding reconstruction artifacts.

Method: MSCA-FFT consists of: (1) a spatial branch based on partially fine-tuned Xception; (2) a frequency branch that processes log-scaled FFT magnitude spectra through shallow convolutional layers, bypassing inverse reconstruction; (3) transformer encoders refining both representations; (4) cross-attention fusion; and (5) an MLP classifier for real/fake prediction.

Result: MSCA-FFT consistently outperforms DCT-based state-of-the-art spatial-frequency fusion methods and baseline models. Ablation studies confirm that FFT-based frequency features provide complementary spectral cues. Grad-CAM/LIME explanations highlight manipulation-sensitive regions (eyes, mouth, nose, facial boundaries), aligning with FFT-based frequency analysis.

Conclusion: The proposed FFT-based multi-scale cross-attention framework is more effective than DCT-based alternatives for deepfake detection, leveraging complementary spatial and frequency information without inverse reconstruction. The explainability analysis further validates that the model attends to semantically meaningful facial manipulation regions.

Abstract: Deepfake generation has raised growing concerns regarding digital media authenticity, misinformation, identity fraud, and public trust. Recent studies show that combining spatial and frequency features leads to stronger detection results than using independently. This paper presents MSCA-FFT, a Fast Fourier Transform (FFT)-based multi-scale cross-attention framework for image-level deepfake detection. The model combines a partially fine-tuned Xception spatial branch with an FFT-based frequency branch. The frequency branch processes the log-scaled FFT magnitude spectrum through shallow convolutional layers, avoiding inverse frequency-to-image reconstruction used in DCT-based pipelines. The spatial and frequency representations are refined by transformer encoders, fused through cross-attention, and passed to an MLP classifier for real/fake prediction. Experimental results show that MSCA-FFT achieves consistently higher performance than the DCT-based state-of-the-art spatial-frequency fusion method and the compared baseline models. The ablation study further indicates that the FFT-based frequency branch provides complementary spectral cues when fused with spatial features. In addition, FFT-based frequency analysis and Grad-CAM/LIME explanations show consistent evidence around manipulation-sensitive facial regions, including the eyes, mouth, nose, and facial boundaries.

</details>


<div id='cs.SE'></div>

# cs.SE [[Back]](#toc)

### [55] [From Obligation to Specification: A Survey on Validating EU AI Act Requirements in RE](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21608)
*T. Y. Emmy Lai,Sven Giesselbach,Matthias Koch,Héctor Allende-Cid*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This mixed-method study (N=25) investigates organizational readiness for EU AI Act compliance in Requirements Engineering and finds that while the regulation is deemed highly relevant, systematic processes for traceability and evidence management are lacking; LLM-based agentic tools are seen as promising but require human oversight and safeguards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The EU AI Act imposes new obligations on organizations developing AI systems regarding transparency, risk management, and traceability, but many organizations lack systematic RE processes to translate these into testable requirements and verifiable evidence.

Method: Mixed-method exploratory study combining expert interviews (N=10) and an online survey (N=15) with participants spanning RE, data science, development, and compliance roles.

Result: The EU AI Act is viewed as highly relevant, but structured mechanisms for capturing regulatory obligations, propagating updates, and maintaining lifecycle-wide traceability are often missing. LLM-based tools are seen as promising for mapping obligations to requirements and organizing evidence, but full automation raises strong concerns.

Conclusion: The authors outline minimum requirements for an EU AI Act-ready closed-loop approach, emphasizing the need for human-in-the-loop safeguards when using LLM-based validation tools.

Abstract: With the EU AI Act entering into force, organizations developing or operating AI systems face new obligations on transparency, risk management, and traceability. For Requirements Engineering (RE), these obligations must be translated into testable, auditable requirements and verifiable evidence. However, many organizations currently lack systematic processes to achieve this. We hypothesize that LLM-based agentic validation tools can support this translation, thereby helping to close this gap. We present a mixed-method exploratory study with expert interviews (N=10) and an online survey (N=15) to assess organizational preparedness for EU AI Act-oriented RE and perceptions of LLM-based, agentic closed-loop validation tools, with participants spanning RE, data science, development, and compliance roles. Our results show that, although the EU AI Act is viewed as highly relevant, structured mechanisms to capture regulatory obligations, propagate updates into projects, and maintain lifecycle-wide traceability and evidence are often missing. Participants see LLM-based tools as promising for mapping obligations to requirements, assessing coverage, and organizing evidence, but express strong concerns about full automation and stress the need for safeguards. Based on these findings, we outline minimum requirements for an EU AI Act-ready closed-loop approach.

</details>


### [56] [Tool-Guided Retrieval-Augmented Repair for Securing LLM-Generated C Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21641)
*Vidyut Sriram,Saatvik Pradhan,Suman Saha*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents an analysis-and-repair workflow combining compilation diagnostics, CodeQL static analysis, and KLEE symbolic execution with retrieval of prior repair patterns to improve the reliability and security of LLM-generated C code for embedded systems. Evaluated on 5,000 tasks, the approach significantly reduces compilation failures (e.g., 42%→22% for DeepSeek Coder 1.3B) and security defects (e.g., 49%→19% for CodeLlama 7B).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models frequently generate C code with security vulnerabilities and compilation errors, especially problematic for embedded and resource-constrained systems where safety is critical.

Method: An iterative analysis-and-repair workflow that combines compilation diagnostics, CodeQL static analysis, KLEE symbolic execution, and retrieval of prior repair patterns to refine LLM-generated C code.

Result: On 5,000 embedded C programming tasks: For CodeLlama 7B, security defects dropped from 49% to 19%, total CodeQL errors reduced 83.7% (15,088→2,463). For DeepSeek Coder 1.3B, compilation failures reduced from 42% to 22%, security defects from 35% to 15%.

Conclusion: Integrating lightweight analysis tools (compilation, static analysis, symbolic execution) with retrieval-based repair can substantially improve the safety and reliability of LLM-generated code for embedded development.

Abstract: Large language models can generate C code from natural-language descriptions, but resulting programs often contain security vulnerabilities and compilation errors, posing risks for embedded and resource-constrained systems. This work investigates how feedback and retrieval improve reliability of LLM-generated C code. We present an analysis-and-repair workflow that combines compilation diagnostics, CodeQL static analysis, and KLEE symbolic execution with retrieval of prior repair patterns for iterative refinement.
  Evaluated on 5,000 C programming tasks exercising embedded relevant vulnerabilities, baseline models show substantial reliability gaps, with compilation failure rates up to 46% and security defect rates up to 49%. Our approach improves both metrics. For CodeLlama 7B, security defect rates decrease from 49% to 19% and total CodeQL errors drop from 15,088 to 2,463 (83.7%). For DeepSeek Coder 1.3B, compilation failures are reduced from 42% to 22% and security defects from 35% to 15%. These results show that integrating lightweight analysis tools can improve the safety of LLM-generated code for embedded development.

</details>


### [57] [Vibe Coding in Software Development: A Multivocal Literature Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21652)
*Shahbaz Siddeeq,Muhammad Waseem,Kai-Kristian Kemell,Mika Saari,Jussi Rasku,Pekka Abrahamsson*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: A multivocal literature review on vibe coding (47 sources) reveals it is an iterative generation-evaluation-revision loop rather than one-shot prompting. Developer work shifts from writing code to specification, supervision, and validation. Productivity gains are reported in 45% of sources, but evidence on maintainability, long-term quality, and safeguard effectiveness remains limited. Strongest evidence is for prototyping/UI work; weakest for production, data-intensive, and safety-critical settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Knowledge of how vibe coding is defined, practiced, and governed is scattered across academic and practitioner sources, and existing reviews have not yet integrated both evidence streams.

Method: Multivocal literature review of peer-reviewed and grey literature following established guidelines. Searches spanned 2022 to October 2025. After screening, credibility assessment, and snowballing, 47 sources (28 peer-reviewed, 19 grey) were analyzed through descriptive mapping and thematic synthesis across eight research questions.

Result: Vibe coding is an iterative generation-evaluation-revision loop. Developer work shifts from writing code to specification, supervision, and validation. 21 of 47 sources (45%) report short-term productivity and time-to-prototype gains. Evidence on maintainability, long-term quality, and safeguard effectiveness is limited. Evidence is strongest for prototyping/UI work and weakest for production, data-intensive, and safety-critical settings. Tool visibility does not imply effectiveness.

Conclusion: This is one of the first reviews to integrate peer-reviewed and grey literature on vibe coding under a single documented protocol. Future work should evaluate safeguard effectiveness, study session-level dynamics and long-term maintainability, and test vibe coding in production, data-intensive, and safety-critical settings.

Abstract: Vibe coding is a software development practice in which developers state intent in natural language and large language models generate code. It is often framed as one-shot prompting, but the evidence describes an intent-driven, iterative workflow whose outcomes depend on how generated code is evaluated and governed. Knowledge of how vibe coding is defined, practiced, and governed is scattered across academic and practitioner sources, and, to our knowledge, existing reviews have not yet integrated both evidence streams. We conducted a multivocal literature review of peer-reviewed and grey literature following established guidelines. Searches spanned 2022 to October 2025. After screening, credibility assessment, and snowballing, 47 sources were retained (28 peer-reviewed and 19 grey) and analyzed through descriptive mapping and thematic synthesis across eight research questions. Vibe coding is consistently described as an iterative generation-evaluation-revision loop rather than a one-shot activity, and developer work shifts from writing code towards specification, supervision, and validation. Short-term productivity and time-to-prototype gains are reported in 21 of 47 sources (45%), while evidence on maintainability, long-term quality, and safeguard effectiveness remains limited. Evidence is strongest for prototyping and user-interface work and weakest for production, data-intensive, and safety-critical use, and tool visibility does not imply effectiveness. This is one of the first reviews to integrate peer-reviewed and grey literature on vibe coding under a single documented protocol. Future work should evaluate safeguard effectiveness, study session-level dynamics and long-term maintainability, and test vibe coding in production, data-intensive, and safety-critical settings.

</details>


### [58] [Cross-Model LLM Code Review: Should you use Claude to review Codex or vice versa?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21656)
*Zuodong Xiang,Yike Zhang,YueMing Zhang,Hailu Xu*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Claude reviewing Codex drafts significantly improves pass rates (71.6%→89.7%), while Codex reviewing Claude drafts harms performance (91.4%→82.8%). The pairing is asymmetric: use Claude to review Codex, not vice versa.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether the common practice of pairing two coding agents (writer + reviewer) is cost-effective and whether the ordering of the pairing matters.

Method: Controlled experiment on 116 LCv2 hard/medium tasks with Claude and Codex across six conditions (solo baselines, cross-model orderings, same-model orderings), where the reviewer sees the problem and draft but cannot execute tests.

Result: Claude reviewing Codex: 71.6%→89.7% (p=0.001); Codex self-review: 71.6%→84.5% (p=0.022); Codex reviewing Claude: 91.4%→82.8% (p=0.046); Claude self-review: 91.4%→91.4% (no change).

Conclusion: The useful pairing is asymmetric: use Claude to review Codex, not the other way around.

Abstract: Developers increasingly use two coding agents together: one writes a draft, and the other reviews it. However, it is not clear whether the pairing is worth its cost and time, or whether the order of the pairing matters. We run a controlled experiment on 116 recent hard and medium lcb tasks with Claude and Codex across six conditions to approximate a software practitioner's workflow: both solo baselines, both cross-model orderings, and both same-model orderings. The reviewer sees the problem and the writer's draft but cannot execute tests, which approximates a code review step. Claude review raises Codex drafts from 71.6% to 89.7% ($p_{BH}=.001$); Codex self review raises them to 84.5% ($p_{BH}=.022$). The reverse direction does not pay off: Codex reviewing Claude drafts drops the pass rate from 91.4% to 82.8% ($p_{BH}=.046$), and Claude self review leaves the 91.4% baseline unchanged. Our evaluation indicates that the useful pairing is asymmetric: use Claude to review Codex, not the other way around.

</details>


### [59] [Pixels for Programs? A Cross-Provider Case Study of Input-Token Accounting for Source Code as Text and Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21672)
*Ronak Bhalgami*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: A measurement study comparing how commercial API providers (Anthropic, OpenAI, Google Vertex AI) count input tokens for code rendered as images versus raw source text, finding that image-based representation reduces reported tokens by 75-86% on average, though individual providers show vastly different break-even behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long code contexts consume many tokens, motivating the use of image-based code rendering for vision-language models. However, it's unclear how commercial APIs actually count tokens for such image inputs, which directly affects cost and feasibility.

Method: Systematic measurement study with 675 paired text/image requests across 5 programming languages, 9 source lengths (20-2000 lines), and 15 model aliases from 3 providers. Provider-reported input tokens were collected and analyzed for image-to-text token ratios.

Result: Aggregate image-to-text token ratios: Anthropic 0.135 (86.5% reduction), OpenAI 0.194 (80.6% reduction), Google Vertex AI 0.242 (75.8% reduction). However, Gemini images required 6.95x more tokens at 20 lines and only crossed below text at ~200 lines. Non-monotonic Gemini image accounting across page boundaries was also observed.

Conclusion: Image-based code rendering can substantially reduce reported input tokens for some but not all providers; billing behavior varies significantly and includes counterintuitive patterns. The study does not address task accuracy or latency.

Abstract: Long source-code contexts consume many text tokens, motivating the proposal to render code as images for vision-language models. Recent work asks whether models can still solve code tasks after this transformation. We examine a different systems question: how commercial APIs count the resulting requests. We present a reproducible measurement case study of provider-reported input tokens for raw source text and a compact rendered-image representation. The benchmark pairs requests across five programming languages, nine source lengths from 20 to 2,000 lines, and 15 available model aliases exposed by Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google Vertex AI. These aliases collapse to approximately five distinct accounting signatures and are not independent model replications. Across 675 complete text/image pairs, aggregate image-to-text ratios are 0.135, 0.194, and 0.242, corresponding to reported input-token reductions of 86.5\%, 80.6\%, and 75.8\%, respectively. These totals conceal materially different break-even behavior: Anthropic and OpenAI images receive lower counts at every tested size, while Gemini images require 6.95 times as many tokens at 20 lines and cross below text only at 200 lines in the aggregate. A targeted audit also reproduces non-monotonic Gemini image accounting across a page boundary. This study measures black-box request accounting for one compact rendering pipeline. It does not measure semantic fidelity, task accuracy, latency, monetary cost, or coding-agent efficiency. We release the scripts, revision-pinned corpus specification, raw usage records, validators, and deterministic analysis needed to reproduce and extend the study.

</details>


### [60] [Output Format x Model Identity: Interaction Effects in Single-Round Coding Agent Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21674)
*Yang Yang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Output format (full file, JSON Patch, unified diff) significantly impacts coding agent success rates, with no universally optimal format. In 4,013 runs across 3 models and 4 projects, Doubao excels with JSON Patch (94%), DeepSeek with unified diff (66%), and Qwen with full file (50%). Format misuse (e.g., one-line fix applied as full-file replacement) is a distinct failure mechanism.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether output format (a seemingly neutral implementation detail) systematically affects coding agent performance, model rankings, and success/failure outcomes.

Method: Controlled single-round experiment: 3 models (DeepSeek V4, Doubao 2.0 Pro, Qwen 3.7 Max) × 3 output formats (full file, JSON Patch, unified diff) × 6 tasks × 20 repetitions, totaling 4,013 runs across 4 open-source projects (tqdm, dotenv, requests, jsoup).

Result: Only tqdm yielded non-zero success rates; dotenv, requests, and jsoup had 0 successes in 2,551 runs. Doubao achieved 94% success with JSON Patch (Cohen's h=1.57, p<0.001), DeepSeek 66% with unified diff (h=0.63), Qwen 50% with full file (h=0.29, p<0.05). Format misuse (over-scope application) identified as distinct failure mechanism.

Conclusion: No universally optimal output format exists; a format × model interaction governs performance. Proposes model-specific output strategies and tool-design principles constraining format semantics to the agent's own localization step.

Abstract: Output format is not a neutral implementation detail -- it can reorder model rankings, amplify or suppress individual model differences, and determine whether a coding agent succeeds or fails. We conducted a controlled single-round experiment with 3 models (DeepSeek V4, Doubao 2.0 Pro, Qwen 3.7 Max) x 3 output formats (full file, JSON Patch, unified diff) x 6 tasks x 20 repetitions, totaling 4,013 runs across 4 open-source projects. Only one project (tqdm) yielded non-zero success rates: dotenv, requests, and jsoup yielded zero successes in 2,551 runs. Our central finding is a format x model interaction with no universally optimal format. Doubao achieves 94% success with JSON Patch (Cohen's h = 1.57, p < 0.001), DeepSeek excels at unified diff (66%, h = 0.63), and Qwen shows a small but significant full-file preference (50%, h = 0.29, p < 0.05). Beyond these headline results, we identify a distinct failure mechanism -- format misuse -- where agents correctly diagnose a problem but execute it with excessive scope, most vividly when a one-line fix is applied as a full-file replacement. We propose a model-specific output strategy, a tool-design principle that constrains format semantics to the agent's own localization step, and release all data and templates for reproducibility. All experimental data are available at https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.21505157.

</details>


### [61] [Directed Symbolic Execution for Vulnerability Discovery: An LLM-Guided Approach in KLEE](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21676)
*Lingfeng Chen,Tao Xiao,Masanari Kondo,Yasutaka Kamei*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: KLEECopilot is an LLM-guided symbolic execution engine built on KLEE that uses LLMs to mark potentially vulnerable code and prioritize path exploration while integrating loop-exit prioritization to escape cycles. It improves coverage by 42-125% and discovers 87 unique violations, outperforming baselines like Empc.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Symbolic execution suffers from path explosion, and path prioritization heuristics (e.g., in KLEE) can become trapped in cyclic control-flow regions, exhausting the exploration budget before reaching vulnerable code beyond those cycles.

Method: KLEECopilot integrates LLMs to mark potentially vulnerable code and guide path prioritization, combined with loop-exit prioritization to escape non-vulnerable cycles and progress toward deeper vulnerabilities.

Result: Improves basic block coverage by 42.24% and line coverage by 125.82% over baselines. Discovers 1,335 total violations and 87 unique violations, outperforming second-best baseline by 32.2% in total violations and Empc by 24.3% in unique violations. Sensitive to model family but marginally sensitive to model scale.

Conclusion: Integrating security semantics via LLMs and loop-exit prioritization is effective for directed symbolic execution. Ablation studies confirm individual components contribute to effectiveness, with alternative configurations yielding only 54-61 unique violations.

Abstract: Symbolic execution effectively discovers security violations but suffers from path explosion. Engines like KLEE therefore use path prioritization heuristics to order state exploration, typically optimizing code coverage. However, path prioritization can become trapped in cyclic control-flow regions, where repeated branching consumes the exploration budget before exploration reaches vulnerable code beyond these cyclic regions. We propose KLEECopilot, a Large Language Model (LLM)-guided directed symbolic execution approach built on KLEE. KLEECopilot uses LLMs to mark potentially vulnerable code and guide path prioritization. It also integrates loop-exit prioritization to escape potentially non-vulnerable cycles and progress toward deeper vulnerabilities. Compared with baselines such as Empc, KLEECopilot improves basic block coverage by 42.24% and line coverage by 125.82%. It discovers 1,335 total violations and 87 unique violations, outperforming the second-best baseline by 32.2% in total violations and Empc by 24.3% in unique violations. Although KLEECopilot is sensitive to model family, it exhibits only marginal sensitivity to model scale, supporting the efficacy of integrating security semantics and loop-exit prioritization. Ablation studies further show that individual components contribute to effectiveness: alternative configurations involving searchers, internal components, marking sources, and prompt variants yield only 54--61 unique violations, while KLEECopilot maintains competitive code coverage.

</details>


### [62] [Enhancing SLMs for Sustainable Code Optimization in Radio-Astronomy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21677)
*Elisa Chiarotto,Jingbo Li,P. Chris Broekema,Rob V. van Nieuwpoort*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper proposes using Small Language Models (SLMs) enhanced with multi-sampling generation and compiler feedback to optimize large-scale scientific code for the LOFAR radio telescope upgrade, achieving 40x computational efficiency gains while maintaining energy sustainability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The LOFAR telescope upgrade will increase computational requirements 40-fold, and existing optimization/porting to accelerators across a very large codebase is daunting. Additionally, the solution must not increase energy budget, and traditional LLM-based approaches are themselves energy-intensive, creating a sustainability paradox.

Method: The authors propose using SLMs instead of LLMs to minimize environmental impact, and enhance SLMs via two strategies: (1) multi-sampling generation strategy, and (2) incorporating compiler feedback into the generation loop. The approach is generic and can also incorporate RAG plus static/dynamic analysis tools.

Result: Multi-sampling SLMs can match or surpass larger single-generation models with fewer computational resources. Feeding compiler output back into SLMs leads to consistent improvements across all tested models.

Conclusion: Agentic AI-enhanced SLMs offer a sustainable, effective alternative to LLMs for large-scale scientific code optimization, balancing performance gains with energy efficiency.

Abstract: Recent Large Language Models (LLMs) can produce and optimize complex code. We investigate the use of LLMs to generate and optimize code for large-scale sciences, focusing on radio astronomy and sustainability. The LOFAR telescope is currently being upgraded, significantly increasing the sky area observed, while simultaneously processing more data faster. However, this is expected to increase the computational requirements 40-fold. This upgrade thus critically depends on rigorous performance optimization of existing software and widespread adoption of accelerators. The code base is very large, making this a daunting task. We therefore investigate and demonstrate an AI-driven approach meant to assist developers in evaluating and optimizing their code, including porting to hardware accelerators. The LOFAR community is committed to sustainable solutions, and needs to achieve these improvements without increasing the energy budget. We thus need to optimize existing codes or port them to accelerators, while making sure that the optimization process itself is also energy efficient. This poses a challenge, since LLMs are energy-intensive. We therefore propose to use Small Language Models (SLMs) instead to limit environmental impact. In this paper, we show how to enhance SLMs through the use of agentic AI. We extend the SLMs in two ways to improve code generation quality and performance: first with a multi-sampling generation strategy and second with incorporating compiler feedback. We demonstrate that multi-sampling SLMs can match or surpass larger single-generation models with fewer computational resources and that feeding compiler output back into the SLMs leads to consistent improvements across all tested models. Our approach is generic, and can also use Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) as well as static and dynamic analysis tools in the code generation pipeline.

</details>


### [63] [When Model Release Meets Model Reuse: Producer-Consumer Misalignment in Hugging Face](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21738)
*Adekunle Ajibode,Oussama Ben Sghaier,Bram Adams,Ahmed E. Hassan*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: A dual-perspective survey of 50 Hugging Face producers and 95 GitHub consumers reveals significant misalignments in model discovery, documentation practices, lineage tracing, and governance adoption within PTLM supply chains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore the previously unstudied human expectations, processes, and interactions underlying misalignment between PTLM producers' release practices and consumers' model reuse needs.

Method: Dual-perspective survey of 50 Hugging Face producers and 95 GitHub consumers across four AI supply chain dimensions: model discovery, documentation practices, lineage tracing, and model governance adoption.

Result: Producers and consumers disagree on where critical metadata should be recorded; producers trace lineage for provenance/reproducibility while consumers do so for quality/reliability; producers favor governance mechanisms that streamline release, while consumers prioritize documentation and dependency transparency.

Conclusion: Findings highlight opportunities to improve model documentation conventions, lineage visibility, and governance support in PTLM supply chains.

Abstract: Pre-trained Language Models (PTLMs) are increasingly reused as dependencies in modern software systems, even though prior work has documented persistent structural problems in PTLM supply chains, such as inconsistent release practices, incomplete metadata, and divergence between Hugging Face and GitHub repositories. One previously unexplored angle on these problems is that misalignment between PTLM producers' release practices and consumers' model reuse needs may explain several of these challenges, yet the human expectations, processes and interactions underneath this misalignment have never been studied. As such, we conducted the first dual-perspective survey of 50 Hugging Face producers and 95 GitHub consumers to examine producer-consumer misalignments across four AI supply chain dimensions: model discovery, documentation practices, lineage tracing, and model governance adoption. We find that, although producers and consumers rely on the same documentation artifacts, they disagree on where critical metadata should be recorded. Even though 27.9% of producers and 31.4% of consumers trace lineage beyond the immediate parent model, producers primarily trace model lineage for provenance and reproducibility, whereas consumers are mainly driven by quality and reliability concerns. Furthermore, producers believe that governance mechanisms that streamline model release would have the greatest positive impact, whereas consumers prioritize documentation and dependency transparency. These findings highlight opportunities to improve model documentation conventions, lineage visibility, and governance support in PTLM supply chains.

</details>


### [64] [How Do AI Coding Agents Contribute to Software Development? an Empirical Study of Agentic Pull Requests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21832)
*Iren Mazloomzadeh,Mohammad Mehdi Morovati,Foutse Khomh*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This study empirically analyzes AI coding agent contributions (via pull requests) compared to human-generated PRs across the software development lifecycle, examining merge rates, task distributions, and software quality characteristics over time using the AIDev dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While AI coding agents are increasingly adopted in software engineering, their impact on software quality and how their contributions evolve across the development lifecycle remain insufficiently understood.

Method: Using the AIDev dataset, the study analyzes merge rate differences between agentic and human PRs over time, identifies the types of development tasks where AI agents are applied and how these distributions evolve across development quarters, and compares key characteristics of agentic vs. human PRs focusing on software quality implications and temporal dynamics.

Result: The findings provide an empirical and longitudinal perspective on AI coding agents' role, revealing differences in merge rates, task distributions, and software quality characteristics between agentic and human contributions, and how these properties change over the development lifecycle.

Conclusion: The study offers a more nuanced understanding of the benefits and limitations of AI coding agents in real-world software development practices, grounded in longitudinal empirical evidence.

Abstract: Recent advances in large language models and their rapid adoption across software engineering tasks have made Artificial Intelligence (AI) coding agents an integral component of modern software development workflows. While developers increasingly benefit from these coding agents, their impact on software quality remains insufficiently understood. In particular, how agentic contributions evolve across the software development lifecycle has not been thoroughly investigated. This study aims to characterize agentic pull requests (PR) in comparison to human generated PRs and to examine how their properties change across different stages of the development lifecycle. Using the AIDev dataset, we first analyze how differences in merge rates between agentic and human generated PRs vary over time. We then identify the types of development tasks where AI coding agents are predominantly applied and investigate how these task distributions evolve across development quarters. Finally, we compare a set of key characteristics of agentic and human generated PRs, focusing on their implications for software quality and their temporal dynamics. Overall, our findings provide an empirical and longitudinal perspective on the role of AI coding agents in software development, offering a more nuanced understanding of their benefits and limitations in real-world practices.

</details>


### [65] [No Snake Oil: Verifying Python Package Builds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21888)
*Jens Dietrich,Spencer Sun,Tim W. White,Behnaz Hassanshahi*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper analyzes 12,180 popular PyPI releases, finding low byte-for-byte equivalence rates in automated rebuilds, and proposes daleq4py, a tool using datalog-based normalization to establish wheel equivalence, increasing accepted rebuilds from ~15-19% to 60-79%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Python packages distributed via PyPI need supply chain analysis to detect compromised builds. Independent rebuilds in hardened environments can generate provenance and increase trust, but existing tools face low byte-for-byte equivalence rates, motivating the need for a more flexible equivalence mechanism.

Method: The authors study 12,180 popular PyPI releases using two rebuild tools (macaron and oss-rebuild). They analyze reasons for non-equivalent wheels and propose daleq4py, which establishes equivalence through a normalization function using provenance-preserving datalog rules to compare wheel kernels.

Result: Only 15.4% of macaron rebuilds and 19.1% of oss-rebuild rebuilds are byte-for-byte identical. daleq4py establishes wheel equivalence for 60.2% and 78.9% of source-equivalent rebuilds respectively, substantially expanding acceptable rebuilds.

Conclusion: Byte-for-byte equivalence is rare in practice but wheel equivalence can often still be established, preserving most security guarantees. daleq4py effectively bridges the gap between raw rebuild results and trustworthy provenance verification at scale.

Abstract: Python has become the default language for interacting with AI, with packages being distributed through registries like the Python Package Index (PyPI). This creates a need to analyse supply chains comprising such packages. One such analysis is to rebuild packages in order to identify compromised builds injecting malware. Independent rebuilds in hardened environments have the added advantage that they can generate and record provenance in order to increase the trustworthiness of packages. Two tools that are designed to automate such rebuilds and run them at scale are macaron and oss-rebuild. We study 12,180 popular releases from PyPI and find that the byte-for-byte equivalence rate is generally low. We analyse the reasons why they produce different wheels, and find that equivalence between the original and rebuilt wheels can often still be established, preserving most of the guarantees users expect from rebuildable releases. We present and evaluate daleq4py, a tool to establish the equivalence of Python wheels through the kernel of a normalisation function that is based on provenance-preserving datalog rules. Experimental results show that daleq4py substantially expands the set of rebuilds that can be accepted as equivalent. Although only 15.4% of macaron rebuilds and 19.1% of oss-rebuild rebuilds are byte-for-byte identical to the published PyPI wheels, daleq4py establishes wheel equivalence for 60.2% and 78.9% of source-equivalent rebuilds, respectively.

</details>


### [66] [Claim Plane: Enforceable Change Intents and Dynamic Scope for Parallel Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21909)
*Maxim Nikolaev*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Claim Plane is a model-agnostic coordination architecture for parallel coding agents that treats concurrent software change as a pre-write admission problem. Workers declare versioned ChangeIntents (with base commit, resource types, dependencies, and committed/contingent operations) before implementation. A deterministic control plane atomically admits compatible intents, constrains parallelism, handles overlap and invalidation, and fails closed on ambiguity. A preliminary 6-pair CooperBench test shows feasibility but is too small for comparative claims.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches to parallel coding agents suffer from integration-time interference, scope creep, and reliance on invalidated premises. Current solutions (communication, isolated workspaces, late repair, supervision, runtime recovery) are insufficient.

Method: Claim Plane architecture with: (1) pre-write admission control via versioned ChangeIntent declarations, (2) deterministic control plane for atomic intent admission, same-file parallelism constraints, serialization of unresolved overlap, dependency invalidation tracking, and fail-closed semantics, (3) dynamic scope promotion where contingent mutations trigger atomic re-admission, (4) brokered execution with capability binding to intent versions, leases, OS worktree locks, fencing tokens, and Git-tree provenance.

Result: Preliminary CooperBench 6-pair mechanism check: static Claim Plane achieved 6/6 pair passes with full serialization; dynamic scope retained parallel admission on 3/6 pairs, performed 7 successful scope promotions, and failed closed on 2 undeclared mutations. Sample too small for comparative claims.

Conclusion: Separating probabilistic planning from deterministic authority enables a foundation for future learned semantic-dependency models and frontier-model escalation only on unresolved cases.

Abstract: Parallel coding agents can independently produce locally valid changes while still interfering at integration time, expanding beyond planned scope, or relying on premises invalidated by concurrent work. Existing responses emphasize communication, isolated workspaces, late merge-time repair, continuous supervision, or post-hoc runtime recovery. This paper presents Claim Plane, a model-agnostic coordination architecture that treats concurrent software change as a pre-write admission problem. Before implementation, each worker declares a versioned ChangeIntent containing an exact base commit, typed resources, dependencies, and operations marked as committed or contingent. A deterministic control plane atomically admits compatible intents, constrains same-file parallelism to declared regions, serializes unresolved overlap, tracks dependency invalidation, and fails closed on ambiguous authority. During execution, a contingent mutation does not reserve write ownership initially; the first attempted mutation triggers atomic scope promotion and re-admission against the current active set. Brokered execution binds capabilities to intent versions, leases, OS-level worktree locks, monotonic fencing tokens, and Git-tree provenance, while integration verifies immutable patches and evidence. A preliminary six-pair CooperBench mechanism check is reported only as feasibility evidence: static Claim Plane achieved 6/6 pair passes with full serialization, while dynamic scope retained parallel admission on half of the pairs, performed seven successful scope promotions, and failed closed on two undeclared mutations. The sample is intentionally too small for comparative claims. We argue that separating probabilistic planning from deterministic authority provides a foundation for a future learned semantic-dependency model and frontier-model escalation only on unresolved cases.

</details>


### [67] [Leveraging Resolved Incident History for LLM-Assisted Software Bug Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21911)
*Boyuan Guan,Hailu Xu,Jamie Rogers*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: OM-RAG indexes resolved software issues as structured symptom-root cause-resolution triples and retrieves via single-hop embedding, outperforming chunk-based and graph-based RAG approaches in diagnosis and fix accuracy over a six-month production deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RAG approaches for software bug diagnosis fail to provide both the right knowledge source (operational failure history) and the right retrieval structure (structured records), limiting their effectiveness.

Method: OM-RAG indexes resolved issues as structured (symptom, root cause, resolution) triples, retrieves similar historical precedents via single-hop embedding, and powers an LLM administrator within a MAPE-K feedback loop, deployed on a production Dataverse instance for over six months.

Result: OM-RAG achieves Diagnosis Accuracy of 0.931 and Fix Correctness of 0.809, outperforming chunk-based retrieval by 186% and a documentation-anchored concept graph by 77%, with mean similarity of 0.880.

Conclusion: Structured operational failure history with single-hop embedding retrieval significantly improves automated bug diagnosis and fix correctness over unstructured or documentation-based RAG approaches.

Abstract: Effective software bug diagnosis requires two ingredients: the right knowledge source (operational failure history, not just system documentation) and the right retrieval structure (structured records, not unstructured chunks). Current retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) approaches fall short on one or both dimensions. We propose Operational Memory RAG (OM-RAG), which indexes resolved issues as structured symptom-root cause-resolution triples and retrieves the most similar historical precedent via single-hop embedding. OM-RAG powers a purpose-built large language model (LLM) administrator that has operated a production Dataverse instance for over six months, operationalizing the Knowledge component of the Monitor-Analyze-Plan-Execute-Knowledge (MAPE-K) feedback loop with the system's own resolved failure history. In a controlled four-configuration ablation, LLM-based judging covers all 1,172 issues and manual verification a 123-issue subset. OM-RAG achieves Diagnosis Accuracy of 0.931 and Fix Correctness of 0.809, outperforming chunk-based retrieval (+186%) and a documentation-anchored concept graph (+77%). Model-free retrieval metrics (mean similarity 0.880) independently corroborate the ranking.

</details>


### [68] [KaPilot: LLM-Assisted Generation of Kani Specifications for Unsafe Rust Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21957)
*Minghua Wang,Yuxi Ling,Mingzhi Gao,Yuwei Liu,Lin Huang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: KaPilot is a multi-agent LLM framework that automatically generates formal specifications to verify memory safety in unsafe Rust code using Kani, achieving 88.9% success on functions with ground truth and 71.4% without, outperforming AutoSpec by 14.8% in verifiable specifications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Unsafe Rust code poses memory safety risks despite Rust's ownership guarantees. Formal verification can help but writing precise specifications is challenging and manual. Existing LLM-based approaches are code-centric, inherit implementation flaws, and lack systematic quality assessment.

Method: KaPilot uses a multi-agent framework: (1) lightweight program analysis and proof harness generation; (2) SafetyReq agent extracts safety requirements from documentation; (3) SpecGenerate agent produces initial specifications; (4) iterative generate-precheck-verify loop with SpecGenerate, SpecPrecheck, and SpecVerify agents; (5) shuffle-and-implication strategy to select the best specification from candidates.

Result: Evaluated on 54 unsafe Rust functions with ground truth (88.9% success) and 70 without (71.4% success). 57.4% of generated specifications were equivalent to or stronger than ground truth. Outperforms AutoSpec by 14.8% in verifiable specifications and 25.9% in equivalent-or-better specifications.

Conclusion: KaPilot demonstrates that a multi-agent LLM approach with iterative refinement and systematic candidate selection can effectively automate specification generation for verifying unsafe Rust memory safety, outperforming existing code-centric approaches.

Abstract: Rust's ownership and type system provide strong memory safety guarantees, but unsafe code still presents memory safety risks. Formal verification is crucial for ensuring memory safety, but writing precise specifications for unsafe Rust is challenging and largely manual. Large language models (LLMs) have shown promise in generating formal specifications but are often code-centric, prone to inheriting implementation flaws, and lack systematic quality assessment.
  In this paper, we present KaPilot, a multi-agent framework for automatically generating specifications to verify unsafe Rust memory safety using Kani. The process begins with lightweight program analysis and proof harness generation. The SafetyReq agent extracts a concise, refined list of safety requirements from the target Rust function's documentation, which guides the SpecGenerate agent in producing initial specifications that specify memory safety concerns. Then, the specifications are iteratively refined through a generate-precheck-verify loop involving SpecGenerate, SpecPrecheck, and SpecVerify agents, which assess quality and feed errors back. By executing this loop multiple times, KaPilot generates a set of candidate specifications. Finally, the shuffle-and-implication strategy is applied to systematically determine the best specification from these candidates. We evaluated KaPilot on 54 unsafe Rust functions with ground truth and 70 without. KaPilot achieved 88.9% and 71.4% specification generation success, respectively, with 57.4% of generated specifications equivalent to or stronger than the ground truth. Compared with AutoSpec, KaPilot produces 14.8% more verifiable specifications and 25.9% more equivalent-or-better specifications.

</details>


### [69] ["Go Home Copilot, You're Drunk": Understanding Developer Responses to Agent-Generated Code Review Comments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21997)
*Shamse Tasnim Cynthia,Ratnadira Widyasari,Banani Roy,Ting Zhang,David Lo*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This large-scale empirical study analyzes 54,791 code review comments generated by five AI coding agents (Copilot, Cursor, Codex, Devin, Claude) across 342 Python repositories, finding that resolution rates vary significantly by agent (Copilot leads at 72.9%), core developers handle most design/evolvability comments while peripheral developers address functional defects, and the strongest predictor of comment resolution is the presence of inline code suggestions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite the increasing use of AI coding agents to generate code review comments, there is little understanding of how developers actually respond to such agent-generated feedback in practice.

Method: Empirical analysis of 54,791 agent-generated review comments from five coding agents across 342 Python repositories on GitHub, examining resolution rates, developer experience roles, and comment characteristics through quantitative analysis and open card sorting of 470 unresolved discussions.

Result: Resolution rates vary considerably across agents; Copilot accounts for 72.9% of resolved comments; core developers resolve most design/evolvability comments while peripheral developers resolve functional defects; ten discussion patterns for unresolved comments were identified, with incorrect suggestions and intentional design decisions being most prevalent; inline code suggestions are the strongest predictor of resolution, while lengthy/complex comments are less likely to be acted upon.

Conclusion: The findings provide insights for improving AI-generated code review feedback and its integration into development workflows.

Abstract: Code review is a critical quality assurance practice in software engineering development, and AI coding agents are increasingly generating review comments on pull requests. However, little is known about how developers actually respond to such agent-generated feedback. In this paper, we present the first large-scale empirical study on the resolution of agent-generated code review comments. We analyze $54{,}791$ comments generated by five widely used coding agents (i.e., Copilot, Cursor, Codex, Devin, and Claude) across $342$ Python repositories on GitHub. We examine (1) resolution rates across agents and comment types, (2) the role of developer experience, and (3) characteristics that influence comment usefulness. Our results show that resolution rate varies considerably across agents, with Copilot accounting for the majority of resolved comments (72.9\%). Core developers resolve the majority of agent-generated feedback, particularly for \textit{design} and \textit{evolvability}-related comments, while peripheral developers are more involved in resolving \textit{functional defect} comments. Through open card sorting of 470 unresolved comment discussions, we identify \textit{ten} discussion patterns explaining why comments remain unresolved, with \textit{incorrect suggestions} and \textit{intentional design decisions} being the most prevalent. Finally, our analysis reveals that the presence of an inline \textit{code suggestion} is the strongest predictor of comment resolution, while lengthy and complex comments are less likely to be acted upon. Our findings provide insights for improving AI-generated code review feedback and its integration into development workflows.

</details>


### [70] [Are Production Cloud Skills Adequately Tested? Measuring and Governing Skill Test Coverage in Practice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22015)
*Haotian Si,Junyi Chen,Shuyang Yu,Ruifeng Nie,Jiate Li,Jianqiang Zhao,Meng Li,Dengcheng He*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper introduces Skill Test Coverage, a method to measure how completely a cloud Skill's test suite covers its operational obligations. It combines model-assisted analysis with expert review to identify untested behaviors and generate test-improvement recommendations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Skill evaluation only measures whether a Skill improves task success, but passing testcases does not reveal which specified behaviors have never been tested. There is no systematic way to assess test completeness for workflow-oriented cloud Skills.

Method: The method defines coverage units, testcase coverage relation, calculation procedure, and claim boundary. It builds a measurement pipeline that recovers operational obligations from natural-language Skill packages, organizes workflow context, maps testcases using user prompts and initial resource states, and produces auditable coverage reports. Model-assisted candidate generation is combined with expert review, and a post-review module translates coverage gaps into test-improvement recommendations.

Result: The pipeline produces auditable coverage reports that identify untested operational behaviors in cloud Skill test suites, along with source-grounded test-improvement recommendations.

Conclusion: Skill Test Coverage provides a systematic, evidence-grounded approach to evaluating test completeness for cloud Skills, enabling identification and remediation of untested operational behaviors.

Abstract: Cloud platforms increasingly deliver reusable Cloud Skills that guide AI agents in creating, configuring, monitoring, and troubleshooting cloud resources. Workflow-oriented Skills specify multiple resource operations, user choices, validation steps, and recovery behavior. Existing Skill evaluation primarily measures whether a Skill improves task success. Passing the available testcases, however, does not reveal which behaviors specified by the Skill have never been tested. This paper introduces Skill Test Coverage, which measures how completely a Skill's test suite covers its operational test obligations. We define the coverage units, testcase coverage relation, calculation procedure, and claim boundary. Because these units and links are not explicit in natural-language Skill packages, we develop a measurement pipeline that recovers operational obligations, organizes their workflow context, maps testcases using both user prompts and initial resource states, and produces an auditable coverage report. Model-assisted candidate generation is combined with expert review to ensure that the reported coverage remains grounded in the original Skill and testcase evidence. A post-review module then translates confirmed coverage gaps into source-grounded test-improvement recommendations.

</details>


### [71] [Code Review is a Conversation: Toward Conversational AI Review Assistants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22095)
*Rosalia Tufano*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This vision paper argues that AI code review should shift from one-shot commenting to conversational AI assistants that interact with developers, ask questions, negotiate trade-offs, and participate in review as interactive partners rather than static comment generators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI code review tools frame review as a one-shot commenting task, overlooking that human code review is fundamentally a conversation involving questions, explanations, negotiation, knowledge transfer, and collective decision-making.

Method: The paper presents a vision and research agenda arguing for conversational AI review assistants, outlining capabilities such as identifying when conversation is needed, asking grounded questions, responding to explanations, summarizing issues, capturing rationale, and knowing when to abstain or escalate.

Result: The paper proposes a paradigm shift from automated commenting to human-AI sensemaking before integration, alongside novel evaluation methodologies needed for conversational review systems.

Conclusion: AI code review should evolve to conversational assistants that participate interactively in review discussions, requiring new research in studying review conversations, designing conversational capabilities, and evaluating their impact on software evolution.

Abstract: AI-based code review tools increasingly promise to help developers inspect pull requests, identify defects, and improve code quality. Yet most current approaches frame code review as a one-shot commenting task: given a diff, the system produces warnings or suggestions. This framing overlooks a central property of modern code review: review is a conversation. Human reviewers do not merely comment on code; they ask questions, explain expectations, negotiate design trade-offs, request evidence, transfer project knowledge, document rationale, and collectively decide whether a change is good enough to integrate. In this vision paper, we argue for conversational AI review assistants: systems that participate in code review as interactive partners rather than static comment generators. Such assistants should identify when conversation is needed, ask grounded questions, respond to developer explanations, summarize unresolved issues, help capture rationale, and know when to abstain or escalate to human reviewers. Such a paradigm shift requires novel evaluation methodologies as well. We outline a research agenda for studying review conversations, designing conversational AI review capabilities, and evaluating their impact on software evolution and maintenance. Our vision reframes AI code review from automated commenting to human-AI sensemaking before integration.

</details>


### [72] [No Edges, No Verdict: A Large-Scale Empirical Study of Declared Dependency Graphs in 78K SBOMs in the Wild](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22140)
*Artur Zięba-Kozarzewski*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Analysis of 78,612 SBOMs reveals three connectivity regimes (52.9% no edges, 8.8% degenerate, 38.3% well-connected), showing edge completeness depends on the generator tool rather than the software described. A degeneracy detector that replaces unsound "unreachable" inferences with "unknown" status improves KEV recall from 0.600 to 0.950 without alert flooding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SBOMs are increasingly used as dependency graphs for vulnerability triage and reachability analysis, but no large-scale study has characterized whether the declared dependency edges are complete enough to support sound closed-world reasoning.

Method: Large-scale characterization of 78,612 real-world SBOM files from the Wild SBOMs dataset, analyzing edge completeness, connectivity regimes, and generator-level effects. A degeneracy detector was designed and tested via controlled re-scoring and live end-to-end runs.

Result: 52.9% of SBOMs declare no edges; 8.8% have sparse edges with median 93% orphan share; only 38.3% form well-connected graphs. Edge completeness is determined by generator tool, not software described. CycloneDX compositions used by only 0.10%. Degeneracy detector improved KEV recall from 0.600 to 0.950 (controlled) and 0.957 (live).

Conclusion: The common consumer inference "no path implies unreachable" is unsound for the majority of SBOMs. Replacing this veto with an explicit "unknown" level guarded by a degeneracy detector restores recall without alert flooding. The dataset and scanner are released publicly.

Abstract: Software Bills of Materials (SBOMs) are consumed not only as component inventories but as dependency graphs: vulnerability triage, reachability filtering, and impact analysis all traverse the edges an SBOM declares. We present the first large-scale characterization of the declared dependency graph across 78,612 real-world SBOM files from the Wild SBOMs dataset (77,092 parseable). We find that the population splits into three regimes: 52.9% of SBOMs declare no edges at all (failing the NTIA minimum-elements requirement of dependency relationships), 8.8% declare a dependency block yet leave the majority of components isolated (degenerate regime; among such SBOMs with at least 50 components the median orphan share is 93%, and our 11 Syft-generated container-image SBOMs fall in this regime at 95-98% orphans), and 38.3% form well-connected graphs. Edge emission is determined by the generator, not the described software (0%-100% no-edge rates across tools), and the specification-level mechanism for declaring graph incompleteness (CycloneDX compositions) is used by 0.10% of the population. We argue that in the first two regimes, the common consumer inference "no path implies unreachable" is an unsound closed-world conclusion drawn from a demonstrably incomplete artifact; in a production vulnerability-prioritization system, replacing the resulting veto with an explicit "unknown" level guarded by a degeneracy detector recovered KEV recall from 0.600 to 0.950 (controlled re-scoring; 0.957 in a live end-to-end run) without alert flooding. We release our streaming scanner and the full per-SBOM topology dataset.

</details>


### [73] [HarnessLLM: Rust Verification Harness Generation with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22161)
*Minghua Wang,Yuwei Liu,Lin Huang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: HarnessLLM is an automated workflow using LLMs to generate verification harnesses for Rust code from existing test suites, achieving 94.66% precision on 9 real-world Rust codebases and detecting 6 memory safety bugs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rust's ownership model provides memory safety, but unsafe code and panics still pose risks. Formal verification is needed but developing verification harnesses is challenging and manual. Existing LLM-based approaches suffer from inaccurate API calls, inefficient nondeterministic data generation, and fabricated fixes.

Method: HarnessLLM automatically extracts calling scenarios from test cases, generates nondeterministic arguments via dependency analysis, incrementally synthesizes harnesses, and iteratively refines them by preserving critical code regions and reporting fabricated types/functions to LLMs for correction.

Result: On 9 real-world Rust codebases, extracted 294 calling scenarios from 494 test cases with 94.66% precision, generated harnesses in avg 145 seconds each, outperformed Autoharness (41% success rate), and detected 6 real-world memory safety bugs.

Conclusion: HarnessLLM is the first work to use LLMs for generating harnesses aimed at memory safety verification in real-world Rust projects, demonstrating practical utility.

Abstract: Rust's ownership model and type system offer strong memory safety guarantees, but unsafe code and runtime panics still present significant risks. Formal verification is essential to ensure memory safety, but developing verification harnesses remains a challenging and manual task. Although large language models (LLMs) have shown strong performance in various code analysis tasks, directly applying them to harness generation often results in inaccurate API invocations, inefficient nondeterministic data generation, and fabricated fixes.
  In this paper, we present HarnessLLM, an automated workflow that leverages LLMs to generate verification harnesses for Rust code directly from existing test suites. HarnessLLM automatically extracts calling scenarios from test cases, generates nondeterministic arguments based on dependency analysis, and incrementally synthesizes harnesses. It then iteratively refines the harnesses, preserving critical code regions and reporting fabricated types or functions to LLMs for correction. In our evaluation on 9 real-world Rust codebases, HarnessLLM extracted 294 calling scenarios from 494 test cases with 94.66% precision and generated harnesses for all scenarios in an average of 145 seconds each. It outperformed the existing approach, Autoharness, which succeeded on only 41% of those scenarios. Finally, 6 real-world memory safety bugs were detected using the generated harnesses, demonstrating the practical utility of our approach in verification. To our knowledge, this is the first work to use LLMs for generating harnesses aimed at memory safety verification in real-world Rust projects.

</details>


### [74] [Integrating Energy Efficiency into Software Development: Developer Perspectives and Requirements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22168)
*Anika Hennig,Leonie Schulte,Philipp M. Zähl,Mathias Eggert*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This study investigates software developers' perceptions of energy efficiency in daily work through ten semi-structured interviews. Results show energy efficiency is rarely explicitly considered (achieved indirectly via performance optimization), with barriers including limited awareness and delivery pressure. Requirements for AI-assisted energy-aware tools include actionable suggestions, seamless IDE integration, and transparency regarding data usage, actual energy savings, and training data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Software energy consumption optimization is under-researched from a practitioner perspective; despite technical advances, adoption in everyday development is limited.

Method: Ten semi-structured interviews with professional software developers, analyzed via Mayring's qualitative content analysis, partially interpreted through the Technology Acceptance Model.

Result: Energy efficiency is rarely an explicit concern; savings are indirect via performance optimization. Barriers: low awareness, delivery pressure. Requirements: actionable suggestions, seamless integration, transparency on data usage, energy trade-offs, and training data.

Conclusion: A developer-centered perspective reveals key requirements and barriers for energy-aware tools; AI-assisted solutions must prioritize transparency and practical integration to achieve real-world adoption.

Abstract: In the context of the growing energy footprint of information and communication technology, industry optimization efforts have primarily focused on hardware, while the impact of software on energy consumption is often overlooked. Although technical approaches for optimizing software energy consumption have been developed in research, their adoption in everyday development practice remains limited. This study investigates how software developers perceive energy efficiency in their daily work and which requirements and barriers they formulate for AI-assisted tools supporting energy-aware development. As part of the European GreenCode project, ten semi-structured interviews with professional software developers were conducted and analyzed using qualitative content analysis following Mayrings methodology. The identified requirements were subsequently partly interpreted through the lens of the Technology Acceptance Model. The results indicate that energy efficiency rarely plays an explicit role in daily development activities. Instead, energy savings are typically achieved indirectly through performance optimization. Identified barriers to the explicit consideration of energy efficiency include limited awareness and a strong focus on timely delivery. The interviews further revealed requirements for practical tool support, such as actionable optimization suggestions and seamless integration into common development environments. Furthermore, the acceptance of AI-assisted optimization tools strongly depends on transparency regarding the use of data, the actual energy savings compared to the energy consumption of the tool itself, and the disclosure of the training data used. This study contributes a developer-centered perspective on requirements for energy-aware software development tools and provides insights for designing AI-assisted solutions that align with real-world development practices.

</details>


### [75] [Comparing and Conceptualizing Data Protection Requirements Worldwide for Privacy Regulatory Compliance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22270)
*Claudia Negri-Ribalta,Lorena Sanchez Chamorro,Ioana Visescu,Muriel Frank,Anastasia Sergeeva,Alberto García,Rene Noel*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper identifies common and divergent regulatory data protection requirements (RDPRs) across G20 economies through interviews with 70 legal experts and content analysis, translating findings into DPO stories classified by SDLC phase and enterprise architecture layer to help organizations manage transborder personal data flows (TPDF) compliance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The growing digitalization and cross-border flow of personal data create complex compliance challenges, as organizations must reconcile differing data protection requirements across jurisdictions. Addressing requirements late in the SDLC causes costly rework, motivating the need for early identification of common and divergent RDPRs.

Method: The study combines deductive qualitative analysis of interviews with 70 legal experts from G20 economies and systematic content analysis of these economies' data protection regulations to identify common and divergent RDPRs.

Result: Identifies common requirements (e.g., consent) and divergent requirements (e.g., right to be forgotten) across jurisdictions, translated into DPO stories using user story notation, classified by SDLC phase and enterprise architecture layer.

Conclusion: Early identification of shared and divergent RDPRs is critical for managing TPDF compliance; the DPO stories provide a practical artifact to bridge regulatory requirements and software engineering practices across the SDLC and enterprise architecture.

Abstract: The growing digitalization of society has intensified the collection, processing, and sharing of personal data, increasingly moving across national borders and regulatory jurisdictions, prompting a proliferation of data protection frameworks worldwide. These transborder personal data flows (TPDF) are essential to today's economy, but organizations managing them must reconcile data protection requirements that differ, sometimes subtly, across jurisdictions. For requirements engineering, this is the central challenge: regulatory data protection requirements (RDPRs) are complex and not directly translatable into software requirements, especially when frameworks impose similar, non-identical, or contradictory obligations. Identifying which requirements are shared and which diverge is therefore critical to managing TPDF, and addressing them late in the software development lifecycle (SDLC) causes costly rework, making early identification essential for compliance and stakeholder communication. This paper identifies and conceptualizes common RDPRs worldwide from the perspective of data protection legal experts, answering: (SQ1) which requirements are common across regulations and how are they conceptualized, and (SQ2) which requirements diverge and how do they differ conceptually. We combine deductive qualitative analysis of interviews with 70 legal experts from G20 economies and other countries and systematic content analysis of these economies' data protection regulations. We identify common requirements, such as consent, and divergent ones, such as the right to be forgotten. Given their impact across the SDLC and enterprise architecture, we translate these findings into a set of Data Protection Officer DPO (DPO) stories, using the user story notation, classified by SDLC phase and enterprise architecture layer, to help organizations manage TPDF compliance.

</details>


### [76] [Vibe Coding: An Experiment with Test-Driven Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22406)
*Moritz Mock,Barbara Russo*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This exploratory study investigates human-CLLM collaboration in software development through "vibe coding," comparing four interaction models (solo, collaborative, fully automated, and agentic) using TDD workflows. Findings show agentic workflows excel at rapid, functionally correct production code but introduce untested decisions, while collaborative workflows produce better test suites.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
