Motivation: To investigate how humans and Conversational Large Language Models (CLLMs) can collaborate as peers through "vibe coding" — an approach integrating prompt engineering, agile design, and human-AI co-creation — to enhance collaboration quality in software development.

Method: Designed four interaction models (solo, collaborative, fully automated, agentic) representing different collaboration patterns. Implemented corresponding TDD workflows using structured prompts and Python scripts. Conducted a controlled pre-experimental study with TDD professionals comparing solo vs. collaborative workflows, plus repeated exploratory executions of fully automated and agentic workflows on the same tasks.

Result: Agentic workflows are best for rapid development and functionally correct production code but may introduce untested implementation decisions not required by functional specs. Collaborative workflows produce higher-quality, better-organized test suites. The choice of interaction model should depend on the development objective.

Conclusion: The appropriate interaction model for human-CLLM collaboration depends on the specific development objective, with trade-offs between development speed/code correctness and test suite quality/organization.

Abstract: Context: Conversational Large Language Models (CLLMs) can automatically generate code by collaborating with users through natural language. However, poor collaboration can lead to poor quality output.
  Objective: This exploratory study aims to investigate how humans and CLLMs can collaborate as peers through vibe coding, an approach that integrates principles from prompt engineering, agile design, and human-AI co-creation to enhance collaboration.
  Method: We designed four interaction models representing different collaboration patterns in the software development process: the solo model (human-only development), the collaborative model (human-CLLM collaboration), the fully automated model (development autonomously performed by a CLLM), and the agentic model (development autonomously performed by the MetaGPT~X platform). Based on these models, we implemented corresponding Test-Driven Development (TDD) workflows using structured prompts and Python scripts. We then conducted a controlled pre-experimental study with TDD professionals to compare the solo and collaborative workflows. In addition, we performed repeated exploratory executions of fully automated and agentic workflows on the same development tasks to obtain complementary evidence.
  Results: Our findings suggest that the choice of interaction model should depend on the development objective. Agentic workflows are best suited for rapid development and functionally correct production code but may introduce additional implementation complexity. However, they may also introduce additional implementation decisions that are not explicitly required by the functional specifications, resulting in untested decision points. In contrast, collaborative workflows produce higher-quality, better-organized test suites.
  Conclusions: Our work explored how...

</details>


### [77] [MineValiCoder: Reliable Code Generation with Test Case Quality Mining and Bipartite Graph-Based Mutual Validation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22471)
*Zhen Zhao,Qihang Yang,Feifei Dai,Xiangfang Li,Bo Li*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: MineValiCoder is a closed-loop TDD framework that collaboratively improves test-case and code quality through self-validation, parallel refinement, and bipartite-graph mutual validation, achieving state-of-the-art code generation results (e.g., 96.34% Pass@1 on HumanEval) across four LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based TDD approaches rely on human-crafted tests, fail with only natural-language requirements, and suffer from LLM stochasticity causing faulty tests (misleading feedback) and mixed-quality tests (conflicting evaluation signals).

Method: MineValiCoder has three modules: (1) TCQM filters faulty tests via self-validation; (2) Parallel TDD Refinement iteratively optimizes code using validated test feedback; (3) BiCoTeV uses bipartite graph modeling for mutual validation scoring to select optimal code.

Result: Pass@1 scores: 96.34% on HumanEval, 87.40% on MBPP, 64.00% on APPS, 51.33% on LiveCodeBench, outperforming SOTA across four LLMs.

Conclusion: MineValiCoder effectively mitigates LLM stochasticity and improves automated code generation reliability through mutual reinforcement of test and code quality.

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM)-based Test-Driven Development (TDD) has advanced automated code generation. However, existing approaches depend heavily on human-crafted test cases and cannot operate effectively when only natural-language requirements are available. Although recent work enables automatic test generation, it often overlooks the inherent stochasticity of LLMs, leading to two key defects: faulty tests generate misleading feedback that distorts code optimization, while mixed-quality test cases produce conflicting evaluation signals that hinder reliable code selection.
  To address these challenges, we propose MineValiCoder, a collaborative closed-loop TDD framework based on the mutual reinforcement of test-case quality and code quality. MineValiCoder comprises three modules. The Test Case Quality Mining (TCQM) module filters faulty test cases through self-validation, providing reliable optimization supervision. The Parallel TDD Refinement module iteratively optimizes code and generates diverse high-quality code candidates using validated test-case feedback. The Bipartite Graph-Based Code-Test Mutual Validation (BiCoTeV) module dynamically models code-test interactions and performs mutual validation scoring for stable and reliable optimal-code selection.
  Extensive evaluations across four LLMs and mainstream benchmarks show that MineValiCoder significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods. Specifically, it achieves Pass@1 scores of 96.34% on HumanEval, 87.40% on MBPP, 64.00% on APPS, and 51.33% on LiveCodeBench. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of MineValiCoder in mitigating LLM stochasticity and improving the reliability of automated code generation.

</details>


### [78] [A Preliminary Search for Evidence on Government Software Engineering Practices: Results from Three Rapid Reviews](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22485)
*Sebastián Pizard,Matías Porro,Andrea Muñoz,Andrea Delgado*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents three rapid reviews of 2024 publications (984 papers screened) finding only 4 studies on government software engineering practices, mostly in regional venues. It highlights the low visibility of government SE evidence in mainstream venues and proposes solutions like dedicated venues, academia-government collaboration incentives, and better grey literature evaluation methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Government agencies are major software developers and drivers of digital transformation, yet empirical evidence on their software engineering practices remains largely unexplored. This gap between practice and academic evidence needs investigation.

Method: Three rapid reviews of 2024 peer-reviewed publications: top-tier international SE venues, regional South American SE conferences, and a curated newsletter on public sector digitalization. 984 papers were screened.

Result: Only 4 studies reporting on SE practices conducted by, for, or in collaboration with government bodies were identified, mostly published in regional venues as experience reports or case studies.

Conclusion: Academic evidence on government SE practices has low visibility in mainstream SE venues. Implications include need for dedicated publication venues, incentives for government-academia collaboration, and improved methods for evaluating grey literature.

Abstract: Government agencies are major software developers and drivers of digital transformation, yet empirical evidence on their software engineering (SE) practices remains largely unexplored. This paper reports three rapid reviews of 2024 peer reviewed publications to assess the availability of evidence on government SE practices. We examined a subset of top tier international SE venues, regional South American SE conferences, and a curated newsletter on public sector digitalization. Across 984 screened papers, we identified only four studies reporting on SE practices conducted by, for, or in collaboration with government bodies, most published in regional venues as experience reports or case studies. Our findings are consistent with practitioners' perceptions that academic evidence on government SE practices has low visibility in mainstream SE venues. We discuss implications including the need for dedicated publication venues, incentives for government academia collaboration, and improved methods for evaluating grey literature, currently a key information source for government agencies. This work provides a preliminary study for understanding the evidence landscape and identifies directions for future research to better support evidence based software engineering in the public sector.

</details>

### [79] [Unsupervised Multimodal Intent Discovery via MLLM-Guided Concept Generation and Semantic Propagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21908)
*Yunjin Gu,Qianrui Zhou,Hua Xu*

Main category: cs.MM

TL;DR: MCSP is a fully unsupervised method for multimodal intent discovery that uses MLLM-guided contrastive reasoning to extract interpretable semantic concepts, then propagates them over a semantically weighted graph to generate reliable pseudo-labels for representation refinement, consistently outperforming state-of-the-art methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing unsupervised multimodal intent discovery methods lack interpretability because they rely on geometric similarity rather than high-level semantic guidance, and there is no explicit semantic supervision available.

Method: MCSP identifies high-quality representative samples per cluster, uses them for MLLM-guided contrastive reasoning against neighboring clusters to produce interpretable semantic concepts, then performs semantic propagation over a semantically weighted graph to align conceptual information with local structural consistency and generate pseudo-labels for representation refinement.

Result: MCSP consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods on three challenging multimodal intent datasets while producing interpretable clusters grounded in semantic concepts.

Conclusion: Introducing concept-based semantic refinement into unsupervised multimodal intent discovery improves both clustering performance and interpretability.

Abstract: Unsupervised multimodal intent discovery aims to uncover latent intents from unlabeled multimodal dialogues, but remains challenging due to the lack of explicit semantic supervision. Existing methods often provide limited interpretability, as their refinement mainly relies on geometric similarity rather than high-level semantic guidance. To address these limitations, we propose MCSP, a fully unsupervised method that introduces semantic refinement based on concepts into multimodal intent discovery. To obtain reliable semantic evidence for intent discovery, we identify high-quality representative samples for each cluster and use them to support MLLM-guided contrastive reasoning against neighboring clusters, which produces interpretable high-level semantic concepts. Building on these concepts, we perform semantic propagation over a semantically weighted graph to align conceptual information with local structural consistency and generate reliable pseudo-labels for representation refinement. Extensive experiments on three challenging multimodal intent datasets show that MCSP consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods while producing interpretable clusters grounded in semantic concepts.

</details>


### [80] [CARA: Concept-Aware Risk Attention for Interpretable Collision Anticipation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22494)
*Zhishan Tao,Ruoyu Wang,Yucheng Wu,Enjun Du,Yilei Yuan,Sherwin Ho,Yue Su,Jinbo Su,Yi Hong*

Main category: cs.MM

TL;DR: CARA is an intrinsically interpretable spatio-temporal framework for collision anticipation that uses domain-grounded risk concepts derived from accident narratives, aligned with video frames via vision-language similarity, to guide spatial attention, temporal attention, and anticipation, improving both accuracy and interpretability over existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing collision anticipation methods lack interpretability: feature-driven models are opaque, post-hoc explanations have fidelity issues, and concept-based methods are designed for static recognition rather than dynamic driving scenes. There is a need for an approach that provides interpretable reasoning about risk factors and their evolution over time.

Method: CARA derives domain-grounded risk concepts from accident narratives, aligns them with video frames via vision-language similarity, and organizes them into evolving concept trajectories. These trajectories guide spatial attention, temporal attention, and anticipation, allowing semantic concepts to directly influence the predictive process rather than serving as post-hoc explanations.

Result: Extensive experiments on three benchmarks show that CARA consistently improves anticipation accuracy and warning earliness over strong baselines, while providing sparse and semantically grounded concept evidence.

Conclusion: CARA demonstrates that intrinsically interpretable collision anticipation is achievable by tightly coupling semantic risk concepts with the predictive process, offering both improved performance and interpretability over existing approaches.

Abstract: Collision anticipation in autonomous driving requires not only accurate early warnings but also interpretable reasoning about what risk factors are being tracked and how risk evolves over time. Existing methods fall short in this regard: feature-driven models are opaque, post-hoc explanations often lack fidelity, and concept-based methods are mostly designed for static recognition rather than dynamic driving scenes. We propose CARA (Concept-Aware Risk Attention), an intrinsically interpretable spatio-temporal framework for collision anticipation. CARA derives domain-grounded risk concepts from accident narratives, aligns them with video frames via vision-language similarity, and organizes them into evolving concept trajectories. These trajectories provide explicit risk evidence that guides spatial attention, temporal attention, and anticipation, allowing semantic concepts to directly influence both where the model attends and how it predicts risk over time. By treating semantic risk factors as dynamic intermediate evidence rather than auxiliary post-hoc explanations, CARA tightly couples interpretability with the predictive process. Extensive experiments on three benchmarks show that CARA consistently improves anticipation accuracy and warning earliness over strong baselines, while providing sparse and semantically grounded concept evidence.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [81] [Physiological Signals as a Forensic Modality for Talking-Face Deepfake Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21776)
*Othmane Harraq,Tamer Aldwairi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a deepfake detection framework for talking-face synthesis using rPPG physiological signals extracted by RhythmFormer, achieving 0.806 AUC on Celeb-DF++ TF subset, and shows detection difficulty varies significantly across generators (0.985–0.690 AUC) due to interpretable physiological artifacts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current image-based detectors consistently fail to identify talking-face deepfakes, which synthesize facial video from a source image and audio without an underlying real video. Unlike face-swap, TF forgeries lack inherent physiological characteristics, making rPPG-based detection a uniquely motivated modality.

Method: Extracts per-video rPPG waveforms via RhythmFormer, then trains lightweight classifiers (e.g., 1D ResNet) to distinguish real from synthesized physiological signals. Evaluated under strict subject-independent protocol on the TF subset of Celeb-DF++.

Result: 1D ResNet achieves AUC 0.806 and EER 27.8% on Celeb-DF++ TF subset, within 2.4 points of best general-purpose detector (Effort, ICML 2025). Prior rPPG detector DeepFakesON-Phys degrades from 0.999 AUC (legacy data) to 0.622 (TF subset). Detection AUC ranges from 0.985 (Real3DPortrait) to 0.690 (IP-LAP) across 7 TF generators, with stable ranking across protocols.

Conclusion: Detection difficulty is strongly method-dependent and reflects interpretable physiological properties of each generator rather than evaluation noise, constituting the primary theoretical contribution. rPPG-based detection is viable for TF deepfakes but performance varies substantially across synthesis methods.

Abstract: Talking-face (TF) deepfake generation synthesizes photore- alistic facial video from a static source image and an au- dio signal, producing forgeries that current image-based detectors consistently fail to identify. Unlike face-swap ma- nipulation, TF synthesis has no underlying real video from which to inherit physiological characteristics, making re- mote photoplethysmography (rPPG) a uniquely motivated detection modality for this forgery category. We propose a detection framework that extracts per-video rPPG wave- forms via RhythmFormer and trains a suite of lightweight classifiers to distinguish real from synthesized physiologi- cal signals. Evaluated on the TF subset of Celeb-DF++ un- der a strict subject-independent protocol, where test identi- ties are completely separated from training identities, our 1D ResNet achieves an AUC of 0.806 and EER of 27.8%, placing it within 2.4 points of the best published general- purpose detector (Effort, ICML 2025) while operating ex- clusively on the physiological channel. We document a con- trolled reproduction study of DeepFakesON-Phys, the rep- resentative prior rPPG detector, demonstrating degrada- tion from AUC 0.999 on legacy face-swap data to 0.622 on the TF subset of Celeb-DF++. We further show that detec- tion difficulty is strongly method-dependent: AUC ranges from 0.985 (Real3DPortrait) to 0.690 (IP-LAP) across the seven TF generators, with the ranking remaining perfectly stable across all evaluation protocols. This spread reflects an interpretable physiological property of each generator rather than evaluation noise, and constitutes the primary theoretical contribution of the work.

</details>


### [82] [Synthetic Speech, Real Signal: Paralinguistic Preservation and Cross-Lingual Augmentation via Voice Cloning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22304)
*Roseline Polle,Owen Parsons,George Fairs,Luis Miguel San Martin Fernandez,Cole Looney,Xiaoliang Wu,Alexandra Livia Georgescu,Stefano Goria*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper benchmarks eight voice cloning models on five paralinguistic tasks, showing they largely preserve paralinguistic signal with modest degradation, and demonstrates that cloning English clinical speech into Japanese outperforms raw cross-lingual transfer for depression and anxiety detection on real Japanese speech.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Synthetic data augmentation is well-explored for linguistic tasks (e.g., ASR) but understudied for paralinguistic tasks, especially clinical ones where labeled data is expensive and some patient groups are underrepresented.

Method: Benchmarked eight voice cloning models on five paralinguistic tasks across public and clinical datasets, and evaluated cross-lingual cloning by training on cloned English clinical speech and testing on real Japanese speech for depression/anxiety detection.

Result: Most voice cloning models preserve paralinguistic signal with modest degradation; cloned English clinical speech trained on outperforms raw cross-lingual transfer for depression and anxiety detection on real Japanese speech.

Conclusion: Voice cloning is a promising direction for augmenting clinical speech data in low-resource languages.

Abstract: Synthetic data augmentation in speech is common practice for linguistic tasks like ASR, but has seen far less work for paralinguistic ones, especially clinical tasks where labelled data is expensive and some patient groups are underrepresented. Voice cloning is one such augmentation approach, but is typically evaluated on speech intelligibility (WER) or speaker similarity (SS) rather than on downstream performance, and it remains unclear whether these preserve the paralinguistic signal such tasks depend on. We benchmark eight voice cloning models on five paralinguistic tasks across public and clinical datasets, showing most preserve signal with modest degradation. We then clone English clinical speech into Japanese and find that training on cloned data outperforms raw cross-lingual transfer for depression and anxiety detection on real Japanese speech, suggesting voice cloning is a promising direction for augmenting clinical speech data in low-resource languages.

</details>


### [83] [Cloud-Native Evaluation-as-a-Service: A Microservices Architecture for Scalable AI Monitoring with Conformal Guarantees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21623)
*Lei Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EaaS is a cloud-native reference architecture that operationalizes AI evaluation as six stateless Kubernetes microservices, validated across coverage, calibration, drift detection, and fairness metrics, with sub-2ms p99 latency for conformal prediction and calibration services.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI evaluation platforms lack integration of conformal prediction, microservice decomposition, and DAG-based orchestration in a cloud-native architecture.

Method: Six stateless Kubernetes microservices: conformal prediction (finite-sample-corrected Adaptive Prediction Sets), calibration assessment, drift detection (RFF-approximated MMD), fairness monitoring (bootstrap CIs), DAG-based pipeline orchestrator, and result storage API.

Result: Empirical coverage within 1.4pp of nominal target over K=50 splits; 0% imputation needed for MMLU top-20 logprobs; RFF-MMD achieves 100% detection power with 5-8.5% Type I error; fairness monitoring reveals DP gap=0.33 by race; conformal prediction and calibration achieve sub-2ms p99 latency at batch size 100; RFF-MMD requires ~500ms.

Conclusion: EaaS is the first platform combining conformal-prediction-as-a-service, microservice decomposition, and DAG-based orchestration for AI evaluation in the cloud.

Abstract: We present EaaS, a cloud-native reference architecture that operationalizes AI evaluation methods as six stateless Kubernetes microservices: conformal prediction with finite-sample-corrected Adaptive Prediction Sets, calibration assessment, drift detection via RFF-approximated Maximum Mean Discrepancy, fairness monitoring with bootstrap confidence intervals, a DAG-based pipeline orchestrator, and a result storage API. We validate four key methodological concerns. First, empirical coverage is consistent with the marginal conformal guarantee across K=50 random calibration/test splits, with mean coverage within 1.4 percentage points of the nominal target. Second, all four MMLU answer tokens appear in the top-20 logprobs with 0% imputation needed, and simulated imputation at 10% produces less than 1.5% coverage impact. Third, RFF-MMD achieves 100% detection power for mild and severe drift at the median heuristic bandwidth, with Type I error between 5-8.5%. Fourth, fairness monitoring on the UCI Adult Income dataset reveals significant demographic parity disparities by race (DP gap=0.33) with stable alerts across sequential batches. Conformal prediction and calibration services achieve sub-2ms p99 latency at batch size 100; RFF-MMD requires ~500ms suited for periodic batch monitoring. A comparison with four open-source tools suggests that, to the best of our knowledge, no current platform combines conformal-prediction-as-a-service, microservice decomposition, and DAG-based orchestration.

</details>


### [84] [On the Depth Scalability of Logic Gate Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21633)
*Taegun An,Dohun kim,Haebeom Lee,Changhee Joo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Logic Gate Networks (LGNs) struggle with increased depth due to optimization collapse and topology limitations. The authors propose Input-Anchored Logic Gate Networks (IALGNs), where each gate combines a hidden feature with a direct input anchor, preserving a computational spine. IALGNs achieve consistent depth-accuracy improvements beyond 100 layers on MNIST, CIFAR-10, and CIFAR-100, unlike standard LGNs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LGNs do not reliably benefit from increased depth, even when optimization issues (collapse, unstable training) are mitigated. The authors argue that trainability alone is insufficient—deeper layers must also receive information that supports useful computation.

Method: IALGNs use an input-anchored topology where each gate combines an evolving hidden feature with a direct input anchor, forming a computational spine conditioned on original inputs at every layer. Random-k anchor relaxation improves anchor selection without breaking the spine.

Result: IALGNs achieve consistent fixed-width depth-accuracy improvements beyond 100 layers on MNIST, CIFAR-10, and CIFAR-100, while alternative LGN topologies saturate or degrade. Layer-wise probes, topology ablations, and effective-depth analysis confirm that input anchoring yields progressively more informative representations and longer computational paths.

Conclusion: Scalable depth in LGNs requires both stable optimization and an information-access pattern that supports input-conditioned refinement, as provided by input anchoring.

Abstract: Logic Gate Networks (LGNs) implement computation through compositions of Boolean operations, yet unlike classical Boolean circuits, existing LGNs do not reliably benefit from increased depth. We identify two distinct causes: optimization collapse in deep relaxed LGNs and a topology-induced limitation that persists even when skip-biased initialization and straight-through estimation stabilize training. Thus, trainability alone is insufficient; deeper layers must also receive information that supports useful computation.
  We introduce Input-Anchored Logic Gate Networks (IALGNs), in which each gate combines an evolving hidden feature with a direct input anchor. This topology preserves a computational spine while conditioning every layer on the original input. We show that a depth-D path can depend on up to D+1 input bits and establish a strict path-wise depth hierarchy. Random-k anchor relaxation further improves anchor selection without relaxing the spine.
  Across MNIST, CIFAR-10, and CIFAR-100, IALGNs achieve consistent fixed-width depth--accuracy improvements beyond 100 layers, whereas alternative LGN topologies saturate or degrade. Layer-wise probes, topology ablations, and effective-depth analysis show that input anchoring produces progressively more informative representations and preserves longer computational paths. These results demonstrate that scalable depth in LGNs requires both stable optimization and an information-access pattern that supports input-conditioned refinement.

</details>


### [85] [MotifRole-Diff: Risk-Optimal Role-Aware Corruption for Masked Molecular Graph Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21634)
*Tasfia Nuzhat Ornee,Elias Hossain,Ivan Garibay,Niloofar Yousef*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MotifRole-Diff introduces a role-aware corruption schedule for masked discrete diffusion in molecular graph generation, allocating masking rates based on empirical denoising difficulty and graph-level impact, outperforming uniform schedules on QM9 and MOSES benchmarks under matched compute.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Uniform corruption schedules in masked discrete diffusion treat all molecular graph tokens equally, ignoring substantial variation in denoising difficulty and reconstruction importance across structurally heterogeneous token roles.

Method: Formulates schedule selection as risk-optimal allocation of a fixed masking budget across token roles, using empirically measured denoising difficulty and graph-level perturbation impact, while keeping model architecture, clean sequence space, and decoder unchanged.

Result: QM9: validity improved from 0.905 to 0.944, FCD reduced from 1.701 to 1.609. MOSES: validity improved from 0.920 to 0.938, FCD reduced from 2.125 to 1.850.

Conclusion: Structurally informed corruption is a more effective masking strategy than uniform schedules for serialized molecular graph diffusion, as demonstrated by improved validity and FCD under matched architecture, training budget, and sampling compute.

Abstract: Masked discrete diffusion for molecular graph generation typically applies a uniform corruption schedule to all tokens in a lossless graph-to-sequence representation, implicitly treating structurally heterogeneous molecular components as equally difficult and equally important to reconstruct. However, different molecular graph token roles exhibit substantial variation in denoising difficulty and their influence on the decoded molecule, motivating role-specific corruption strategies. We introduce MotifRole-Diff, a role-aware corruption process that allocates masking rates according to empirically measured denoising difficulty and graph-level perturbation impact while preserving the model architecture, clean sequence space, and lossless molecular-graph decoder. We formulate schedule selection as the risk-optimal allocation of a fixed masking budget across token roles. Our theorem characterizes optimality for the modeled role-weighted residual risk, while downstream generation performance is evaluated empirically. Under matched architecture, training budget, and sampling compute, MotifRole-Diff improves validity on QM9 from 0.905 to 0.944 while reducing FCD from 1.701 to 1.609, and on MOSES improves validity from 0.920 to 0.938 while reducing FCD from 2.125 to 1.850. Role-wise diagnostics further show improved reconstruction across molecular graph token categories. Together, these matched-compute results indicate that structurally informed corruption is a more effective masking strategy than uniform schedules for serialized molecular graph diffusion.

</details>


### [86] [Toward User-Conditioned Evaluation of Personal LLM Agents under Temporal Interventions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21635)
*Pin Qian,Su Wang,Yihang Chen,Qiaolin Yu,Xiaoyuan Wang,Zhitong Guo,Zhicheng Wang,Junxian You*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This position paper argues that personal-agent evaluation requires a protocol replaying temporal interventions across persistent user-conditioned states with induced cross-dimensional effects and variation. No existing benchmark from an audited set satisfies all four conditions. A minimal benchmark design and candidate metrics are proposed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks (tool, memory, safety) evaluate capabilities in isolation, but personal agents maintain evolving persistent state (memories, skills, tool configs, policy) that interacts across dimensions. A unified evaluation protocol capturing failure propagation across components is needed.

Method: Formalizes four required conditions: (1) explicit temporal intervention, (2) persistent state across intervention, (3) induced cross-dimensional effects, (4) variation in user-conditioned state. Conducts a focused audit of public benchmark protocols against inclusion criteria.

Result: No existing benchmark protocol in the audited set satisfies all four conditions. The paper presents a minimal benchmark design and candidate reporting metrics for user-conditioned adaptation evaluation.

Conclusion: Future personal-agent evaluation should adopt a protocol that replays temporal interventions across varying user-conditioned persistent states and measures cross-component failure propagation. The proposed design and metrics serve as concrete reporting tools for this requirement.

Abstract: Personal agents maintain memories, learned skills, tool configurations, and policy state that evolve with each user. Existing agent benchmarks often evaluate these capabilities in isolation: tool benchmarks test invocation under fixed APIs, memory benchmarks test recall or forgetting, and safety benchmarks test static policy compliance. We argue that personal-agent evaluation requires a different protocol: replaying the same temporal intervention across different persistent user-conditioned states and measuring how failures propagate across agent components. We formalize this requirement as four conditions: explicit temporal intervention, persistent state across the intervention, induced cross-dimensional effects, and variation in user-conditioned state. A focused audit of public benchmark protocols selected by explicit inclusion criteria identifies several close cases. Under our explicitly narrow operationalization, we did not find a protocol in that audited set satisfying all four conditions. This claim is scoped as a focused gap analysis with bounded literature coverage. This position paper proposes a minimal benchmark design and candidate reporting metrics for user-conditioned adaptation. The result is a concrete design requirement for future personal-agent evaluation, with metrics used as reporting tools for that requirement.

</details>


### [87] [Measuring the Dependency Gap: Diagnosing Inter-Column Fidelity in Tabular Generative Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21636)
*Jie Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Standard metrics for synthetic tabular data are largely blind to inter-column dependencies; the authors introduce a dependency-aware diagnostic, find a persistent dependency gap in state-of-the-art flow-matching generators, show it is not a structural limitation but stems from missing direct dependency supervision, and demonstrate that no cheap intervention (capacity increase, copula correction, in-model mechanism) closes it.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current metrics for certifying synthetic tabular data fail to detect the destruction of inter-column dependencies, which are critical for minority-class utility in imbalanced domains like fraud and clinical risk.

Method: The authors decompose a strong classifier two-sample test (XGB-C2ST) into marginal, dependency, and numerical-categorical cross components, anchored between a fully-factorized reference and a real-data oracle, then apply it to a state-of-the-art flow-matching generator (TabbyFlow/EF-VFM).

Result: A real dependency gap exists that standard metrics miss; destroying dependency collapses minority-class utility; the gap persists despite 16x capacity increase; the generative objective is asymptotically exact but lacks direct dependency supervision; no cheap intervention (in-model dependency mechanism, post-hoc copula correction, capacity increase) closes the gap.

Conclusion: The dependency gap in synthetic tabular data is stubborn and not attributable to structural or capacity limits; it originates from missing direct dependency supervision, and commonly assumed fixes are ineffective, serving as a caution for the field.

Abstract: Synthetic tabular data is valued for preserving not only each column's marginal distribution but the dependencies between columns -- structure that carries much of the discriminative signal for minority classes in imbalanced domains such as fraud and clinical risk. Yet the metrics most commonly used to certify synthetic tabular data are, we show, largely blind to inter-column dependency: a baseline that models every column independently (and therefore destroys all dependency) is judged indistinguishable from real data by the logistic-regression C2ST, and the pairwise Trend score is only partially sensitive. We introduce a dependency-aware fidelity diagnostic that decomposes a strong classifier two-sample test (XGB-C2ST) into marginal, dependency, and numerical-categorical cross components, anchored between a worst-case fully-factorized reference (all dependency destroyed) and a best-case real-data oracle. Applying it to a state-of-the-art flow-matching generator (TabbyFlow/EF-VFM), we find a real dependency gap that standard metrics miss; destroying dependency outright collapses minority-class utility, and the generator's residual gap carries a smaller, consistent utility cost. We then ask whether this gap reflects a structural limitation of mean-field generative objectives. It does not: consistent with recent recovery results for variational flow matching, the objective is asymptotically exact. Yet the gap is stubborn -- a 16x increase in model capacity does not close it -- pointing to the absence of direct dependency supervision rather than a capacity or structural limit. Consistent with this, and because the residual gap is higher-order, no cheap intervention closes it: not an in-model dependency mechanism, not post-hoc copula correction, and not the 16x capacity increase -- a caution for a field that assumes such fixes help.

</details>


### [88] [Quasi-Monte Carlo Initialization for Meta-Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21637)
*Julian G. Soltes*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates quasi-Monte Carlo (QMC) weight initialization for meta-reinforcement learning, showing improvements in training convergence for similar tasks compared to orthogonal defaults, though orthogonal methods remain superior for dissimilar tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore whether QMC-based weight initialization can improve meta-reinforcement learning performance in benchmark environments.

Method: Various QMC sampling methods bound a population-based search to aggregate an optimal prior from a baseline task set, compared against modern orthogonal (SB3) defaults.

Result: QMC meta-priors improve training convergence on similar unseen continuous control tasks; orthogonal orientation is globally superior for dissimilar tasks.

Conclusion: QMC weight initialization is beneficial for similar task extrapolation, but orthogonal initialization remains more robust for unbiased search across dissimilar tasks.

Abstract: This paper explores the efficacy of quasi-Monte Carlo (QMC) weight initialization for meta-reinforcement learning within modern benchmark environments. Various sampling methods are used to bound a population-based search and aggregate an optimal prior from a baseline set of tasks. The QMC meta-priors show improvements in training convergence compared to modern orthogonal (SB3) defaults when extrapolated to similar unseen continuous control environments. In dissimilar tasks, the orthogonal orientation was globally superior for an unbiased search.

</details>


### [89] [Toward Goal-Agnostic Joint-Embedding Predictive Control of Partial Differential Equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21644)
*Jonathan Gallagher,Roberto Guglielmi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper presents a goal-agnostic PDE control framework using JEPA with a 2D ViT encoder and latent dynamics trained offline. A learned linear kinetic-energy probe on frozen latent rollouts outperforms latent-space L2 planning for control. On the PDE Control Gym 2D Navier-Stokes benchmark, KE-probe planning achieves -10.90 vs -12.08 native reward, lowers RMSE by 53% on held-out targets, and supports stabilization tasks with 2.7% error, supporting that calibrated physical observables are better control objectives than raw latent distances.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a goal-agnostic world model for PDE control that can be reused across diverse downstream tasks without retraining, and to investigate whether physical observables (like kinetic energy) are better control objectives than raw latent-space distances.

Method: A joint-embedding predictive architecture (JEPA) with a small 2D ViT encoder and action-conditioned latent dynamics trained offline without rewards or goals. Control via MPPI planning using either latent L2 distance or a learned linear kinetic-energy probe on frozen latent rollouts.

Result: KE-probe planning improves native reward from -12.08±0.86 to -10.90±0.91 on Navier-Stokes benchmark, lowers late RMSE from 0.0765 to 0.0692, achieves 53% lower RMSE (0.0220 vs 0.0469) on held-out targets winning all paired episodes, and supports stabilization with 2.7% mean relative error.

Conclusion: Latent dynamics can remain goal-agnostic while calibrated physical observables (ensuring unique continuation) serve as better control objectives than raw latent-space distances, though the approach is brittle to measurement noise and missing pixels.

Abstract: We present a goal-agnostic control framework for partial differential equations (PDEs) built around a joint-embedding predictive architecture (JEPA). The small 2D ViT encoder and action-conditioned latent dynamics are trained offline without a reward or downstream goal, frozen, and reused by a model-predictive path integral (MPPI) controller. We find that when available, the control objective is better applied to an explicit physical observable (provided injectivity) than to minimizing raw Euclidean distance ($L^2$) in the learned latent space. For a learned linear kinetic-energy (KE) probe on frozen latent rollouts we can reproduce held-out trajectories with $R^2=0.989$, while requiring no change to the underlying world model. On the PDE Control Gym 2D Navier--Stokes benchmark, using KE-probe planning improves the matched 50-episode native reward from $-12.08\pm0.86$ for latent-$L^2$ planning to $-10.90\pm0.91$ (95\% CI), while lowering last-quarter velocity-field RMSE from $0.0765$ to $0.0692$. Across three intentionally withheld, dissimilar, aperiodic targets, KE planning lowers late field RMSE by $53\%$ relative to latent-$L^2$ planning ($0.0220$ versus $0.0469$), winning all 30 paired episodes. The same frozen model also supports controls targeting stabilization around a steady configuration via direct regulation of KE achieving $2.7\%$ mean relative error. While the latent probe is brittle to measurement noise and missing pixels, we believe the results support the claim that latent dynamics can remain both dynamic and goal-agnostic while calibrated observables (granted they guarantee unique continuation) may be a better objective for state control

</details>


### [90] [Multi-Horizon Consistency as Geometry: When Latent Dynamics Contract, and When They Do Not](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21645)
*Kavya Bhand,Aadi Joshi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Multi-horizon latent consistency (controlled by λ) reduces prediction expansion and error in Moving-MNIST but not in action-conditioned or video domains; a stochastic-forcing law unifies control domains on a common curve via calibrated noise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Practitioners use multi-horizon latent consistency in video predictors and world models without understanding its effect on transition geometry; this paper aims to diagnose what λ (weight on multi-step latent agreement) actually does to the learned dynamics.

Method: Treat λ as a diagnostic control; measure expansion proxy L20 (q95 quantile) and horizon-20 prediction error E20 across Moving-MNIST, Pendulum-v1, CartPole-v1, and KTH Actions; use associational mediation analysis, defensive checks (architectural baselines, exogenous stress, WorldTest, MPC, scaling), and a stochastic-forcing law L20 ~ 1.23 + 1.82 η to unify control domains via calibrated η_eff.

Result: On MMNIST, λ=0.8 reduces L20 from 4.96→1.01 (p=0.005) and halves E20 (0.365→0.177, p=1.1e-13); four of six seeds cross L<1. Same loss does not produce L<1 on Pendulum-v1, CartPole-v1, or KTH Actions despite E20 improvements. Mediation analysis on MMNIST gives r̂=0.94 [0.88, 1.00]. A linear L20(η) law with slope ~1.69–2.00 unifies control domains.

Conclusion: Soft consistency can push passive video toward a near-contractive band, but that band is domain-limited; the effect does not generalize to action-conditioned or real-video domains. The stochastic-forcing law provides a unified description across control domains.

Abstract: Multi-horizon latent consistency is a common training knob in video predictors and world models, but practitioners rarely know what it does to transition geometry. We treat lambda, the weight on multi-step latent agreement, as a diagnostic control and measure an empirical expansion proxy L20,q95 together with horizon-20 prediction error E20. On Moving-MNIST (n=6 seeds at the critical pair), raising lambda from 0 to 0.8 cuts L20 from 4.96 +/- 2.01 to 1.01 +/- 0.06 (paired t p=0.005, Wilcoxon p=0.031) and halves E20 (0.365 to 0.177, paired t p=1.1e-13). Four of six seeds cross L<1 at lambda=0.8. The same loss does not produce population L<1 on action-conditioned Pendulum-v1 or CartPole-v1, nor on KTH Actions video, even when E20 improves. An associational mediation analysis on MMNIST gives r-hat=0.94 (95% CI [0.88, 1.00], n=27, B=2000); lambda was not randomized. Defensive checks (architectural baselines, exogenous stress, WorldTest, MPC, scaling) mostly support a narrow claim: soft consistency can push passive video toward a near-contractive band, and that band is domain-limited. A stochastic-forcing law L20 ~ 1.23 + 1.82 eta at lambda=0.8 (bootstrap slope CI [1.73, 1.92], R^2=0.96) unifies control domains on the same curve via calibrated eta_eff. Complete joint slices at lambda in {0.4, 1.2} (30/30 cells, 5 eta x 3 seeds) show comparable linear L20(eta) slopes (~1.69 and ~2.00); we do not fit a continuous (lambda, eta) surface. We do not report DreamerV3 or TD-MPC2 returns.

</details>


### [91] [Adjustment Speed as a Safety Constraint for Nonstationary Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21646)
*Timothy Tomashevskiy*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes using adjustment speed as a safety constraint for reinforcement learning in nonstationary environments. It defines safety in terms of adaptation feasibility: when required adaptation exceeds the system's recovery capacity, proactive shielding is triggered. Experiments in a driving environment show reduced safety violations during environmental changes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing safe RL methods assume stationary environments and ignore adaptation speed as a safety concern, leading to transient unsafe behavior when environments evolve over time.

Method: The framework uses learned context representations and short-horizon context forecasts to estimate adaptation demand, compares it with the agent's achievable adaptation capacity, and proactively tightens the admissible action set with an action-level shield when demand exceeds capacity.

Result: The approach reduces safety violations in short-horizon windows aligned with context changes. Shielding is more conservative for peak- and tail-risk suppression, while optimization-level adjustment further reduces short-horizon switch-conditioned violations.

Conclusion: Adaptation feasibility is a practical safety principle for RL under nonstationarity, and proactive intervention can improve safety during environmental change.

Abstract: Ensuring safety in reinforcement learning under nonstationarity requires determining whether a learning system can safely adapt to forecasted environmental change within the required recovery horizon. Existing safe reinforcement learning methods typically assume stationary environments and do not explicitly consider adaptation speed as a safety concern. However, when environments evolve over time, delayed adaptation may result in transient unsafe behavior.
  This paper proposes adjustment speed as a safety constraint for nonstationary reinforcement learning. The central idea is to define safety in terms of adaptation feasibility: future states or regions may become unsafe when the adaptation required to remain safe exceeds the learning system's calibrated recovery capacity. The proposed framework uses learned context representations and short-horizon context forecasts to estimate adaptation demand and compare it with the agent's achievable adaptation capacity.
  When predicted adaptation demand exceeds the calibrated recovery capacity, the framework proactively tightens the admissible action set and activates an action-level shield to reduce unsafe behavior before violations occur.
  Experiments in a nonstationary driving environment show that the proposed approach primarily reduces safety violations in short-horizon windows aligned with context changes. Ablation studies further show that shielding is more conservative for peak- and tail-risk suppression, while optimization-level adjustment provides additional reductions in short-horizon switch-conditioned violations.
  These results support adaptation feasibility as a practical safety principle for reinforcement learning under nonstationarity and demonstrate that proactive intervention can improve safety during periods of environmental change.

</details>


### [92] [A Drift Stable Quantum Federated Learning for Intelligent Services](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21647)
*Shanika Iroshi Nanayakkara,Shiva Raj Pokhrel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DUQFL-Prox, a drift-stable quantum federated learning framework using deep-unfolded local optimization with adaptive SPSA updates and proximal terms, improves stability, generalization, and client fairness in heterogeneous distributed environments for privacy-aware services like fraud detection and genomic classification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing quantum federated learning suffers from unstable local updates, client drift, and unfair performance across clients due to heterogeneous client data and noisy quantum optimization, which undermines reliability and fairness in privacy-sensitive distributed decision systems.

Method: DUQFL-Prox uses deep-unfolded local optimization where each client performs adaptive unfolded SPSA (Simultaneous Perturbation Stochastic Approximation) updates instead of a fixed local optimizer. A proximal term keeps local models close to the global model, and a lightweight controller learns step-specific optimization parameters to improve post-aggregation performance.

Result: Experiments on financial fraud and genomic classification tasks demonstrate that DUQFL-Prox improves stability, generalization, and client fairness compared with standard QFL baselines.

Conclusion: Deep-unfolded quantum federated learning can support more reliable and fair intelligent services in heterogeneous distributed environments.

Abstract: Quantum federated learning enables distributed clients to train quantum neural networks without sharing local data, making it promising for privacy-aware intelligent services. Intelligent services in this context refer to privacy-sensitive distributed decision systems, such as fraud detection and genomic classification, where reliable and fair client-level learning is as important as the accuracy of the aggregate model. However, heterogeneous client data and noisy quantum optimization often cause unstable local updates, client drift, and unfair performance between clients. This paper proposes DUQFL-Prox, a drift-stable quantum federated learning framework based on deep-unfolded local optimization. Instead of using a fixed local optimizer, each client performs adaptive unfolded SPSA updates, while a proximal term keeps the local model close to the global model. A lightweight controller learns step-specific optimization parameters to improve post-aggregation performance. Experiments on financial fraud and genomic classification tasks show that DUQFL-Prox improves stability, generalization, and client fairness compared with standard QFL baselines. The results suggest that deep-unfolded quantum federated learning can support more reliable and fair intelligent services in heterogeneous distributed environments.

</details>


### [93] [Shallower ReLU Network Representations via Exact Linear Algebra](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21651)
*Kilian Rueß,Gennadiy Averkov,Florestan Brunck,Moritz Grillo,Christoph Hertrich,Georg Loho,Jack Stade,Moritz Stargalla,Matthew Sun,Martin Winter*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proves that the maximum of n real numbers can be exactly represented by a ReLU network with two hidden layers for n ≤ 10, and for n > 10, with ⌈log₅(n/2)⌉+1 hidden layers. The result improves prior work by extending two-hidden-layer representations from n=5 to n=10 and reducing the depth bound from log₃ to log₅.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve the known depth bounds for exact ReLU representation of the max function and, by extension, all continuous piecewise-linear functions, advancing the theoretical understanding of neural network expressivity.

Method: The problem is reduced to exact rational linear algebra via symmetry reduction, encoding necessary cancellations into finite linear systems over ℚ, which are solved computationally. The max₁₀ representation uses a structured first hidden layer of pairwise maxima, enabling recursive substitution into larger networks.

Result: Two-hidden-layer ReLU representation for maxₙ (n ≤ 10); for n > 10, depth ⌈log₅(n/2)⌉+1 < log₅(n)+1.5694; every continuous piecewise-linear function on ℝ^d (d ≤ 9) has a two-hidden-layer ReLU representation.

Conclusion: The results improve upon Bakaev et al. (STOC'26), who showed two-hidden-layer representation for max₅ and a log₃ depth bound. The log₅-based bound yields shallower networks for representing maxₙ and piecewise-linear functions.

Abstract: We prove that the maximum of $n$ real numbers is exactly representable by a ReLU network with two hidden layers for every $n\le 10$. The constructions are obtained by reducing the problem to exact rational linear algebra: after a symmetry reduction, the necessary cancellations are encoded in finite linear systems over $\mathbb{Q}$, which we solve and verify computationally. The representation of $\max_{10}$ has a structured first hidden layer consisting only of pairwise maxima, a feature that allows it to be recursively substituted into larger networks. We use this to show that for every $n>10$, the maximum $\max_{n}$ can be exactly represented with $\lceil{\log_5 (n / 2)\rceil}+1 < \log_5(n) +1.5694$ hidden layers. Via the generalized hinging-hyperplane representation [Wang, Sun, IEEE Trans. Inf. Theory 2005], the same depth bound holds for all continuous piecewise-linear functions on $\mathbb{R}^d$, with $d+1$ in place of $n$. In particular, every continuous piecewise-linear function on $\mathbb{R}^d$ for $d\le 9$ admits a two-hidden-layer ReLU representation. Our results improve on [Bakaev, Brunck, Hertrich, Stade, Yehudayoff, STOC'26]. In that work, the authors established a two-hidden-layer representation for $\max_{5}$ and an upper bound of $\lceil{\log_3 (n-2)\rceil}+1$ hidden layers for $\max_{n}$.

</details>


### [94] [Molt: A Scalable PyTorch-Native Training Framework for Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21653)
*Jian Hu,Huiying Li,Hao Zhang,Binfeng Xu,Yifan Zhang,Shaokun Zhang,Hemil Desai,Michael Demoret,Pavlo Molchanov,Jan Kautz,Yi Dong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Molt is a compact, PyTorch-native reinforcement learning training framework designed for researcher efficiency. It maintains full algorithm traceability, supports asynchronous training of multimodal/MoE policies with generation-consistent tokens, and achieves performance statistically comparable to Megatron-based systems while being simple enough for both humans and AI coding assistants to understand entirely.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current RL training frameworks require costly and complex modifications across multiple layers (trainer, distributed backend, rollout glue) for each algorithm change, placing a heavy burden on researchers.

Method: Molt is built as a PyTorch-native framework with a compact codebase that is fully readable by humans and AI assistants. It treats the agent as an ordinary program and uses a single asynchronous loop to train multimodal and mixture-of-experts policies, ensuring token-generation consistency, policy version consistency, and model semantic consistency.

Result: Under a matched, fully asynchronous protocol, Molt achieves statistically comparable performance to a state-of-the-art Megatron-based stack.

Conclusion: Molt demonstrates that leanness does not sacrifice performance; it provides open-source recipes and containers for reproducible RL research.

Abstract: Agentic reinforcement learning research is constant algorithm modification, new estimators, new pipeline stages, new rollout schemes, and in mainstream frameworks each change threads through layers of trainer, distributed backend, and rollout glue: the cost lands on the researcher at every iteration. Molt is a PyTorch-native training framework built to keep that cost small: a codebase compact and clean enough for a researcher to hold in their head, and for an AI coding assistant to read and reason about in its entirety, so the algorithm flow can be traced and changed end to end. The agent is an ordinary program, and one asynchronous loop trains multimodal and mixture-of-experts policies while never training on a token it did not generate, consistent in tokens, policy versions, and model semantics. Leanness does not cost performance: under a matched, fully asynchronous protocol, Molt is statistically comparable to a state-of-the-art Megatron-based stack. Molt is open source and provides recipes and containers at https://github.com/NVIDIA-NeMo/labs-molt.

</details>


### [95] [Physically Constrained Federated Additive Models for O-RAN SLA-Risk Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21665)
*Aubida A. Al-Hameed,Mohammed M. H. Qazzaz,Maryam Hafeez,Syed A. Zaidi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes Monotone FedNAM, a federated additive model with physically constrained monotone splines, for predicting per-slice SLA violations in O-RAN while preserving auditability and data privacy across base stations. It eliminates physically invalid predictions and reduces uplink traffic by 65% with minimal AUC loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing neural additive models (NAMs) for per-slice SLA violation prediction in O-RAN produce physically invalid effects (e.g., predicting higher risk when channel quality improves), and federated training (especially non-IID FedAvg) exacerbates these violations. Additionally, per-slice KPIs are commercially sensitive, prohibiting centralized data pooling.

Method: Monotone FedNAM: a federated additive model where KPIs with unambiguous physical direction are represented as monotone splines whose constraints survive FedAvg aggregation by construction, while contestable KPIs remain unconstrained. Trained as a Non-RT RIC rApp and deployable as a Near-RT RIC xApp.

Result: Eliminates all monotonicity violations, raises constrained shape consistency from 0.71 to 1.00, generalizes to an unseen scheduling policy, reduces uplink traffic by 65%, with a cost of 0.04–0.07 AUC.

Conclusion: Physically constrained federated additive models can support auditable SLA risk inference for multi-tenant O-RAN service assurance, balancing physical validity, privacy, and communication efficiency.

Abstract: Proactive service assurance in O-RAN requires predicting per-slice SLA violations before they occur. The prediction model must be auditable by operators and must train across base stations without pooling per-slice KPIs, which are commercially sensitive because slices are leased to individual tenants. Neural additive models (NAMs) offer auditability because each KPI contributes through a visible shape function. However, visibility alone does not guarantee physical validity. On the ColO-RAN testbed dataset, unconstrained NAMs learn effects that contradict wireless physics, for example predicting higher risk when channel quality improves. This failure appears under both local and centralized training, and non-IID federated averaging worsens it. We present Monotone FedNAM, a federated additive model in which KPIs with unambiguous physical direction are represented as monotone splines whose constraints survive FedAvg aggregation by construction, while contestable KPIs remain unconstrained. The model trains and operates as a Non-RT RIC rApp and is compact enough for deployment as a Near-RT RIC xApp. Monotone FedNAM eliminates all monotonicity violations, raises constrained shape consistency from 0.71 to 1.00, generalizes to an unseen scheduling policy, and reduces uplink traffic by 65%, at a cost of 0.04 to 0.07 AUC. These results show that physically constrained federated additive models can support auditable SLA risk inference for multi-tenant O-RAN service assurance

</details>


### [96] [Neural Feature Governance: Extending Atom Prevalence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21671)
*Idris Karel Seunda Ekwe,Patrick Tenga Shako,Ernest Parfait Fokoué*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NAP is a Bayesian framework for structured node-level model selection that achieves extreme sparsity (as few as 8% active nodes), well-calibrated uncertainty (3-4% epistemic variance, 93.4% interval coverage vs 95% target), and interpretability through a four-phase pipeline combining lottery ticket identification, Spike-and-Slab variational training, Poisson-Binomial layer-size selection, and Bayesian fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern deep neural networks with billions of parameters suffer from lack of transparency, computational inefficiency, and unreliable uncertainty quantification. There is a need for a principled framework that simultaneously addresses sparsity, accuracy, interpretability, and uncertainty calibration.

Method: NAP introduces a neural atom (activation unit) and operates via a four-phase pipeline: (1) Bayesian Lottery Ticket identification via Iterative Magnitude Pruning, (2) soft variational training of Spike and Slab Independent Gaussian model, (3) Poisson-Binomial optimal layer-size selection, and (4) Bayesian fine-tuning for a sparse, stable, interpretable, and accurate model.

Result: Achieved active nodes as few as 8% of original dense architecture on MNIST; aleatoric-epistemic decomposition shows model ignorance accounts for only 3-4% of total predictive variance; regression reliability diagrams show 93.4% predictive interval coverage against 95% target.

Conclusion: NAP is a reliable, theoretically grounded, and computationally tractable solution for simultaneously achieving sparsity, accuracy, interpretability, and uncertainty quantification in Bayesian neural networks.

Abstract: Neural network compression and interpretability remain open challenges in modern deep learn- ing, where billion-parameter architectures deliver impressive accuracy at the cost of trans- parency, computational efficiency, and reliable uncertainty quantification. This paper introduces Neural Atom Prevalence (NAP), a principled Bayesian framework for structured node-level model selection in feedforward neural networks. NAP introduces the neural atom (activation unit) and functions as a hybrid method operating through a four-phase pipeline: Bayesian Lottery Ticket (BLT) identification via Iterative Magnitude Pruning (IMP), soft variational training of the Spike and Slab Independent Gaussian (SS-IG) model, Poisson-Binomial (PB) optimal layer-size selection, and Bayesian fine-tuning to produce a sparse, stable, interpretable, and accurate model. Extensive empirical validation across simulated nonlinear regression, two UCI benchmark datasets (Concrete, YearPredictionMSD), and the MNIST image classification task demonstrates that NAP achieves state-of-the-art structural sparsity, reducing active nodes to as few as 8% of the original dense architecture on MNIST, while well-calibrated probabilisti- cally: the aleatoric-epistemic uncertainty decomposition reveals that model ignorance accounts for only 3 to 4% of total predictive variance across all experiments, and regression reliability diagrams confirm a near-nominal predictive interval coverage (93.4% observed against a 95% target). These results establish NAP as a reliable, theoretically grounded, and computation- ally tractable solution to the simultaneous pursuit of sparsity, accuracy, interpretability, and uncertainty quantification in Bayesian neural networks.

</details>


### [97] [Self-Poisoning in Adaptive Out-of-Distribution Detection: A Sharp-Threshold Theory and Certified Label-Free Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21673)
*Vishnu Bindu Balachandran*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper analyzes test-time adaptive OOD detectors that update a memory bank from unlabeled streams, proving they obey a dynamical law (generalized Pólya urn) that converges to a mean-field equilibrium. When the equilibrium slope (reproduction number) exceeds one, the bank becomes fully poisoned and the detector collapses. The admission kernel is near-critical (slope just below one) across all encoder families. A certified admission gate using a frozen reserve eliminates the contamination transition, while CDC addresses static-calibration drift. A two-world impossibility theorem shows drift and contamination are indistinguishable without labels, establishing a complete possibility/impossibility characterization for label-free adaptive OOD detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand and characterize the fundamental dynamics and limitations of test-time adaptive out-of-distribution detectors that update from unlabeled data streams, particularly investigating why they can fail catastrophically due to contamination accumulation.

Method: Models memory bank impurity evolution as a generalized Pólya urn process; proves almost-sure convergence to mean-field equilibrium; analyzes admission kernel empirically across encoder families; proposes certified admission gate (frozen reserve) and CDC for drift; proves impossibility theorem for label-free distinction of drift vs. contamination.

Result: Admission kernel is affine (R² ≥ 0.996) with slope just below one across all encoder families (near-critical by design). Predicted threshold matches empirical collapse across 96 settings; ungated dictionaries lose up to 0.163 AUROC. Certified admission gate removes the transition at all contamination rates. CDC restores nominal FPR on drift-affected cells. Closed-form power ceiling is established.

Conclusion: Provides a complete possibility/impossibility characterization of label-free adaptive OOD detection, showing that drift and contamination are fundamentally indistinguishable without labels, and that the proposed methods approach the theoretical ceiling.

Abstract: Test-time adaptive out-of-distribution (OOD) detectors update a memory bank from the unlabelled stream. We show this adaptation obeys a provable dynamical law. Modelling bank impurity as a generalized Pólya urn, we prove almost-sure convergence to a mean-field equilibrium whose slope acts as a reproduction number. Below one, impurity stays benign. Above one, the bank is fully poisoned and the detector collapses. The measured admission kernel is affine ($R^2 \ge 0.996$) with slope just below one in every encoder family (a protocol signature), so this detector class is near-critical by design, and across 96 settings the predicted threshold matches the empirical collapse, where ungated dictionaries lose up to $0.163$ AUROC. We then prove that a certified admission gate, reading only a frozen reserve, severs the feedback loop and removes the transition at every contamination rate, even adversarially, while controlling false positives label-free. For the complementary static-calibration failure under drift we give CDC, which restores nominal FPR label-free on all tested drift-affected cells. Finally we prove a two-world impossibility theorem. Drift and contamination are indistinguishable without labels, forcing a closed-form power ceiling our procedure approaches. Together these give a complete possibility/impossibility characterization of label-free adaptive OOD detection.

</details>


### [98] [Encoding Invisible Causation for Bridge Diagnostic Agents: Triple-Guided Retrieval-Augmented Fine-Tuning with QLoRA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21680)
*Takato Yasuno*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a Damage Cause Encoder that uses Knowledge Triple Extraction from manuals, retrieval-augmented context, and fine-tuning comparison (LoRA/QLoRA/QA-LoRA) to classify 10 damage causes from visible damage descriptions. QLoRA achieves 87.07% accuracy with 72% lower GPU memory and 11% faster inference than full-precision LoRA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge infrastructure deteriorates due to invisible root causes (salt intrusion, freezing, fatigue cracking, etc.) that expert diagnosis relies on tacit knowledge built over years. Automating this latent causal reasoning is challenging but necessary for autonomous bridge diagnostic agents.

Method: Three-component pipeline: (i) Knowledge Triple Extraction using LLM to extract causal triples from 15-35 diagnostic PDF manuals indexed in FAISS; (ii) Retrieval-Augmented Context concatenating relevant causal triples with damage descriptions; (iii) Systematic Fine-tuning Comparison of LoRA, QLoRA, and QA-LoRA on a fixed Golden Testset.

Result: QLoRA achieves identical test accuracy (87.07%) to full-precision LoRA, 11% faster inference, 72% lower GPU memory usage, and superior generalization. On a 100-sample diverse evaluation, QLoRA outperforms LoRA by 13 percentage points across all 10 damage cause classes.

Conclusion: QLoRA provides the optimal trade-off for memory-efficient, high-accuracy bridge diagnostic agents deployable on consumer-grade edge hardware, with a reusable Golden Testset benchmark contribution.

Abstract: Bridge infrastructure deteriorates gradually, yet its root causes---salt intrusion, freezing, fatigue cracking, and others---remain invisible to the naked eye. Expert diagnosis relies on tacit knowledge built over years of practice. We address the challenge of automating this latent causal reasoning by proposing a Damage Cause Encoder that classifies 10-class damage causes from visible damage descriptions $S_i$ for use in autonomous bridge diagnostic agents. Our approach chains three components: (i)Knowledge Triple Extraction---a large language model extracts causal triples of the form (damage $\xrightarrow{\mathtt{caused\_by}}$ cause) from 15--35 diagnostic PDF manuals and indexes them in a FAISS vector store; (ii)Retrieval-Augmented Context---at training and inference time, relevant causal triples $\mathcal{C}_i$ are retrieved and concatenated with $S_i$, converting implicit domain knowledge into explicit Encoder context; (iii)Systematic Fine-tuning Comparison---we conduct a rigorous comparison of LoRA, QLoRA, and QA-LoRA on a fixed Golden Testset (116 stratified samples), demonstrating that QLoRA achieves the optimal trade-off: identical test accuracy (87.07%) to full-precision LoRA, 11% faster inference, 72% lower GPU memory, and superior generalization across diverse unseen inputs. A controlled Golden Testset---stratified, deduplicated, and difficulty-tagged---is introduced as a reusable benchmark contribution. QLoRA further outperforms LoRA by 13 percentage points on a 100-sample diverse evaluation spanning all 10 damage cause classes.These findings enable memory-efficient, high-accuracy diagnostic agents on consumer-grade hardware for edge deployment.

</details>


### [99] [CARNet Cycle-Conditioned Core Aggregation and Redistribution for Multivariate Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21681)
*Awsaf Tausif Adib,Md. Shahria Sarker Shuvo,Md. Estehaar Ahmed Emon,Mustafa Kamal,Fuad Rahman,Shafin Rahman,Nabeel Mohammed*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CARNet is a cycle-conditioned core aggregation and redistribution framework for multivariate time series forecasting that integrates global periodic structure into linear-complexity core-based cross-variate dependency modeling, outperforming transformer and non-attention baselines across multiple benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing attention-based models suffer from quadratic complexity and scale poorly with increasing variates, while attention-free core aggregation methods fail to explicitly leverage global periodic structure in the data.

Method: CARNet integrates global recurrent cycle information into efficient core-based interaction modeling via Multihead Core Aggregation within a Cycle-Conditioned Core Aggregation and Redistribution framework.

Result: Consistently outperforms strong transformer and non-attention baselines across diverse prediction horizons on multiple real-world multivariate forecasting benchmarks.

Conclusion: CARNet achieves superior multivariate time series forecasting performance while preserving linear-complexity modeling of cross-variate dependencies, effectively leveraging periodic patterns.

Abstract: Accurately modeling cross-variate dependencies remains a key challenge in multivariate time series forecasting, particularly in the presence of strong periodic patterns. Many existing approaches rely on attention-based mechanisms that incur quadratic complexity and scale poorly with increasing numbers of variates. Recent attention-free aggregation models address this issue through linear-complexity core-based interactions, but they do not explicitly leverage the global periodic structure present in the data. To overcome this limitation, we propose CARNet, a Cycle-Conditioned Core Aggregation and Redistribution framework that integrates global recurrent cycle information into efficient core based interaction modeling via Multihead Core Aggregation. Extensive experiments on multiple real-world multivariate forecasting benchmarks demonstrate that CARNet consistently outperforms strong transformer and non-attention baselines across diverse prediction horizons while preserving linear-complexity modeling of cross-variate dependencies.

</details>


### [100] [Learning What Matters: Supervising Sparse Attention Routing with Causal Evidence Sets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21692)
*Jim Allchin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Attention patterns in transformers often misalign with actual causal dependence; training sparse attention selectors on causal evidence (via masking) outperforms distillation from attention, matching or exceeding teacher performance without annotation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test whether attention patterns in dense transformers reveal the actual evidence used for predictions, and whether distilling attention into sparse selectors is a reliable training target.

Method: Uses causal masking to measure evidence dependence (vs. attention), compares selectors trained on attention vs. causal evidence, evaluates on retrieval tasks and pretrained models (Qwen2.5-3B, Gemma-2-9B).

Result: Attention and causal dependence often disagree; distilled selectors inherit the mismatch. Causal-evidence-trained selectors reach 99% accuracy vs. 41% for attention-trained ones. Causal evidence is recoverable via masking alone without annotation.

Conclusion: Attention shows where a model looks, not necessarily what it depends on; causal dependence is a superior training target for sparse attention selectors.

Abstract: Sparse attention reduces the cost of long contexts by allowing each query to read only selected parts of the input. These selectors are often trained by distilling the attention patterns of a dense teacher, assuming that attention reveals which context the teacher actually uses. We test that assumption on retrieval tasks where the evidence for each answer is known exactly. By masking parts of the context and measuring whether the answer changes, we find that attention and causal dependence often disagree, and distilled selectors inherit the mismatch. Teachers attend to outdated facts they have learned to ignore, and their attention can vary across training runs even when they rely on the same evidence. In a two-step reference task, attention at the answer skips the intermediate step because it was resolved earlier in the forward pass: a selector trained on attention achieves 41% accuracy, while the same selector trained on causal evidence reaches 99% and matches the teacher. These evidence sets require no annotation: recovered from a frozen teacher by masking alone, they train selectors to the same accuracy. We find the same conflict in pretrained models: Qwen2.5-3B gives more attention to an outdated fact than the current one on 58% of conflicting-fact examples despite answering correctly, while Gemma-2-9B rises from 56% to 99% accuracy when restricted to the two relevant sentences. Attention shows where a model looks, not necessarily what its answer depends on; across the regimes we tested, that dependence matched or outperformed attention as a training target.

</details>


### [101] [An Introduction to Bayesian and Frequentist Simulation-Based Inference with Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21702)
*Maximilian Dax,Theo Heimel,Gilles Louppe*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper reviews machine-learning-based simulation-based inference (SBI) methods for solving inverse problems, covering Bayesian and frequentist frameworks, neural posterior and likelihood estimation techniques, applications to Empirical Bayes and unfolding, and validation approaches with limitations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The growing importance of SBI with machine learning for solving inverse problems in science and engineering motivates a need for a comprehensive overview that bridges statistical frameworks and modern ML-based methods.

Method: The paper provides a structured overview connecting Bayesian and frequentist statistical frameworks to machine-learning-based SBI methods, specifically neural posterior estimation (NPE) and neural likelihood estimation (NLE), and extends these to Empirical Bayes and unfolding tasks.

Result: The same machine-learning-based SBI methods (NPE, NLE) can be applied across Bayesian inference, frequentist inference, Empirical Bayes, and unfolding tasks, with appropriate validation strategies needed for each context.

Conclusion: SBI with machine learning is a versatile tool for parameter estimation and detector effect inversion across statistical paradigms, though careful validation is required and limitations exist that practitioners must consider.

Abstract: Simulation-based inference (SBI) with machine learning is an increasingly important tool for solving inverse problems in science and engineering, including parameter inference and the inversion of detector effects. We provide an overview of the Bayesian and frequentist statistical frameworks, describe how machine-learning-based SBI methods, such as neural posterior estimation and neural likelihood estimation, can be used for parameter estimation within these frameworks, and show that the same methods can also be applied to Empirical Bayes or unfolding tasks.
