We also discuss how to validate inference results and the limitations of SBI with machine learning.

</details>


### [102] [A Defense of the Quadratic Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21716)
*Alexandru Meterez,Pranav Ajit Nair,Depen Morwani,Cengiz Pehlevan,Sham Kakade,Alex Damian*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper shows that a simple quadratic model can accurately predict LLM optimization dynamics (150M params, 3B tokens) over windows up to 10% of training. Using Hessian spectrum analysis via Lanczos quadrature and local stability tests, they reveal structured eigenvalue/eigenvector behavior dependent on batch size, preconditioner, and training time, and find that LLM optimization operates at a stochastic edge of stability determined by batch size.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To bridge the gap between simplified theoretical optimization models and the complexity of neural network loss landscapes by stress testing the quadratic model's predictive power in realistic large-scale LLM training.

Method: Taylor expansion of model and loss at intermediate checkpoints to form local quadratic approximations; Lanczos quadrature with deep probes for Hessian spectrum estimation; empirical testing of local linear stability compared to theoretical predictions.

Result: Quadratic model accurately predicts optimization dynamics over windows up to 10% of training. Hessian spectra reveal rich structure in eigenvalues/eigenvectors depending on batch size, preconditioner, and training time. LLM optimization operates at a stochastic edge of stability influenced by batch size.

Conclusion: The quadratic model serves as a theoretically tractable and empirically accurate proxy for pretraining optimization dynamics in LLMs, despite its simplicity.

Abstract: Due to the complexity of neural network loss landscapes, optimization theory is forced to rely on idealized models, and there is generally a tradeoff between how theoretically tractable the model is, and how accurately it describes the true optimization dynamics. In this work, we stress test the simplest possible model of optimization -- the quadratic model -- and show that it can be surprisingly predictive in an LLM setting with 150M parameters and 3B training tokens. Specifically, we show that Taylor expanding the model and the loss function at intermediate checkpoints through training can accurately predict the optimization dynamics over windows that can last up to 10\% of training. Having established this agreement, we then turn to analyzing the structure of these local quadratic optimization problems through two lenses: the Hessian spectrum and local stability. Using Lanczos quadrature with extremely deep probes, we are able to estimate the Hessian spectrum deep into the tail, and we find a surprising amount of structure in both the eigenvalues and eigenvectors, which depends on the batch size, preconditioner, and training time. We also empirically test local linear stability at intermediate checkpoints and compare it to theoretical predictions to demonstrate that optimization in LLMs typically occurs at a stochastic edge of stability, whose nature is also determined by batch size. Our results indicate the quadratic model may be a theoretically tractable proxy for pretraining optimization dynamics.

</details>


### [103] [RED-PIM: Reducing Data Movement for Transformers using Processing-in-Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21731)
*Zahra Yousefijamarani,Alaa Alameldeen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RED-PIM is an algorithm-architecture co-design that reduces transformer inference latency by minimizing inter-bank data movement from O(N²) to O(N) and shrinking attention matrices from N×N to d×d, achieving 66.42% geometric mean inference time reduction over baseline PIM.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transformers suffer from high data movement between processing units and memory during attention operations. While PIM can mitigate this, prior PIM-based implementations suffer from costly inter-bank communication and limited memory bank capacity, forcing attention data to be split across banks and diminishing PIM benefits.

Method: RED-PIM employs an algorithm-architecture co-design that: (1) reorganizes matrix operations to perform computations locally within memory banks, (2) reduces inter-bank data movement from O(N²) to O(N), (3) shrinks intermediate attention matrices from N×N to d×d, and (4) uses an optimized data transfer strategy to minimize computation cost and interconnect traffic.

Result: Compared to baseline PIM, RED-PIM achieves 16.05% to 99.99% inference time reduction (geometric mean 66.42%), with largest gains on longer sequences. On real-world datasets, it improves performance by 99.60% for long documents and 13.44% for shorter ones, while maintaining or improving accuracy.

Conclusion: RED-PIM demonstrates effectiveness for scalable and efficient transformer inference, achieving significant latency reductions especially on long sequences, proving the value of co-designing algorithms with PIM architectures to overcome memory bottlenecks.

Abstract: Transformers are widely used across many domains, including natural language processing, computer vision, web search, and DNA sequence analysis. Given their broad applicability, improving the performance of transformer models is critical. However, the high volume of data movement between processing units and memory during attention operations significantly limits their efficiency. Processing-In-Memory (PIM) mitigates this issue by performing computations directly inside memory. While prior work has proposed PIM-based transformer implementations, they suffer from costly inter-bank communication, and struggle to scale due to the limited capacity of memory banks. As a result, attention-related data must be split across banks, diminishing the potential benefits of PIM.
  In this work, we propose RED-PIM, an algorithm-architecture co-design that reduces attention latency by minimizing inter-bank data movement from O(N^2) to O(N) and shrinking intermediate attention matrices from N x N to d x d. By reorganizing matrix operations, performing computations locally, and employing an optimized data transfer strategy, RED-PIM significantly reduces computation cost and interconnect traffic. Compared to baseline PIM implementation, RED-PIM achieves inference time reductions ranging from 16.05% to 99.99% (geometric mean of 66.42%), with the largest gains on longer sequences. On real-world datasets, RED-PIM improves performance by 99.60% for long documents and 13.44% for shorter ones, while maintaining or improving accuracy. These results demonstrate RED-PIM's effectiveness for scalable and efficient transformer inference.

</details>


### [104] [Parameter-free Adaptive Sparse Attention via Compression-Based Content Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21752)
*Debarshi Kundu,Swaroop Ghosh,Vasant Honavar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces a novel approach using gzip compression ratios as data-adaptive sparse attention masks for long sequences, requiring no additional parameters, custom kernels, or auxiliary losses. It outperforms dense attention and existing sparse/adaptive methods on PG-19 byte-level language modeling, achieving 1.71 BPB at 8K context with 92M parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing adaptive sparse attention methods (e.g., SBM-Transformer, Dynamic Mask Attention, NSA) rely on extra learnable parameters, custom gradient estimators, or specialized CUDA kernels, increasing complexity. The goal is to achieve data-adaptive sparse attention without these overheads.

Method: Compute per-block gzip compression ratios to identify non-redundant content blocks. Blocks with low compressibility (high entropy) are selected as long-range attention targets, forming an input-dependent sparse mask. No learned parameters, auxiliary losses, or custom CUDA kernels are needed.

Result: On PG-19 byte-level LM (92M params, 8K context): 1.71 BPB vs dense (2.89), BigBird (2.34), Longformer (3.21), SBM-Transformer (3.38). Gap over BigBird widens from 0.05 BPB at 4K to 0.63 BPB at 8K. Convergence is 3.3× faster.

Conclusion: Classical data compression (gzip) provides an effective, parameter-free masking signal for adaptive sparse attention, outperforming both fixed and learned-mask baselines, with growing advantages on longer sequences.

Abstract: Data-adaptive sparse attention masks substantially outperform fixed patterns (e.g., BigBird and Longformer) and can even exceed dense attention on long sequences. Existing adaptive approaches---including SBM-Transformer, Dynamic Mask Attention, and NSA---typically require additional learnable parameters, custom gradient estimators, or specialized CUDA kernels.
  We show that classical data compression provides an effective masking signal with \textbf{no additional parameters}. By computing per-block gzip compression ratios, we identify non-redundant content blocks and route long-range attention selectively through them. Intuitively, blocks that gzip cannot compress contain information not predictable from local repetition, making them natural long-range attention targets. Because the compression profile is input-dependent, the resulting sparse mask adapts dynamically to content without learned parameters, auxiliary losses, or custom kernels.
  On PG-19 byte-level language modeling at 92M parameters with 8K context, our method achieves 1.71 bits-per-byte (BPB), outperforming dense attention (2.89), BigBird (2.34), Longformer (3.21), and a reimplemented SBM-Transformer (3.38)---the only learned-mask baseline---by up to 1.67 BPB while adding no parameters. The advantage grows with sequence length, with the gap over BigBird widening from 0.05 BPB at 4K context to 0.63 BPB at 8K, while convergence is 3.3$\times$ faster.

</details>


### [105] [Smart predict-then-robustly-optimize](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21773)
*Aakil Caunhye,Xuefei Lu,Belen Martin-Barragan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a robust variant of the smart predict-then-optimize approach that handles corrupted covariates by integrating robust optimization into the predictive-prescriptive pipeline, with theoretical guarantees on approximation error and Fisher consistency, and demonstrates superior performance over standard methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional predict-then-optimize models assume side information is perfectly revealed, but in practice, empirical features are often corrupted or noisy at decision time, leading to fragile operational policies.

Method: Integrates robust optimization into the predictive-prescriptive pipeline via a smart predict-then-robustly optimize loss, establishing a computationally tractable convex surrogate that hedges against worst-case feature perturbations.

Result: The surrogate's approximation error probability decays exponentially (sub-Gaussian concentration); the surrogate is Fisher consistent with high probability under mild assumptions; the framework outperforms standard smart predict-then-optimize and maintains superiority even when the standard method uses regularized upstream predictions.

Conclusion: The robust framework consistently yields significant performance improvements over standard methods in both out-of-sample terms and training stability.

Abstract: In this paper, we propose and study a robust variant of the smart predict-then-optimize approach that accounts for prediction shifts due to disturbance in the covariate feature space. While traditional integrated-learning-and-optimization models assume that side information is perfectly revealed, empirical data-driven features are frequently corrupted or noisy at the time of decision-making, leading to fragile operational policies. To bridge this gap, we integrate principles of robust optimization directly into the predictive-prescriptive pipeline via a smart predict-then-robustly optimize loss and establish a computationally tractable convex surrogate, designed to hedge against worst-case feature perturbations. On the theoretical front, we formalize the structural validity of this surrogate by proving its approximation error probability decays exponentially according to a sub-Gaussian concentration profile. Furthermore, we establish that under mild assumptions, the surrogate is Fisher consistent with high probability. We also prove necessary conditions under which our framework outperforms standard smart predict-then-optimize and maintain its superiority even when the standard method is equipped with regularized upstream predictions. Numerical experiments validate that our robust framework consistently yields significant performance improvements over standard methods, both in out-of-sample terms and in training stability.

</details>


### [106] [Bounding the Causal Impact of ML-assisted Decision-Making via Counterfactual Correctness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21806)
*Jonathan Zhang,Erik Skalnes,Jacob Chen,Michael Oberst*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a partial-identification method using prior RCT data to bound the causal effect of a new ML model, leveraging monotonicity assumptions about predictive accuracy and downstream outcomes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RCTs are the gold standard for evaluating the causal impact of deployed ML models, but repeated trials are infeasible when models are frequently updated or retrained. The paper aims to address this evaluation gap.

Method: The authors develop a partial-identification approach that uses prior RCT data to construct bounds on the causal effect of a new model. They introduce two monotonicity assumptions: (1) individual-level 'counterfactual correctness'—a correct prediction leads to non-inferior outcomes; (2) a subgroup-level assumption relating predictive performance to outcomes, interpreted as trust in model outputs.

Result: Simulation study demonstrates that incorporating the proposed monotonicity assumptions yields more informative (tighter) bounds compared to prior work.

Conclusion: Leveraging fine-grained predictive accuracy assumptions enables more informative causal effect bounds for new ML models without requiring new RCTs, addressing a key practical bottleneck in ML deployment evaluation.

Abstract: Predictive machine learning (ML) models are increasingly used to aid human decision-makers across various high-risk domains such as healthcare and criminal justice. There is a growing recognition of the need to evaluate the causal impact of deploying these systems on downstream outcomes, such as patient survival or crime recidivism. Randomized control trials (RCTs) can provide high-quality evidence on the impact of a deployed model, but they run into a challenge: it is often infeasible to run repeated trials when models are updated or retrained to improve predictive performance. In this work, we present a partial-identification approach to using prior RCT data to construct bounds on the causal effect of a new model. The core innovation in our approach is to leverage assumptions relating fine-grained predictive accuracy to downstream outcomes. We do so via two monotonicity assumptions: first, on individual-level `counterfactual correctness' (all else being equal, a correct prediction leads to non-inferior outcomes); and second, on the relation between subgroup predictive performance and outcomes, interpretable as an assumption regarding trust in model outputs. We demonstrate our method with a simulation study, illustrating how incorporating this information can lead to more informative bounds compared to prior work.

</details>


### [107] [Data eccentricity, asymptotics of Gaussian RBF reproducing kernel Hilbert space, and kernel PCA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21823)
*Sergio A. Alvarez*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Gaussian RBF RKHS becomes asymptotically isometric to Euclidean space as bandwidth σ → ∞, causing kernel methods (CKA, PCA) to converge to linear kernel results, with convergence rate governed by a geometric eccentricity ratio ρ.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the asymptotic behavior of Gaussian RBF kernels in the large bandwidth limit and establish theoretical connections between kernel methods and classical linear methods.

Method: Theoretical analysis proving asymptotic isometry between Gaussian RBF RKHS and Euclidean space; analysis of Gaussian CKA and kernel PCA convergence to linear counterparts; derivation of convergence rate bound O((ρ/σ)²) where ρ is the eccentricity ratio; experimental validation across diverse datasets.

Result: Gaussian RBF eigenvalues, eigenprojections, and principal components converge to those of linear PCA as σ→∞. The RKHS feature embeddings and PCA eigenframes differ by a geometric similarity transformation with residual O((ρ/σ)²). The eccentricity ratio ρ reliably predicts dataset-specific convergence behavior.

Conclusion: For large bandwidths, Gaussian RBF kernels behave like linear kernels, with ρ serving as a simple predictor of convergence rate. This bridges kernel methods and classical PCA, providing practical guidance for bandwidth selection.

Abstract: We show that, up to isotropic scaling, the Gaussian RBF reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS) is asymptotically isometric to Euclidean space in the large bandwidth limit. This strongly suggests that kernel-based constructions reliant on metric properties of the RKHS will yield results for Gaussian RBF kernels that similarly approach those of linear kernels for large bandwidths. The asymptotic behavior of Gaussian CKA can be understood in this light. We further consider kernel PCA, showing that Gaussian RBF eigenvalues, eigenprojections, and principal components all converge to those of classical (linear) PCA as bandwidth $σ\rightarrow \infty$. For a given data representation, both the RKHS feature embeddings and the orthogonal PCA eigenframes of the two kernel types differ asymptotically by a geometric similarity transformation, up to a residual of size $O \left (\fracρσ \right )^2$, where $ρ$ is a measure of geometric eccentricity of the representation, equal to the ratio of maximum to median pairwise distance between data examples. Experiments over a diverse collection of data sets demonstrate that $ρ$ provides a simple and reliable predictor of dataset-specific convergence behavior in the top principal directions.

</details>


### [108] [A Graph-Based Control Interface for Traffic Signals on Heterogeneous Road Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21831)
*Bertil Braun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A traffic-signal control interface using a shared GNN with deterministic incidence matrices enables variable-sized phase sets independent of network parameters. PPO experiments on synthetic grids and five city graphs show geometry robustness but sensitivity to signal-coverage shifts, with heterogeneous city transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To design a traffic-signal control interface where graph size and action counts are independent of learned parameter shapes, enabling flexible transfer across different road network geometries and signal configurations.

Method: Shared GNN assigns scores to movements; deterministic incidence matrices convert scores to variable-sized phase sets per junction. Directed corridor nodes provide context; typed mean aggregation yields scalars. PPO trains policies on synthetic grids and five city graphs.

Result: Policies retained performance on unseen synthetic grid geometries but showed sensitivity to signal-coverage distribution shifts. Single city-policy executed across five city graphs with heterogeneous outcomes.

Conclusion: The approach shows feasibility for transfer within similar geometry families but not generalizable transfer to arbitrary road networks; signal-coverage shifts remain a challenge.

Abstract: We present a traffic-signal control interface in which a shared graph neural network assigns scores to individual traffic movements. Each junction converts these scores into its own variable-sized set of legal signal phases using a deterministic incidence matrix. Directed corridor nodes provide traffic context, while movement nodes represent controlled input-to-output paths through junctions. Typed mean aggregation produces one scalar per movement; phase definitions and signal timing remain outside the learned network. This makes graph size and junction-specific action count independent of the learned parameter shapes. PPO experiments evaluate the interface on unseen synthetic grid geometries, altered signal coverage, and five heterogeneous city graphs. The policies retained performance across unseen geometries within the synthetic grid family, while changes in signal coverage exposed sensitivity to a signal-coverage distribution shift. A single trained city-policy instance executed across all five city graphs, with heterogeneous outcomes. These results provide feasibility evidence rather than a general estimate of transfer to arbitrary road networks.

</details>


### [109] [Searching the Space of Feed-Forward Neural-Network Weight-Update Rules with Fixed Depth Symbolic Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21855)
*Charles Brum,Edward Finkelstein*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Symbolic regression discovers neural network weight-update rules that outperform hand-designed optimizers on small symbolic regression benchmarks, achieving 44.47% MSE reduction in 25 out of 30 cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether symbolic regression can discover explicit neural network weight-update rules that outperform standard hand-designed optimizers.

Method: Candidate update rules are represented as fixed-depth symbolic expressions over operands derived from common optimizers (gradient, momentum, adaptive-gradient, moment-estimate). Tested across 30 benchmark/neural network combinations.

Result: Found update rule outperforming best hyperparameter-tuned established optimizer in 25/30 cases, with aggregate MSE reduction of 44.47% over improved cases.

Conclusion: Symbolic regression can serve as a lightweight mechanism for discovering compact optimizer variants, though larger-scale validation is needed.

Abstract: We investigate whether symbolic regression can discover explicit neural network weight-update rules that outperform standard hand-designed optimizers on small symbolic regression benchmarks. Candidate update rules are represented as fixed-depth symbolic expressions over operands derived from common optimizers, including gradient, momentum, adaptive-gradient, and moment-estimate quantities. Across 30 benchmark/neural network combinations, the symbolic regression procedure found an update rule outperforming the best hyperparameter-tuned established optimizer in 25 cases, with an aggregate MSE reduction of 44.47\% over the improved cases. The discovered rules do not all share a single common symbolic form, but many combine adaptive normalization, momentum-like quantities, nonlinear transformations, and rational expressions. These results suggest that symbolic regression can serve as a lightweight mechanism for discovering compact optimizer variants, while also highlighting the need for larger-scale validation.

</details>


### [110] [LeAct: Learning to Reason from Expert Actions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21856)
*Ziran Yang,Chengshuai Shi,Raj Ghugare,Benjamin Eysenbach,Karthik Narasimhan,Chi Jin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LeAct extracts latent chain-of-thought reasoning from silent expert systems (e.g., game engines, planners, theorem provers) by treating the CoT as a hidden variable, optimizing student-generated candidates that improve action recovery. Across games and robotics, LeAct outperforms expert-iteration baselines and enables expert systems as novel reasoning teachers for foundation models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current reasoning models rely on human annotations or distilled LLMs for reasoning data. Expert systems (game engines, planners, theorem provers) produce near-optimal actions but are "silent" — they don't provide chain-of-thought reasoning behind their actions. The goal is to recover natural-language reasoning from expert actions, turning expert systems into reasoning teachers.

Method: LeAct treats the chain-of-thought as a latent variable. The student samples candidate CoTs for each expert action; only those that measurably improve the student's own probability of recovering the action are retained. This optimizes the latent CoT variable to best explain the observed expert action.

Result: On small enumerable imperfect-information games, LeAct reaches the solver's numerical floor. At larger scale, it is 5× closer to the solver than the strongest expert-iteration baseline. On Flop Hold'em (~10⁹ infosets), LeAct wins head-to-head by +60 mbb/g. On robotics, it is the only training recipe that improves on direct imitation.

Conclusion: Expert systems can serve as a categorically new source of reasoning teachers for foundation models, using the LeAct framework to extract latent chain-of-thought reasoning from their actions alone.

Abstract: Modern reasoning models depend on reasoning data, today sourced from human annotations or distilled from stronger LLMs. However, a rich and largely untapped source of supervision lies in expert systems (e.g., game engines, classical planners, theorem provers), which routinely produce near-optimal actions across diverse domains. But these experts are silent: they commit to an action without writing down the chain of thought (CoT) behind it. Recovering that CoT as natural-language reasoning would distill expert knowledge into a student that generalizes beyond the demonstrated actions. We treat it as a latent variable and study how to recover it from the action alone. Our approach, LeAct (Learning to reason from Actions), optimizes this latent variable: the student samples candidate CoTs for each expert action, and we retain those that measurably improve its own probability of recovering the action. Across imperfect-information games at multiple scales and a simulated robotics benchmark, LeAct reaches the solver's numerical floor on small enumerable games. At larger scale, it is $5\times$ closer to the solver than the strongest expert-iteration baseline. At Flop Hold'em ($\sim 10^9$ infosets), LeAct wins head-to-head by $+60$ mbb/g, and on the robotics probe it is the only training recipe that improves on direct imitation. We present a principled framework and the result: expert systems become a categorically new source of reasoning teachers for foundation models.

</details>


### [111] [Scaling Laws for Classical Machine Learning on Tabular Data: A Benchmark Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21866)
*Kaihua Ding*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A large-scale classroom replication study (127 students, 11,536 runs, 1,648 power-law curves) shows that learning curves for tabular data follow power laws (R²>0.8 on 77.7% of cells), with tree ensembles dominating at full data. Model families exhibit approximate shared exponents but not universal scaling (Lasso fails, Ridge is fragile). Replicator-implementation variance introduces exponent spread (CV=0.144) even with fixed seeds, due to unconstrained preprocessing choices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior learning-curve work focused on small-scale, single-team studies of tree, linear, and kernel models on tabular data. This paper aims to validate and extend those findings at much larger scale through distributed replication, and to quantify the variability introduced by different implementers following the same protocol.

Method: 127 students each ran a fixed protocol on 3 assigned datasets from 18 tabular classification/regression datasets and 6 model families (Boosting, Random Forest, SVM, Linear/Logistic, Ridge, Lasso), yielding 11,536 training runs and 1,648 fitted power-law curves of the form error(N) = a N^(-b) + c.

Result: Power laws fit well (R²>0.8 on 77.7% of cells); tree ensembles dominate (Boosting best on 50%, RF on 33%). 5 of 6 families show approximate shared exponents across datasets (R² gap <0.011 versus per-dataset fits), but Lasso fails and Ridge is fragile under cross-validation. Replicator-implementation variance yields mean CV(b)=0.144 even with fixed random seeds.

Conclusion: Power-law learning curves reliably describe ML performance on tabular data at scale. Shared exponents exist within model families but represent approximate compressibility, not universal scaling. Implementation details beyond random seeds cause nontrivial variance in fitted curves, highlighting the need for standardized protocols in replication studies.

Abstract: Prior classical-ML learning-curve work fits power laws to tree, linear, and kernel models on tabular data, but at small scale: typically one curve, one team, a handful of cells. We present a distributed classroom-scale replication: 127 students each ran a fixed protocol on 3 assigned datasets, drawn from 18 tabular classification and regression datasets and 6 model families (Boosting, Random Forest, SVM, Linear/Logistic, Ridge, Lasso), yielding 11,536 training runs and 1,648 fitted power-law curves of the form error(N) = a N^(-b) + c. Three findings. (1) Power laws fit: R^2 > 0.8 on 77.7% of cells, with tree ensembles dominating at full data (Boosting 50% of datasets, RandomForest 33%; linear models underperform on classification). (2) Approximate shared exponents within a model family: for 5 of 6 families, a single family-level exponent predicts each family's cross-dataset curves nearly as well as per-dataset exponents (R^2 gap < 0.011), though AIC favors the unconstrained fit and curve collapse is partial (32-58% of points within +/-0.5 dex). We frame this as approximate predictive compressibility, not dataset-independent universality; Lasso fails outright (negative control) and Ridge is fragile under leave-one-dataset-out. (3) Replicator-implementation variance: with random_state=42 fixed, independent re-implementations of the same protocol still differ by mean CV(b) = 0.144 on the fitted exponent -- not seed variance, but the spread induced by unconstrained parts of the protocol (preprocessing, encoding, missing-value handling). We release the aggregated curves, per-cell fits, and a practical data-requirement table for N* to reach target error 0.15.

</details>


### [112] [Variance-Reduced Q-Learning over Static and Time-Varying Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21876)
*Sreejeet Maity,Feng Zhu,Aritra Mitra,Robert W. Heath*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces VRDQ, a decentralized Q-learning algorithm for multi-agent reinforcement learning that achieves linear speedups from collaboration with only $\tilde{O}(1)$ communication complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a communication-efficient decentralized reinforcement learning algorithm that enables multiple agents to collaboratively learn the optimal Q-function in a shared MDP while minimizing communication overhead.

Method: Proposes VRDQ, an epoch-based distributed Q-learning algorithm where agents locally estimate the Bellman optimality operator and diffuse information via consensus-based protocols, for both static and time-varying networks.

Result: Establishes high-probability finite-time convergence rates for VRDQ that enjoy linear speedups from collaboration, and proves such speedups require only $\tilde{O}(1)$ communication.

Conclusion: VRDQ achieves significant communication efficiency (only $\tilde{O}(1)$ communication) while maintaining linear speedups in sample complexity, substantially improving upon prior work.

Abstract: We investigate a decentralized reinforcement learning problem involving multiple agents that interact with the same Markov Decision Process (MDP). The agents can exchange information over a network to collectively learn the optimal state-action value function. For this setting, we introduce a novel epoch-based distributed $Q$-learning algorithm called VRDQ, where within each epoch, agents locally estimate the Bellman optimality operator and diffuse information using a consensus-based protocol. For both static and time-varying networks, we establish high-probability finite-time convergence rates for VRDQ that enjoy linear speedups from collaboration. Crucially, we prove that such speedups in sample-complexity require only $\tilde{O}(1)$ communication, substantially improving upon the communication costs in prior work.

</details>


### [113] [Remedying Coarsening-Based GNN Training under Heterophily via Adaptive Complementary Enhancement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21885)
*Guoming Li,Jian Yang,Xukun Wang,Zixiao Wang,Shangsong Liang,Yifan Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ACE is a plug-and-play strategy that addresses performance degradation of coarsening-based GNN training on heterophilic graphs by reintegrating discarded information via feature reconstruction and anisotropic structural regularization, balanced by homoscedastic uncertainty weighting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing coarsening-based GNN training methods show significant performance degradation on heterophilic graphs due to inevitable loss of graph information during coarsening, while prior work has been evaluated almost exclusively on homophilic graphs.

Method: ACE learns a projector for reconstructing original node features and applies anisotropic structural regularization to embed local heterophily, using homoscedastic uncertainty weighting to adaptively balance the primary coarsened-graph training loss and the full-graph auxiliary loss with augmented features.

Result: ACE drives consistent gains on heterophilic benchmarks while preserving competitive results on homophilic graphs with minimal computational overhead.

Conclusion: ACE effectively addresses the underexplored heterophilic graph coarsening problem as a model-agnostic, plug-and-play strategy with strong empirical results and minimal overhead.

Abstract: Coarsening-based training for graph neural networks (GNNs), i.e.\ training on coarsened graphs rather than the original large ones, has become a promising direction for scaling GNNs to massive graphs. However, prior work has been evaluated almost exclusively on \textit{homophilic} graphs, leaving the more challenging \textit{heterophilic} settings underexplored. We show, both empirically and theoretically, that existing coarsening-based training methods suffer significant performance degradation on heterophilic graphs due to inevitable loss of graph information during coarsening. To address this, we propose {\bf A}daptive {\bf C}omplementary {\bf E}nhancement, a plug-and-play, model-agnostic strategy that reintegrates the information discarded in coarsening: ACE learns a projector for re-constructing original node features and applies \textit{anisotropic structural regularization} to embed local heterophily. We further adopt \textit{homoscedastic uncertainty weighting} to adaptively balance the combined training objective of primary coarsened-graph training loss and full-graph auxiliary loss with augmented node features re-constructed by the heterophily-aware projector. Extensive experiments show that ACE drives consistent gains on heterophilic benchmarks while preserving competitive results on homophilic graphs with minimal computational overhead. Code is available at the GitHub repository: https://github.com/vasile-paskardlgm/ACE.

</details>


### [114] [MissHyper: Restoring Clinical Synchronicity in Missingness-Guided Hypergraph Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21922)
*Mingyi Ma,Qingxiong Tan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MissHyper is a missingness-guided hypergraph forecasting model that restores co-timestamp context before message passing to improve sparse clinical irregular time series forecasting, achieving consistent gains across PhysioNet 2012, MIMIC-III, and MIMIC-IV.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical irregular multivariate time series are shaped by both physiological dynamics and measurement processes, but existing event-centric models flatten co-timestamp structure too early, embedding measurements at the same timestamp as isolated nodes and leaving local patient-state context unavailable until later message-passing layers.

Method: MissHyper augments each event with a local support-density cue, aggregates co-timestamp records to recover patient-state context, and uses a missingness-guided gate to adaptively fuse node-specific evidence with the recovered context before propagation begins.

Result: Across PhysioNet 2012, MIMIC-III, and MIMIC-IV, MissHyper achieves consistent gains in multi-step forecasting and outperforms a strong hypergraph baseline.

Conclusion: Improving event initialization can benefit sparse clinical forecasting without requiring a redesigned downstream propagation architecture; snapshot restoration, adaptive fusion, and support-density encoding all contribute, pointing to event initialization as a critical design axis.

Abstract: Clinical irregular multivariate time series are shaped not only by physiological dynamics but also by the measurement process that determines when and what to observe. In event-centric models, however, co-timestamp structure can be flattened too early: measurements acquired at the same timestamp are embedded as isolated nodes, leaving local patient-state context unavailable until later message-passing layers. We study this pre-propagation representation bottleneck and address it by restoring co-timestamp context before message passing begins. We propose MissHyper, a missingness-guided hypergraph forecasting model with pre-propagation synchronicity restoration. MissHyper augments each event with a local support-density cue, aggregates co-timestamp records to recover patient-state context, and uses a missingness-guided gate to adaptively fuse node-specific evidence with the recovered context. Across PhysioNet 2012, MIMIC-III, and MIMIC-IV, MissHyper achieves consistent gains in multi-step forecasting and outperforms a strong hypergraph baseline. These results suggest that improving event initialization can benefit sparse clinical forecasting without requiring a redesigned downstream propagation architecture. Ablations indicate that snapshot restoration, adaptive fusion, and support-density encoding all contribute, pointing to event initialization as a critical design axis for sparse clinical forecasting.

</details>


### [115] [RIS-Kernel: A Model-Agnostic Architecture for Long-Context LLM Inference via Sparse Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21927)
*Anderson R. Santos*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RIS is a model-agnostic inference engine that reduces self-attention complexity from O(N²) to O(N log N) using sparse stochastic geometry, enabling long-context LLM inference on commodity CPU hardware (16-128 GB RAM). At 32K tokens, 1% density with 70 seeds achieves 75.00% accuracy (exceeding dense baseline 71.88%), and at 65K tokens (where dense attention OOMs), RIS achieves up to 14.06 pp gain over zero-context baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Full O(N²) self-attention limits LLM long-context analysis to 65,536 tokens and requires expensive GPU clusters, making long-context inference inaccessible on commodity hardware.

Method: RIS is a model-agnostic inference engine that reduces self-attention complexity to O(N log N) via sparse stochastic geometry without modifying model weights. It operates in two modes: RIS-Stochastic (multiple ensemble seeds) and RIS-Structural, evaluated on Qwen2-1.5B-Instruct.

Result: At 32K tokens: RIS-Stochastic at 1% density/70 seeds achieves 75.00% (outperforming dense 71.88%), 5% density/10 seeds matches dense (71.88%). At 65K tokens: RIS yields up to 14.06 pp gain over zero-context floor (51.56%), p=0.078. RIS-Structural at 1% density/10 seeds reaches 68.75%, recovering 75% of contextual gap.

Conclusion: Sparse attention via RIS acts as a regularizer—low density with multiple seeds filters noise, outperforming dense attention—and enables long-context LLM inference on unaccelerated CPU servers with commodity memory, demonstrating feasibility without GPU acceleration.

Abstract: Full self-attention in large language models scales as O(N^2), which limits long-context document analysis to 65,536 tokens and requires costly GPU clusters. The Reduced Interaction Sampling (RIS) inference engine addresses this constraint as a model-agnostic architecture. Without modifying weights, RIS reduces self-attention complexity to O(N log N) using sparse stochastic geometry that fits within commodity memory limits. We validate RIS on Qwen2-1.5B-Instruct across two regimes. In controlled evaluations at 32,768 tokens (where native dense attention serves as the upper bound), RIS-Stochastic at 1% density and 70 ensemble seeds achieves 75.00% accuracy, outperforming the native dense baseline (71.88%), while RIS-Stochastic at 5% density and 10 seeds matches it (71.88%). This demonstrates that sparse attention acts as a regularizer: low density (1%) over multiple seeds filters out sequence-level noise, whereas higher density (5%) reintroduces distractor noise. Under the tightest budget, RIS-Structural reaches 68.75% accuracy at 1% density with just 10 seeds, recovering 75% of the contextual gap relative to the zero-context floor (59.38%). At 65,536 tokens, where dense attention triggers out-of-memory faults, RIS yields retrieval gains of up to 14.06 percentage points over the zero-context floor (51.56%), which is confirmed as marginally significant under McNemar's paired test (p = 0.078 < 0.10). All evaluations run on commodity, unaccelerated CPU servers (16-128 GB of RAM), demonstrating that long-context LLM inference is feasible on standard academic hardware without GPU acceleration.

</details>


### [116] [LatentFlow: Visual Analytics for Latent Space Analysis in Molecular Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21941)
*Shiyi Liu,Jiaqing Chen,Nicholas Hadler,Rostyslav Hnatyshyn,Michael W. Mahoney,Talita Perciano,John F. Hartwig,Gunther H. Weber,Ross Maciejewski*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LatentFlow is a visual analytics system for analyzing latent spaces in molecular graph neural networks, using cluster tracking via modified Sankey diagrams and linking to molecular substructures to help scientists understand model behavior and chemical relationships.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods provide limited support for analyzing latent spaces across layers and across different model states (e.g., training epochs, model configurations, and input data) in molecular GNNs, making it difficult to understand how these latent spaces evolve or relate to chemical concepts.

Method: LatentFlow groups embeddings into clusters and supports exploration of latent spaces by tracking how these clusters change across layers and model states using a modified Sankey diagram. It links clusters to representative molecules and their shared substructures, and allows scientists to introduce their own domain knowledge for comparison with latent space patterns.

Result: Two case studies show that LatentFlow helps scientists understand how latent spaces evolve, identify meaningful molecular patterns, and better interpret model behavior.

Conclusion: LatentFlow helps scientists understand how latent spaces evolve, identify meaningful molecular patterns, and better interpret model behavior.

Abstract: Chemists and materials scientists increasingly use machine learning models, such as graph neural networks (GNNs), to predict properties of molecules and the outcomes of their reactions. Beyond predictive performance, understanding how these models organize chemical information internally in their latent spaces, i.e., the embeddings of the molecules, is critical. Analyzing latent spaces helps diagnose model behavior and assess whether the learned embeddings are organized in ways that reflect meaningful chemical relationships. Unfortunately, existing methods provide limited support for analyzing latent spaces across layers and across different model states (e.g., training epochs, model configurations, and input data), making it difficult to understand how these latent spaces evolve throughout a model or relate to chemical concepts. We present LatentFlow, a visual analytics system developed in collaboration with a domain expert for analyzing latent spaces in molecular GNNs. LatentFlow groups embeddings into clusters and supports exploration of latent spaces by tracking how these clusters change across layers and model states using a modified Sankey diagram. To support interpretation, LatentFlow links these clusters to representative molecules and their shared substructures, and it allows scientists to introduce their own domain knowledge and compare it with the patterns found in the latent spaces. We evaluate LatentFlow through two case studies. The results show that LatentFlow helps scientists understand how latent spaces evolve, identify meaningful molecular patterns, and better interpret model behavior.

</details>


### [117] [Multi-Agent Debate and Visual Information Extraction for SeePhys Pro: A 1st-Place Technical Report from ICML 2026 AI4Math Track 3 Challenge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21946)
*Jiseok Kwak,Suhyeon Jo,Taewoo Kim,Yeongmin Kim,Byeonghu Na,Il-chul Moon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A two-stage framework combining visual information extraction and multi-agent debate that won 1st place in the SeePhys Pro track, improving accuracy from 0.643 to 0.802 on public split and achieving 0.743 on private leaderboard.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Visual physics problems are substantially harder for LLMs when decisive information resides in figures rather than text, and this modality gap widens as more problem content migrates into images.

Method: A two-stage framework: (1) visual information extraction stage that re-expresses figure content as solver-readable text to close the modality gap; (2) reasoning stage that orchestrates three heterogeneous solvers through multi-agent debate.

Result: Overall accuracy improved from 0.643 to 0.802 on public split; won 1st place on both public and private leaderboard (private overall 0.743). Gain from orchestration comes from reliable answer selection rather than additional debate.

Conclusion: The pipeline effectively closes the modality gap between visual and textual information in physics problems, and multi-agent orchestration benefits primarily from answer selection rather than debate. Value of figure aid scales with proportion of problem content in images.

Abstract: This technical report presents our approach to Challenge Track~3: SeePhys Pro at the 3rd AI for Math Workshop, where the task is to answer college-level physics questions whose statement and figure may be given partly or entirely as an image. Visual physics problems become substantially harder for large language models when the decisive information resides in a figure rather than in the text, and this modality gap widens as more of the problem migrates into the image. We address the task with a two-stage framework: a visual information extraction stage that re-expresses figure content as solver-readable text to close the modality gap, and a reasoning stage that orchestrates three heterogeneous solvers through multi-agent debate. Our analysis yields two findings: the gain from orchestration comes from reliable answer selection rather than from additional debate, and the value of a figure aid scales with how much of the problem is locked inside the image. The resulting pipeline improves overall accuracy over a single-agent baseline from 0.643 to 0.802 on the public split, and won 1st place on both the public and the private leaderboard (private overall 0.743).

</details>


### [118] [Class-Balanced Softmax: A Bayes Theory-Based Method for Long-Tailed Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22258)
*Yi-Hang Zhu,Rajeev Raman,Shiqi Su,Jianyuan Sun,Xinyu Yang,Nan Xing,Huiyu Zhou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes Class-Balanced Softmax (CBS), a simple logit adjustment method derived from a Bayesian framework and power-law assumption, to address performance degradation of deep learning classifiers on imbalanced datasets. CBS outperforms Balanced Softmax and other rebalancing methods by mitigating a newly identified "preference issue" where models show higher training error and larger generalization gaps for tail classes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep learning models with traditional softmax classifiers perform poorly on imbalanced datasets. Although Balanced Softmax is a state-of-the-art rebalancing method, it has inherent limitations such as disproportionately lower testing accuracy for tail classes.

Method: The authors propose Class-Balanced Softmax (CBS), a simple logit adjustment method rooted in a theoretical Bayesian framework and a heuristic power-law assumption. They also introduce a novel metric to quantify the "preference issue" — a phenomenon where models exhibit higher training error and larger generalization gap for classes with limited data.

Result: Extensive experiments on large-scale benchmarks demonstrate that CBS is highly scalable and outperforms existing methods including Balanced Softmax. CBS also effectively mitigates the identified preference issue.

Conclusion: CBS is a computationally inexpensive, easily integrable logit adjustment method that addresses limitations of Balanced Softmax by mitigating the preference issue in imbalanced classification, achieving superior performance on large-scale benchmarks.

Abstract: Deep learning models using traditional softmax classifiers have achieved remarkable success in various classification tasks. However, their performance degrades significantly on imbalanced datasets. Although Balanced Softmax is widely adopted as a state-of-the-art rebalancing method, it possesses inherent limitations, such as yielding disproportionately lower testing accuracy for tail classes. To mitigate these shortcomings, we propose the Class-Balanced Softmax (CBS). Rooted in a theoretical Bayesian framework and a heuristic power-law assumption, the CBS is a simple logit adjustment that is computationally inexpensive and easily integrated into existing pipelines. Furthermore, we characterise a fundamental phenomenon in models trained on imbalanced data, termed the preference issue, wherein models exhibit higher training error and a larger generalisation gap for classes with limited data. To quantify this issue, we introduce a novel metric and demonstrate that CBS effectively mitigates the preference issue. Extensive experiments on large-scale benchmarks show that CBS is highly scalable and outperforms existing methods, including Balanced Softmax.

</details>


### [119] [MA-DAR: Manifold-Aligned Dynamic Adaptive Routing for Continual Temporal Knowledge Graph Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21949)
*Xiangjun Shi,Chong Mu,Jinchuan Zhang,Lizong Zhang,Yuefeng He,Shang Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MA-DAR is a lightweight plug-and-play framework that mitigates representation conflicts (norm domination and semantic blurring) during replay-based continual TKG reasoning by aligning representations on a shared manifold and using dynamic adaptive routing with a polarization regularizer for optimal fusion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing replay-based continual learning methods for TKG reasoning focus on what to replay but overlook how replayed representations should be integrated with current ones, leading to two critical conflicts: norm domination and semantic blurring, which degrade continual reasoning performance.

Method: MA-DAR (Manifold-Aligned Dynamic Adaptive Routing) consists of three components: (1) manifold alignment to project replayed and current representations onto a shared space, (2) a dynamic gating mechanism for learning dimension-wise fusion weights, and (3) a polarization regularizer to encourage decisive routing by penalizing ambiguous gating decisions.

Result: Extensive experiments on four public continual TKG benchmarks show MA-DAR consistently improves performance of representative TKG encoders across different replay settings. Ablation studies and visualizations confirm that manifold alignment and dynamic adaptive routing effectively mitigate representation conflicts.

Conclusion: MA-DAR effectively addresses representation conflicts in replay-based continual TKG reasoning through manifold alignment and adaptive routing, offering a lightweight plug-and-play solution that boosts performance across various TKG encoders and replay settings.

Abstract: Continual temporal knowledge graph (TKG) reasoning aims to continuously incorporate newly emerging facts while preserving previously acquired knowledge. Replay-based continual learning has achieved promising performance by revisiting historical representations. However, existing methods primarily focus on what to replay, while largely overlooking how replayed representations should be integrated with current ones. Such direct integration often gives rise to two critical forms of representation conflict: \textit{norm domination} and \textit{semantic blurring}, ultimately degrading continual reasoning performance. To address these challenges, we propose MA-DAR (Manifold-Aligned Dynamic Adaptive Routing), a lightweight plug-and-play framework for replay representation fusion. MA-DAR first aligns replayed and current representations onto a shared manifold to alleviate distribution discrepancies. It then employs a dynamic gating mechanism to learn dimension-wise fusion weights, adaptively determining the contribution of replayed and current representations to the fused representation. Furthermore, a polarization regularizer encourages more decisive routing behaviors by discouraging ambiguous gating decisions, resulting in more stable and effective knowledge integration. Extensive experiments on four public continual TKG benchmarks demonstrate that MA-DAR consistently improves the performance of representative TKG encoders while remaining effective under different replay settings. Comprehensive ablation studies and visualization analyses further verify the effectiveness of manifold alignment and dynamic adaptive routing in mitigating representation conflicts and improving continual reasoning.

</details>


### [120] [Teaching LLMs to Self-Evolve: Cultivating Core Meta-Skills with Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21971)
*Shujin Wu,Cheng Qian,Xiusi Chen,Heng Ji*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MetaEvolve is a framework that develops meta-skills (self-reflection with environment feedback) for test-time scaling via iterative self-evolution. It uses a data synthesis pipeline, evolution-aware RL, and inference-time evolutionary search. In coding, it leverages continuous reward signals from program execution to train models. It outperforms baselines by 10.01% on in-distribution, 24.12% on out-of-distribution coding benchmarks, and 46.9% relative improvement on open-ended algorithm optimization problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional post-training neglects meta-skills like self-reflection with environment feedback that are crucial for effective multi-round refinement in test-time scaling through iterative self-evolution (e.g., AlphaEvolve). The paper aims to bridge this gap by explicitly cultivating such meta-skills.

Method: Three components: (1) a data synthesis pipeline that generates evolution trajectories (current program, fitness score combining correctness & efficiency, history of prior attempts); (2) evolution-aware reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards derived from test case execution; (3) inference-time evolutionary search. Grounded in coding where program execution provides continuous reward signals.

Result: Outperforms strongest baseline by 10.01% absolute on in-distribution coding tasks, 24.12% on out-of-distribution tasks, and 46.9% relative improvement on open-ended algorithm optimization problems entirely outside the training domain.

Conclusion: Explicitly cultivating self-evolution meta-skills offers a principled path toward more capable and autonomously self-evolving AI, with generalizable domain-agnostic skills that transfer broadly to open-ended problems.

Abstract: Test-time scaling through iterative self-evolution with environment feedback, as demonstrated by AlphaEvolve, shows remarkable performance gains. We hypothesize that the success of such evolution frameworks hinges on meta-skills, such as self-reflection with environment feedback, that enable effective multi-round refinement, yet are largely neglected by traditional post-training. To bridge this gap, we present MetaEvolve, a framework designed to develop these meta-skills via a data synthesis pipeline, evolution-aware reinforcement learning (RL), and inference-time evolutionary search. Concretely, we ground MetaEvolve in coding, where program execution provides natural, continuous reward signals beyond binary correctness. Building on these signals, we synthesize evolution trajectories as training data, each containing a current program, its fitness score (combining correctness and efficiency), and a history of prior attempts, and train the model via RL with verifiable rewards derived from test case execution. By training on large-scale code data, we aim to inspire generalizable domain-agnostic meta-skills that can transfer broadly to open-ended problems where such rich training signals are scarce. Across seven coding benchmarks, MetaEvolve outperforms the strongest baseline by 10.01% absolute on in-distribution tasks and 24.12% on out-of-distribution tasks. On open-ended algorithm optimization problems entirely outside the training domain, it further achieves a 46.9% relative improvement. These results demonstrate that explicitly cultivating self-evolution meta-skills offers a principled path toward more capable and autonomously self-evolving AI.

</details>


### [121] [On the Convergence of Stochastic Low-Rank Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21975)
*Ru Wang,Chengchang Liu,John C. S. Lui*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper improves the theoretical analysis of LoRA optimization, showing LoRA-GD requires O(ε⁻⁴) gradient evaluations (improved from exp{O(ε⁻²)}), and proposes two stochastic methods: LoRA-NSGDM (O(ε⁻⁸)) and LoRA-STORM (O(ε⁻⁶) under smoothness).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior analysis of LoRA optimization (LoRA-GD) showed exponential oracle complexity exp{O(ε⁻²)} for finding an ε-stationary point, which is impractically high. This paper aims to sharpen the analysis and extend to the stochastic setting, addressing the need for more practical convergence guarantees for LoRA-based fine-tuning.

Method: The paper first sharpens deterministic LoRA-GD analysis, improving complexity from exponential O(exp{ε⁻²}) to polynomial O(ε⁻⁴). For the stochastic setting, it proposes LoRA-NSGDM (non-convex stochastic gradient method with momentum) achieving O(ε⁻⁸) oracle complexity. With an additional mean-square smoothness condition, it proposes LoRA-STORM, a variance-reduced method that improves complexity to O(ε⁻⁶).

Result: Deterministic LoRA-GD: O(ε⁻⁴) full-gradient evaluations (vs. prior exp{O(ε⁻²)}). Stochastic LoRA-NSGDM: O(ε⁻⁸) stochastic oracle complexity. Stochastic LoRA-STORM (with variance reduction under mean-square smoothness): O(ε⁻⁶) stochastic oracle complexity.

Conclusion: The paper provides significantly tighter convergence guarantees for LoRA optimization, demonstrating polynomial rather than exponential oracle complexity. It also extends theoretical analysis to practical stochastic settings, offering two methods with different efficiency-smoothness trade-offs for fine-tuning large pretrained models.

Abstract: Low-rank adaptation (LoRA) optimizes $J(B,A)=\mathcal L(W_\mathrm{base}+sBA)$ over two adapters $B \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times r}$ and $A \in \mathbb{R}^{r \times n}$ that form a low-rank update to a frozen pretrained weight matrix $W_\mathrm{base} \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$. The prior analysis shows LoRA-GD takes $\exp\{\mathcal{O}(ε^{-2})\}$ oracle calls to find an $ε$-stationary point such that $\|\nabla J(B,A)\|\leq ε$ in the deterministic setting. We sharpen the analysis and show that $\mathcal{O}(ε^{-4})$ full-gradient evaluations suffice for the same first-order criterion. We further study stochastic LoRA under unbiased gradient estimates and finite variance. We propose LoRA-NSGDM, which finds an $ε$-stationary point with $\mathcal{O}(ε^{-8})$ stochastic oracle complexity. Under the additional mean-square smoothness condition, we use variance reduction strategy and propose LoRA-STORM, which improves the stochastic oracle complexity to $\mathcal{O}(ε^{-6})$.

</details>


### [122] [From Perturbation Correction to Geometry-Aware Sampling: Sharpness-Guided Equilibrium Sampling for Balanced Flat Minima in Long-Tailed Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21999)
*Jiaxin Deng,Junbiao Pang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SGS dynamically adjusts sampling probabilities using frequency and sharpness estimates to improve long-tailed learning, achieving significant accuracy gains with minimal overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-tailed learning suffers from two coupled issues: head class dominance in training and sharp minima on tail classes. Existing methods treat data sampling and optimization geometry separately, leaving room for a unified approach.

Method: SGS uses cumulative class counts and EMA sharpness estimates from SAM to dynamically adjust mini-batch sampling probabilities, increasing tail class frequency while suppressing classes with large loss changes. The process is analyzed via SDE and PAC-Bayes.

Result: On CIFAR-100 LT (imbalance 100), SGS-SAM improves Focal-SAM by 10.85 tail accuracy and 3.56 overall. On ImageNet-LT, it improves ImbSAM by 6.59 tail and 1.20 overall. Training time is only 1.02x vanilla SAM.

Conclusion: SGS establishes a sampling-side route to loss-landscape control, suggesting future methods should jointly regulate data exposure and optimization geometry.

Abstract: Long-tailed learning couples two sources of poor generalization: head classes dominate training exposure, while under-represented classes often converge to sharper regions of the loss landscape. Conventional re-sampling addresses the former without considering geometry, whereas existing long-tailed sharpness-aware minimization (SAM) methods modify losses or perturbations only after biased mini-batches have been drawn. We introduce Sharpness-Guided Equilibrium Sampling (SGS), which treats the sampling distribution as an active control variable for optimization geometry. SGS dynamically adjusts subsequent mini-batches by increasing the sampling probability of less frequently sampled classes while suppressing classes with large SAM-induced loss changes, using only cumulative class counts and EMA sharpness estimates obtained from the standard SAM update, without class-wise perturbations or additional backward passes. We characterize this sampling process through a continuous-time stochastic differential equation and a sampling-dependent PAC-Bayes analysis, explaining how frequency-sharpness feedback can move training toward a more balanced flatness profile. On CIFAR-100 LT with an imbalance ratio of 100, SGS-SAM improves Focal-SAM by 10.85 points in tail accuracy and 3.56 points overall. On ImageNet-LT, it improves ImbSAM by 6.59 points on tail classes and 1.20 points overall. Its training time is only $1.02\times$ that of vanilla SAM. Beyond these gains, SGS establishes a sampling-side route to loss-landscape control, suggesting that future long-tailed methods can jointly regulate data exposure and optimization geometry rather than treating either as fixed.

</details>


### [123] [Energy Manifold Natural Gradient Descent: Riemannian Optimization for Neural PDE Solvers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22004)
*Zhangyong Liang,Huanhuan Gao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces EMNGD (Euclideanized Manifold Natural Gradient Descent), a Riemannian manifold optimization framework for neural PDE solvers. It extends energy natural gradient descent to constrained parameter domains, proving theoretical properties including coordinate invariance, convergence guarantees, and exact reduction to ENGD in Euclidean space. Using Woodbury identity and Nyström approximation, it enables scalable sample-space solves. EMNGD achieves higher accuracy and faster convergence on neural PDE benchmarks compared to state-of-the-art baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing energy natural gradient descent (ENGD) formulations assume unconstrained Euclidean parameter domains, limiting their applicability to physics-informed and variational neural PDE solvers where parameters naturally lie on Riemannian manifolds due to constraints.

Method: EMNGD restricts the energy-induced quadratic model to feasible tangent directions and employs retractions to maintain parameter constraints. The Woodbury identity transfers tangent system computations to sample space without altering the direction, and Nyström approximation provides scalable solves with controlled error that recovers exact direction upon iterative convergence.

Result: EMNGD achieves higher accuracy and faster convergence than state-of-the-art baselines on neural PDE benchmarks. The Woodbury identity preserves the EMNGD direction exactly, and scalable-solver diagnostics quantify the accuracy-cost trade-off of preconditioning and residual subsampling.

Conclusion: EMNGD provides a theoretically grounded manifold optimization framework for neural PDE solvers with proven convergence guarantees, coordinate invariance, and practical scalability via Woodbury and Nyström techniques, outperforming existing methods on benchmark tasks.

Abstract: Energy natural gradient descent (ENGD) aligns parameter updates with the curvature of an underlying function-space energy, but existing formulations assume an unconstrained Euclidean parameter domain. We introduce \EMNGDfull{}, a manifold optimization framework for physics-informed and variational neural PDE solvers whose parameters lie on a Riemannian manifold. EMNGD restricts the energy-induced quadratic model to feasible tangent directions and uses retractions to preserve parameter constraints throughout optimization. Under coercivity, we prove that the push-forward of the undamped EMNGD direction is the best feasible approximation to the function-space Newton vector in the energy metric. We establish coordinate invariance, exact reduction to ENGD in Euclidean space, global first-order convergence with Armijo backtracking, and robustness to inexact tangent solves. For quadratic residual energies and generalized Gauss--Newton pullbacks, the Woodbury identity transfers the tangent system to sample space without changing the direction. Nyström approximation provides scalable sample-space solves with controlled direction error and recovers the exact direction after iterative convergence. On the evaluated neural PDE benchmarks, EMNGD achieves higher accuracy and faster convergence than the compared state-of-the-art baselines. Woodbury preserves the EMNGD direction, while scalable-solver diagnostics quantify the accuracy--cost trade-off of preconditioning and residual subsampling.

</details>


### [124] [Cross-Domain Off-Policy Evaluation and Learning for Contextual Bandits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22012)
*Yuta Natsubori,Masataka Ushiku,Yuta Saito*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes Cross-Domain Off-Policy Evaluation and Learning (OPE/L) to handle challenging scenarios (few-shot data, deterministic logging policies, new actions) by leveraging logged data from both target and source domains, developing a new estimator and policy gradient method that significantly improves OPE/L performance in previously unsolved situations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing OPE/L methods suffer from variance issues and limited exploration in challenging but prevalent scenarios such as few-shot data, deterministic logging policies, and new actions, making them ineffective for applications like personalized medicine, recommendations, education, and advertising.

Method: Proposes Cross-Domain OPE/L that utilizes logged datasets from both the target domain (where the new policy will be deployed) and other source domains; develops a new estimator and policy gradient method that jointly leverages target and source datasets to improve evaluation and learning.

Result: The proposed cross-domain approach substantially enhances OPE/L performance in previously unsolved challenging situations (few-shot data, deterministic logging policies, new actions) as demonstrated in empirical evaluations.

Conclusion: Cross-Domain OPE/L effectively addresses key limitations of existing methods by transferring knowledge across domains, enabling reliable policy evaluation and optimization in scenarios that were previously intractable.

Abstract: Off-Policy Evaluation and Learning (OPE/L) in contextual bandits is rapidly gaining popularity in real systems because new policies can be evaluated and learned securely using only historical logged data. However, existing methods in OPE/L cannot handle many challenging but prevalent scenarios such as few-shot data, deterministic logging policies, and new actions. In many applications, such as personalized medicine, content recommendations, education, and advertising, we need to evaluate and learn new policies in the presence of these challenges. Existing methods cannot evaluate and optimize effectively in these situations due to the notorious variance issue or limited exploration in the logged data. To enable OPE/L even under these unsolved challenges, we propose a new problem setup of Cross-Domain OPE/L, where we have access not only to the logged data from the target domain in which the new policy will be implemented but also to logged datasets collected from other domains. This novel formulation is widely applicable because we can often use historical data not only from the target hospital, country, device, or user segment but also from other hospitals, countries, devices, or segments. We develop a new estimator and policy gradient method to solve OPE/L by leveraging both target and source datasets, resulting in substantially enhanced OPE/L in the previously unsolved situations in our empirical evaluations.

</details>


### [125] [DCS: A Unified Conditional Sensitivity Framework for Cross-Modal Copyright Infringement Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22035)
*Xiafeng Man*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a post-hoc detection framework (DCS) for identifying copyright infringement in foundation models by treating infringement evidence as a counterfactual conditional distribution shift, using conditional differential privacy and influence-function analysis to measure observable sensitivity between learning and unlearning branches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current foundation models often reproduce copyrighted content, but output similarity alone is insufficient for infringement detection since similar outputs can arise from public-domain concepts or statistical generalization. A principled, unified framework is needed.

Method: The paper formalizes infringement detection as a counterfactual conditional distribution shift via conditional differential privacy. It introduces Dual-Branch Conditional Sensitivity (DCS), which creates learning and unlearning branches around the deployed model, uses influence-function analysis to connect displacement to counterfactual retraining, and bounds sensitivity by privacy-budget surrogate, local curvature, training-set scale, and perturbation step size. A calibrated statistic subtracts sensitivity under orthogonal conditions to distinguish target-specific memorization from generic instability.

Result: The DCS framework is instantiated for ridge-regularized linear regression, conditional diffusion models, autoregressive language models, and multimodal models, showing how the principle is evaluated through prediction gaps, image-embedding divergence, token-distribution or entropy shifts, and cross-modal representation changes.

Conclusion: The proposed DCS framework provides a unified, theoretically grounded post-hoc detection approach for copyright infringement across diverse model architectures, bridging counterfactual reasoning with observable sensitivity measures.

Abstract: Currently, most foundation models can reproduce or strongly depend on copyrighted training content, but output similarity alone is insufficient for infringement detection, because similar outputs may also arise from public-domain concepts, common stylistic conventions, or ordinary statistical generalization.
  In this paper, we develops a unified post-hoc detection framework that treats copyright infringement evidence as a counterfactual conditional distribution shift: a protected target is suspicious when the model's behavior under aligned conditions would change measurably if that target were included in, or removed from, the training process.
  We formalize this view through conditional differential privacy and introduce Dual-Branch Conditional Sensitivity (DCS), an operational statistic that measures the observable gap between two locally perturbed model states. Specifically, the proposed DCS framework creates a learning branch and an unlearning branch around the deployed model, connects their displacement to the unavailable counterfactual retraining effect through influence-function analysis, and bounds the observable sensitivity by the counterfactual privacy-budget surrogate, local curvature, training-set scale, and perturbation step size. To distinguish target-specific memorization from generic fine-tuning instability, we further define a calibrated detection statistic that subtracts the sensitivity measured under orthogonal conditions.
  The DCS framework is instantiated for ridge-regularized linear regression, conditional diffusion models, autoregressive language models, and multimodal models. These instantiations show how the same principle can be evaluated through prediction gaps, image-embedding divergence, token-distribution or entropy shifts, and cross-modal representation changes.

</details>


### [126] [CEL: Comprehensive Counterfactual Explanations Library and Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22045)
*Oleksii Furman,Łukasz Lenkiewicz,Marcel Musiałek,Maciej Zięba*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CEL is a unified library and benchmark for counterfactual explanations in XAI, providing 18 datasets, 14 methods, and standardized evaluation across multiple metrics to enable fair, reproducible comparison.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fair and systematic evaluation of counterfactual explanation methods is hindered by inconsistent data splits, predictive models, and evaluation metrics across studies, making objective comparison difficult.

Method: The paper introduces CEL (Counterfactual Explanations Library), containing 18 datasets of varying size/complexity and implementations/reimplementations of 14 counterfactual methods, with a standardized evaluation protocol using metrics for validity, coverage, sparsity, proximity, and distributional plausibility (including density- and outlier-based measures).

Result: Comprehensive quantitative comparison of multiple counterfactual methods across diverse datasets using complementary metrics; the first comprehensive benchmark systematically evaluating recent methods in a unified, reproducible framework.

Conclusion: The benchmark improves reproducibility, enables fair comparison, and provides a workbench for developing future counterfactual explanation methods, addressing limitations of prior outdated or limited-scope efforts.

Abstract: Counterfactual explanations are a prominent approach in explainable artificial intelligence (xAI), providing actionable guidance on what input changes would alter a model's prediction to a desired outcome. While early methods primarily focused on minimal feature changes, recent work incorporates additional properties such as sparsity, actionability and plausibility. Despite this progress, fair and systematic evaluation remains challenging. Existing studies often rely on different data splits, predictive models, and evaluation metrics, which limits objective comparison across methods. To fill this gap, we introduce CEL (Counterfactual Explanations Library), a unified library and benchmark for counterfactual explanations designed to support consistent implementation and evaluation. CEL includes 18 datasets of varying size and complexity and provides implementations or reimplementations of 14 widely used counterfactual methods. Using this standardized setup, we conduct a comprehensive quantitative comparison across a variety of methods on datasets that differ in size, number, and types of attributes. The evaluation protocol incorporates multiple complementary metrics capturing validity, coverage, sparsity, proximity, and distributional plausibility, including density- and outlier-based measures to assess the realism of generated counterfactuals. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first comprehensive benchmark that systematically evaluates recent counterfactual explanation methods within a unified and reproducible framework. While prior libraries and benchmarking efforts exist in the literature, many are outdated, limited in scope, or lack consistent evaluation protocols. The proposed benchmark aims to improve reproducibility, enable fair comparison, and establish a workbench for the development of future counterfactual explanation methods.

</details>


### [127] [A Leakage-Free Stacked Ensemble Method for Multiclass Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22081)
*S. P. Sharmila,Aruna Tiwari*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LFS-FRAME is a leakage-free stacked ensemble framework combining Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KAN) for functional learning and XGBoost for rule-based learning, using out-of-fold stacking to prevent data leakage, achieving 89.85% accuracy on major families and 81.74% on sub-families for multiclass classification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address limitations of both rule-based classifiers (XGBoost) which struggle with smooth functional relationships, and neural networks which suffer from overfitting and generalization issues, by integrating complementary strengths for robust multiclass classification.

Method: Proposes LFS-FRAME: a leakage-free stacked ensemble framework that combines Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KAN) for functional learning with XGBoost for rule-based learning, using strict out-of-fold stacking to generate unbiased meta-features with complete train-validation isolation.

Result: Achieves 89.85% accuracy in identifying major families and 81.74% accuracy in identifying sub-families, outperforming strong single-model baselines on multi-class datasets.
