### [152] [Beyond Negative-Ridge Endpoints: Mixed-Sign Spectral Regularization via Negative-Shifted Gradient Descent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22474)
*Peng Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: In overparameterized linear regression, early-stopped negative-shifted gradient descent overcomes limitations of negative ridge by using mixed-sign filters and a Marchenko-Pastur barrier, achieving polynomial improvements in risk under a general high-effective-rank tail condition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Negative ridge in overparameterized linear regression is structurally limited: its pole must stay below the smallest nonzero empirical eigenvalue, and it anti-shrinks smaller eigenvalues more than larger ones. The goal is to escape this constraint via early-stopped negative-shifted gradient descent.

Method: The paper introduces early-stopped negative-shifted gradient descent with a filter that is smooth at the would-be pole and mixed-sign-capable. It analyzes a Gaussian spike-plus-flat model to discover a Marchenko-Pastur barrier. Localized Duhamel integrals control the noncontractive shifted dynamics. A finite-grid hold-out inequality transfers separations to the validation-selected algorithm.

Result: The shift that cancels the implicit penalty lies a bulk width above the smallest empirical eigenvalue. The stopped path improves on every admissible endpoint by a polynomial factor in risk. Under a general high-effective-rank tail, the trace sets the implicit floor, the squared spectrum controls exposure, and the floor-critical path recovers all head scales at once.

Conclusion: Early-stopped negative-shifted gradient descent achieves better risk performance than admissible endpoints, with the Marchenko-Pastur barrier serving as a key structural insight. The method works beyond positive shrinkage and, once scales separate, every uniform rescaling of ridgeless.

Abstract: In overparameterized linear regression, many weak spectral directions act like a ridge penalty on the signal-bearing spectrum; negative ridge is the natural correction, pushing filters above one. The stable negative-ridge endpoint, however, is structurally limited: its pole must stay below the smallest nonzero empirical eigenvalue, and it anti-shrinks smaller eigenvalues more than larger ones. Early-stopped negative-shifted gradient descent escapes this constraint. Its filter is smooth at the would-be pole and mixed-sign-capable: above-ridgeless directions form a leading prefix, with lower directions shrunk or exposure-controlled while stopping sets the crossover. In a Gaussian spike-plus-flat model we discover a Marchenko-Pastur barrier: the shift that cancels the implicit penalty lies a bulk width above the smallest empirical eigenvalue, and the stopped path improves on every admissible endpoint by a polynomial factor in risk under explicit conditions. Our main theorem permits a general high-effective-rank tail: its trace sets the implicit floor, its squared spectrum controls exposure, and the floor-critical path recovers all head scales at once, beyond positive shrinkage and, once scales separate, every uniform rescaling of ridgeless. Handling the noncontractive shifted dynamics is the central technical challenge; localized Duhamel integrals control them. A finite-grid hold-out inequality transfers the separations to the validation-selected algorithm.

</details>


### [153] [Susceptible Reservoir Architectures for Regime-Conditional Volatility Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22491)
*Aliaksei Kaliutau*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces Susceptible Architectures (SUSA), a reservoir-design principle for volatility forecasting, with implementations using complex-valued reservoirs and regime-conditioned experts. Models are evaluated on 16 U.S. equity/ETF series and perform competitively with GARCH, with a stacked ensemble improving mean QLIKE by 0.0116 over the strongest constituent and winning in 75% of test scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Volatility forecasting is dominated by persistence and measurement noise, leaving limited residual structure for nonlinear models to exploit. The authors aim to design reservoir architectures that can capture this residual structure more effectively.

Method: SUSA implements two reservoir designs: complex-valued open-chain and periodic reservoirs, with regime-conditioned experts for four volatility states (calm, onset, recovery, persistent-stress). Open-system q-qubit counterparts are implemented in Qiskit. An AR-Ridge anchor with bounded residual correction trained under QLIKE is used. Evaluation uses 16 U.S. equity/ETF series with three disjoint chronological folds, 12-observation input window, and 5-observation forecast horizon.

Result: Models perform competitively with GARCH, achieving statistically significant QLIKE improvements for IWM and XLP. Forecasts complement HARQ-style predictions: a stacked ensemble improves mean QLIKE by 0.0116 over its strongest constituent and wins in 75% of test scenarios.

Conclusion: SUSA reservoirs offer a viable nonlinear approach for volatility forecasting, complementing established linear models like HARQ, with ensemble methods yielding additional gains.

Abstract: Volatility forecasting is dominated by persistence and measurement noise, leaving limited residual structure for nonlinear models to exploit. We introduce Susceptible Architectures (SUSA), a reservoir-design principle for volatility forecasting, and its two concrete implementations, based on complex-valued open-chain and periodic reservoirs and regime-conditioned experts to interpret reservoir features across calm, onset, recovery, and persistent-stress states. We also implement open-system $q$-qubit counterparts in Qiskit while retaining a common AR-Ridge anchor and a bounded residual correction trained under QLIKE. We evaluate models on 16 U.S. equity and exchange-traded-fund series using three disjoint chronological training, validation, and test folds, a 12-observation input window, and a five-observation forecast horizon. The proposed models perform competitively with GARCH, achieving statistically significant QLIKE improvements for specific assets (IWM, XLP). Also models' forecasts complement HARQ-style predictions: a stacked ensemble improves mean QLIKE by 0.0116 over its strongest constituent and wins in 75% of test scenarios.

</details>


### [154] [Interpretable EEG biomarkers with bag-of-waves: Spatial and temporal waveform dictionaries for low-data regimes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22508)
*Athanasios Papastathopoulos-Katsaros,Steven T. Lee,Lin Yao,Ajay Thomas,Junseok Park,Matthew J. McGinley,Zhandong Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Bag-of-waves is an interpretable EEG analysis framework that learns a dictionary of waveform templates (atoms) via shift-invariant k-means, converts EEG into atom token sequences, and uses token counts and n-gram transitions for classification/clustering. It achieves competitive performance with deep/foundation models on three datasets (mouse genotype clustering, dementia classification, TUEV benchmark) while using far fewer parameters and offering full interpretability, especially excelling in low-data regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing EEG analysis methods suffer from a trade-off: predefined spectral features impose strong bias by fixing what counts as informative, while deep neural networks and foundation models are hard to interpret and require large amounts of data and compute. The authors aim to develop an interpretable, data-efficient alternative that does not rely on fixed feature engineering or heavy black-box models.

Method: The method, bag-of-waves, learns a small dictionary of recurring EEG waveform templates (atoms) using shift-invariant k-means without labels. Continuous EEG is converted into a sequence of atom tokens, and token counts feed a downstream classifier. The representation is extended via: (1) atom-to-atom transitions (n-grams) to capture temporal structure, and (2) regional and cross-channel spatial atoms for multichannel EEG.

Result: On three complementary datasets (single-channel mouse genotype clustering with 16 animals, resting-state dementia classification, and TUEV six-way clinical EEG classification), bag-of-waves achieves performance competitive with state-of-the-art deep and foundation models, while operating with a fraction of the parameter count and providing full interpretability. The method explicitly recovers known clinical morphologies that neurophysiologists can validate.

Conclusion: Bag-of-waves offers a practical interpretable alternative for EEG analysis that matches deep model performance with far fewer parameters. Its main advantage is effectiveness in low-data regimes where heavier models are unsuitable, and its interpretability allows direct validation of learned waveform patterns by domain experts.

Abstract: Electroencephalography (EEG) is widely used to diagnose neurological conditions, but its analysis usually relies on either predefined spectral features or deep neural networks. Predefined features carry a strong bias, since they fix in advance what counts as informative, while deep neural networks and foundation models are hard to interpret and need large amounts of data and compute. We present bag-of-waves, an interpretable framework that learns a small dictionary of recurring EEG waveform templates, called atoms, using shift-invariant k-means without labels. The continuous EEG is then turned into a sequence of atom tokens, whose counts feed a simple downstream classifier or clustering step. We extend this representation in two ways: we add atom-to-atom transitions, which we call n- grams, to capture temporal structure, and we move from single-channel atoms to regional and cross-channel spatial atoms for the multichannel case. We test the method on three complementary datasets, each probing a different aspect: single-channel mouse genotype clustering with only sixteen animals (the low-data and temporal case), resting-state dementia classification (the spatial case), and the TUEV benchmark, a six-way classification of clinical EEG events (a high-data comparison against strong deep and foundation baselines). Across all three datasets, bag-of-waves achieves performance competitive with state-of-the-art deep and foundation models. Yet, it operates with a fraction of the parameter count and provides full interpretability: because every atom corresponds to an inspectable waveform, the method explicitly recovers known clinical morphologies that a neurophysiologist can directly validate. Its main advantage is that it works in the low-data regime where heavier models are a poor fit.

</details>


### [155] [Dysphagia Risk Stratification in Head and Neck Cancer via Two-Stage PRO-Clinical Stacking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22514)
*Siyuan Zhao,Eric Ababio Anyimadu,Zachary G. Brumm,Yue Ma,Clifton David Fuller,Xinhua Zhang,G. Elisabeta Marai,Guadalupe Canahuate*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This study develops a clinically interpretable two-stage stacking model that integrates patient-reported outcomes (PROs) with structured clinical variables to predict swallowing impairment risk in head and neck cancer survivors, eliminating the need for specialized videofluoroscopic imaging.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dysphagia is a debilitating late effect of HNC treatment, but timely identification of at-risk patients is challenging. Videofluoroscopic imaging (CTCAE-DIGEST) is definitive but requires specialized equipment, trained personnel, and high patient burden. Patient-reported outcomes (PROs) are low-cost and scalable, yet a clear clinical framework for translating PRO responses into actionable interventions is lacking.

Method: A single-visit PRO-clinical prediction framework was formulated using a clinically interpretable two-stage stacking model that predicts swallowing impairment risk from PRO responses (MDADI items) and structured clinical variables, without requiring videofluoroscopic imaging.

Result: Individual MDADI responses contain predictive information beyond that captured by composite or global summary scores. Interpretability analyses reveal specific symptom patterns and clinical risk factors associated with swallowing impairment.

Conclusion: Structured PRO-clinical integration supports a practical, imaging-free approach for dysphagia risk stratification in HNC survivorship care, providing a clinically actionable framework for identifying patients who may warrant further evaluation.

Abstract: Dysphagia is a debilitating late effect of head and neck cancer (HNC) treatment, yet timely identification of at-risk patients remains challenging in survivorship care. Definitive assessment relies on videofluoroscopic imaging, as captured by the Dynamic Imaging Grade of Swallowing Toxicity (CTCAE-DIGEST), which, while validated, requires specialized equipment, trained personnel, and significant patient burden, limiting its routine use in surveillance. Patient-reported outcomes (PROs), by contrast, are low-cost, scalable, and easily collected at any clinical encounter, making them an attractive alternative signal for identifying patients who may warrant further evaluation. However, a clear clinical framework for translating PRO responses into actionable interventions is still evolving. In particular, uncertainty remains regarding when a patient's self-reported symptom burden should prompt escalation of care.
  This study addresses this gap by formulating a single-visit PRO-clinical prediction framework and introducing a clinically interpretable two-stage stacking model to predict swallowing impairment risk using PRO responses and structured clinical variables, without requiring videofluoroscopic imaging. The proposed framework quantifies the independent contributions of patient-reported symptoms and clinical factors within a unified and interpretable risk assessment model. Our findings demonstrate that individual MDADI responses contain predictive information beyond that captured by composite or global summary scores, while interpretability analyses reveal symptom patterns and clinical risk factors associated with swallowing impairment. Together, these results support the use of structured PRO-clinical integration as a practical, imaging-free approach for dysphagia risk stratification in HNC survivorship.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [156] [Risk Is Not the Target: A Monotonic Framework for Evaluating Wildfire Operational Risk Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21597)
*Nicolas Caron,Christophe Guyeux,Maxime Coulmeau,Benjamin Aynes*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a monotonic evaluation framework for wildfire risk systems, replacing standard metrics like F1-score with measures of whether increasing risk scores consistently correspond to increasing operational load. Comparing DFE, GRU models, and FARS on French Alpes-Maritimes, they find that expert-based DFE, despite poor classification metrics, exhibits the best monotonic behavior, arguing that a good risk model should explain operational dynamics rather than predict fires accurately.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard ML metrics (F1-score, IoU) assess event prediction accuracy but fail to evaluate whether a continuous risk signal operationally coheres with real-world load (fire counts, intervention time, resources). This mismatch motivates a new evaluation paradigm focused on ordinal coherence.

Method: Proposes a monotonic evaluation framework that measures whether increases in predicted risk scores consistently correspond to increases in observed operational load. Compares three approaches on the French Alpes-Maritimes: expert-based DFE index, GRU-based predictive models, and FARS (hybrid multi-agent system combining predictive AI with LLM-based reasoning).

Result: DFE (expert-based) exhibits the most balanced monotonic behavior across the full risk scale despite poor classification metrics. GRU models achieve strong local monotonicity but produce poorly distributed risk levels. FARS inherits structural limitations from upstream signals without correcting them.

Conclusion: Central paradigm shift: a good risk model does not predict fires accurately but provides an ordinal scale that meaningfully explains operational dynamics.

Abstract: Evaluating wildfire risk systems using standard machine-learning metrics such as F1-score or IoU is fundamentally flawed: these metrics assess event prediction accuracy, not the operational coherence of a continuous risk signal. This work proposes a novel monotonic evaluation framework that measures whether increases in a predicted risk score consistently correspond to increases in observed operational load, such as number of fires, intervention time, and deployed resources. Moreover, we compare three structurally different approaches on the French Alpes-Maritimes department: the expert-based DFE index, GRU- based predictive models, and FARS, a hybrid multi-agent system combining predictive AI with LLM-based reasoning. Experimental results reveal that the DFE, despite poor classification metrics, exhibits the most balanced monotonic behavior across the full risk scale. GRU models achieve strong local monotonicity but fail to produce well-distributed risk levels. FARS inherits and reveals the structural limitations of upstream signals rather than correcting them. The central finding is a paradigm shift: a good risk model does not predict fires accurately, but one whose ordinal scale meaningfully explains operational dynamics, as proved in this paper. Code of the monotonic framework is available on github.

</details>


### [157] [From Frame-Level Recognition to Event-Level Confirmation: Repair Traces and Runtime Failure Analysis of Public-Space Gesture Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21601)
*M. Meng,Yansong Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper analyzes 8 repair records from a scenic-area interactive kiosk project, extracting 20 failure instances across 4 gesture tasks, and proposes an event-level runtime abstraction to bridge the gap between frame-level recognition and stable interaction events.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current gesture interaction evaluation focuses on frame-level recognition accuracy, but deployed systems fail differently—users experience whether intended actions become stable interaction events, not whether individual frames are correctly classified. This gap between recognition and interaction is underexplored.

Method: Analyzed 8 engineering repair records from a scenic-area interactive kiosk project covering 4 gesture tasks. Extracted 20 failure instances and organized them into six non-exclusive failure classes. Proposed an event-level runtime abstraction as a layer between hand-landmark models and interaction tasks.

Result: Six failure classes identified: model-output degeneration, temporal mismatch, geometric-scale instability, coordinate-rendering mismatch, runtime lifecycle failure, and feedback synchronization and recovery failure. An event-confirmation runtime abstraction is proposed to address these failures.

Conclusion: The contribution is a deployment-grounded failure taxonomy, an event-confirmation runtime abstraction, and case-study findings. The work does not claim a new recognition model, large-scale evaluation, or quantified accuracy gains, but focuses on the recognition-to-interaction gap in practical systems.

Abstract: Public-space gesture interaction is often evaluated as a frame-level recognition problem, but deployed systems expose a different failure boundary. In scenic kiosks, exhibition halls, and service terminals, users experience whether an intended action becomes a stable interaction event, not whether individual hand-landmark frames are correct. We call this the recognition-to-interaction gap.
  This paper analyzes 8 engineering repair records from a scenic-area interactive kiosk project, covering 4 gesture tasks: two-hand bowing, single-hand fist shaking, two-hand catching control, and knowledge-graph node hovering. From these traces, we extract 20 failure instances and organize them into six non-exclusive working failure classes: model-output degeneration, temporal mismatch, geometric-scale instability, coordinate-rendering mismatch, runtime lifecycle failure, and feedback synchronization and recovery failure.
  We further organize recurring repair mechanisms into an event-level runtime abstraction between the hand-landmark model and the interaction task. The contribution is deliberately bounded: a deployment-grounded failure taxonomy, an event-confirmation runtime abstraction, and case-study findings. We do not claim a new recognition model, large-scale user evaluation, or quantified accuracy gains.

</details>


### [158] [Transferable Latency Prediction for Fast LLM Screening on Heterogeneous Edge Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21602)
*Xiaolong Tu,Vinod K. Mishra,Venkat R. Dasari,Anu G. Bourgeois,Haoxin Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A runtime-aware latency prediction framework for LLM deployment on heterogeneous edge devices, using gated fusion of static descriptors and dynamic hardware telemetry, validated across multiple mobile and edge platforms with improved R-squared scores and cross-device transfer via lightweight calibration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate latency prediction is critical for deploying LLMs on heterogeneous edge devices where inference latency is affected by model architecture, prompt behavior, runtime backend, hardware utilization, DVFS, and thermal variation; existing static-only approaches fail to capture runtime dynamics.

Method: Represents each inference request as a hardware-runtime-model-prompt configuration; separates inference into prefill and decode phases; adaptively fuses static descriptors with dynamic hardware telemetry via a gated prediction model; supports cross-device transfer through lightweight calibration.

Result: On Pixel 8: total-latency R² from 0.953→0.960, decode-latency R² from 0.957→0.973. On Pixel 8 Pro: prefill-latency R² from -1.383→0.966. Cross-device transfer (Pixel 8 Pro→8): total-latency R² from -0.974→0.940, decode-latency R² from -1.085→0.927. Heterogeneous profiling shows high device/runtime dependence (SmolLM2: 8.42 tokens/s on Orange Pi 5 Pro vs 64.38 tokens/s on RTX 3090-class GPU).

Conclusion: Runtime-aware prediction with lightweight calibration reduces profiling cost and supports latency-aware LLM deployment across heterogeneous edge platforms.

Abstract: Accurate latency prediction is critical for deploying large language models (LLMs) on heterogeneous edge devices, where inference latency is affected by model architecture, prompt behavior, runtime backend, hardware utilization, dynamic voltage and frequency scaling (DVFS), and thermal variation. This paper presents a runtime-aware latency prediction framework for deployment-oriented LLM selection. The framework represents each inference request as a hardware-runtime-model-prompt configuration, separates inference into prefill and decode phases, and adaptively fuses static descriptors with dynamic hardware telemetry through a gated prediction model.
  We evaluate the framework using Pixel mobile devices and validate the profiling pipeline on Jetson Nano, Orange Pi 5 Pro, and an RTX 3090-class GPU platform. On Pixel 8, the full predictor improves total-latency R-squared from 0.953 to 0.960 and decode-latency R-squared from 0.957 to 0.973 over a static-only baseline. On Pixel 8 Pro, it improves prefill-latency R-squared from -1.383 to 0.966. For cross-device transfer, calibration improves Pixel 8 Pro to Pixel 8 total-latency R-squared from -0.974 to 0.940 and decode-latency R-squared from -1.085 to 0.927. Heterogeneous profiling further shows that latency is highly device- and runtime-dependent: the same SmolLM2 model family reaches 8.42 tokens/s on Orange Pi 5 Pro but 64.38 tokens/s on an RTX 3090-class GPU. These results demonstrate that runtime-aware prediction with lightweight calibration can reduce profiling cost and support latency-aware LLM deployment across heterogeneous edge platforms.

</details>


### [159] [AgentKVShift: Efficient KV Cache Reuse for Agentic Memory Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21604)
*Nilesh Prasad Pandey,Jason Kong,Lanxiang Hu,Quanling Zhao,Yujie Zhao,Onat Gungor,Hao Zhang,Tajana Rosing*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentKVShift is a training-free, probe-guided KV residual correction method for memory-augmented LLM agents that reduces prefill latency by correcting KV reuse errors via a shared memory-level offset, achieving near full recompute performance with only 10-30% cache refresh (2-3.5x speedup) and outperforming prior reuse methods on structured agentic memories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Memory-augmented LLM agents incur high inference costs because every retrieval triggers full re-encoding of structured memory units (with LLM-generated metadata) into KV states, dominating prefill latency. Existing training-free KV reuse methods were designed for RAG-style raw passages and degrade on structured agentic memories.

Method: AgentKVShift is a training-free, probe-guided KV residual correction method. It decomposes per-memory KV reuse residual into a shared memory-level offset plus small token-wise fluctuations. A small probe set estimates this offset, enabling a single weighted correction for all reused tokens. Unlike prior methods that only decide which tokens to recompute, AgentKVShift also corrects non-recomputed tokens, turning the refresh budget into useful signal across the entire chunk.

Result: Across four open-source LLMs (3B-32B) on two long-horizon agentic memory benchmarks (long-term dialogue and agentic applications), AgentKVShift achieves near full recompute performance while refreshing only 10-30% of the cache, outperforming baselines at the same recompute ratio. It requires up to 5x lower recompute to reach near-full performance (vs. 45-55% for prior methods). Delivers 2-3.5x prefill speedups over no-KV-reuse on A100. Orthogonally composes with KV cache quantization, retaining over 2x F1 under aggressive 2- and 4-bit settings.

Conclusion: AgentKVShift effectively addresses the KV reuse degradation on structured agentic memories by introducing probe-guided residual correction, achieving substantial prefill speedups with minimal recompute budget while maintaining near-full recompute quality and composing well with KV quantization.

Abstract: Memory-augmented LLM agents maintain context across hundreds of interactions through agentic memory systems that actively curate retrieved content with LLM-generated metadata such as summaries, keywords, and tags. From an inference cost standpoint, every retrieval triggers a full re-encoding of these structured memory units into Key-Value (KV) states, which dominates prefill latency. Existing training-free KV reuse methods mitigate this by selectively recomputing a small fraction of tokens, but were designed for RAG-style raw passages and degrade on structured agentic memories. We present AgentKVShift, a training-free, probe-guided KV residual correction method that operates per retrieved memory unit. One of the crucial insights we demonstrate is that the per-memory KV reuse residual decomposes into a shared memory-level offset plus small token-wise fluctuations. Estimating this offset from a small probe set allows us to correct every reused token by a single weighted correction. Unlike prior reuse methods which decide which tokens to recompute and leave the rest of the cache stale, AgentKVShift also corrects the tokens it does not recompute, turning the refresh budget into useful signal across the entire chunk. Across four open-source LLMs spanning 3B to 32B parameters and two long-horizon agentic memory benchmarks (long-term dialogue and agentic applications), AgentKVShift achieves near full recompute performance while refreshing only 10-30% of the cache, outperforming baselines at the same recompute ratio. It requires up to 5x lower recompute to reach this near-full performance, which prior reuse methods only attain at 45-55% refresh. In this regime, AgentKVShift delivers prefill speedups of 2-3.5x over no-KV-reuse on a single A100. AgentKVShift orthogonally composes with KV cache quantization, retaining over 2x the F1 of prior reuse methods under aggressive 2- and 4-bit settings.

</details>


### [160] [TILT: Improving Compositional Generation in Diffusion Models with a Model-Intrinsic Reward](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21606)
*Debottam Dutta,Jaehoon Hahm,Jianchong Chen,Romit Roy Choudhury*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TILT is a training-free framework that improves compositional text-to-image generation by interpreting failures as overlap modes between joint/single-concept distributions and using a model-intrinsic reward to guide diffusion sampling via tilted distributions, achieving better compositional alignment without external supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Powerful text-to-image models still struggle with complex compositional prompts; existing test-time methods require external supervision or reward models.

Method: Frames compositional failures as overlap modes between joint and single-concept distributions; defines an intrinsic reward favoring joint concept presence; derives a KL-constrained objective with closed-form tilted target distribution and principled guiding steps for diffusion sampling; proposes hybrid guidance strategy balancing two different approaches.

Result: Improves compositional alignment while preserving image quality on T2ICompBench compared to previous baselines.

Conclusion: Training-free test-time reward alignment via tilted distributions effectively enhances compositional faithfulness without external supervision.

Abstract: Recent advances in powerful text-to-image generation models have made it increasingly important to develop test-time methods that modify the sampling trajectory to produce images more faithful to complex compositional prompts. We present TILT, a training-free framework for compositional text-to-image generation via test-time reward alignment. We interpret compositional failures as overlap modes between joint and single-concept distributions, and define a reward that favors samples where all concepts are jointly present. This reward is intrinsic to the base model and does not require any external supervision or reward models. This yields a KL-constrained objective with a closed-form tilted target distribution and principled guiding steps for diffusion sampling. The interaction of concept distributions together with the above reward naturally leads to two different guidance strategies while a hybrid approach that balances their respective benefits produces stronger performance. Experiments on prompts from T2ICompBench show that our method improves compositional alignment while preserving image quality compared to previous baselines.

</details>


### [161] [Spectral Flow Certificates for Depth-Aware Long-Range Propagation in Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21607)
*Ranjan Veerabhadraswamy,Ajith Jubilson Emerson*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Spectral Flow Certificates (SFCs) are single scalars computed from graph Laplacians in seconds, requiring no training or labels, that predict whether GNNs can solve long-range tasks on a given graph by fusing algebraic connectivity with message-passing depth.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is currently no inexpensive way to know, before training begins, whether a graph's structure will allow information to travel far enough between distant nodes for GNNs to solve long-range tasks.

Method: Proposes Spectral Flow Certificates (SFCs), single scalars computed from graphs' normalized Laplacians that fuse algebraic connectivity with the chosen message-passing depth into one number measuring how much of the spectral bottleneck can be traversed within the depth budget.

Result: SFCs explain more than twice as much variance in trained GNN long-range accuracy compared to classical structural statistics; achieve explanatory power above 90% at all tested depths across 25 synthetic graph families; predictive relationships hold on 150 real molecular graph topologies from three benchmark datasets.

Conclusion: A single eigenvalue computation is sufficient to flag topology-limited graphs before committing to expensive training pipelines, providing a principled first filter for GNN deployments.

Abstract: Graph Neural Networks propagate information through local message passing, but the graph topologies themselves can silently prevent any amount of training from solving long-range tasks. When we deploy GNNs on new graphs, there is currently no inexpensive way to know, before training begins, whether the graphs' structures will allow information to travel far enough between distant nodes. We address this gap by proposing Spectral Flow Certificates (SFCs), single scalars computed from the graphs' normalised Laplacians in seconds, requiring no model training and no labelled data. An SFC fuses a graph's algebraic connectivity with the chosen message-passing depth into one number that measures how much of the critical spectral bottleneck can be traversed within the available depth budget. Unlike raw spectral gaps, which are static and depth-agnostic, SFCs adapt as the number of layers increases and therefore carry strictly more diagnostic information when depths vary. Compared with classical structural statistics such as average effective resistance and graph diameter, SFCs explain more than twice as much variance in trained GNN long-range accuracy. Across twenty-five synthetic graph families spanning paths, cycles, grids, regular graphs, and random graphs, SFCs predict trained accuracy before any gradients are computed, achieving explanatory power above ninety percent at all tested depths. The same predictive relationships hold on one hundred fifty real molecular graph topologies drawn from three independent benchmark datasets, confirming that the findings are not artefacts of their synthetic construction. Taken together, these results show that a single eigenvalue computation is sufficient to flag topology-limited graphs before committing to expensive training pipelines, providing a principled first filter for GNN deployments.

</details>


### [162] [Coupled Hierarchical Search over Topology and Execution for Agentic Workflow Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21609)
*Dong Li,Yanchi Liu,Xujiang Zhao,Wei Cheng,Zhengzhang Chen,Xintao Wu,Zhong Chen,Chen Zhao,Haifeng Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HierFlow is a training-free, test-time hierarchical search framework that automates LLM workflow design by combining feedback-guided topology adjustments with MCTS-inspired tree search, achieving superior performance across QA, math, and code benchmarks without additional training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automating structured workflow creation for LLMs is hindered by a vast combinatorial search space and often requires inflexible, resource-heavy offline training; the paper aims to address this bottleneck.

Method: HierFlow adopts a topology-and-execution intertwined search paradigm: a hierarchical search architecture with feedback-guided topology adjustments and MCTS-inspired sub-workflow optimization, plus an intelligent gating module that selectively triggers execution-level searches based on contextual necessity.

Result: Consistently outperforms strong baselines on question answering, mathematical reasoning, and code generation benchmarks, delivering high-quality results and computational efficiency without additional training.

Conclusion: HierFlow demonstrates that training-free, test-time hierarchical search can effectively automate agentic workflow design, achieving an optimal balance of quality and efficiency while avoiding offline training overhead.

Abstract: Although structured workflows empower Large Language Models (LLMs) to tackle complex problems, automating their creation is severely hindered by a vast combinatorial search space, frequently resulting in inflexible and resource-heavy offline training dependencies. To address this, we conceptualize workflow generation as an intertwined topology-and-execution search paradigm, where the broader topological layer dictates subtask boundaries and lower-level execution outcomes actively reshape the topology itself. Building on this foundation, we introduce HierFlow, a training-free, test-time hierarchical search architecture that automates agentic workflow design by merging feedback-guided topology adjustments with a fast, MCTS-inspired tree search for sub-workflow optimization. HierFlow maximizes efficiency through an intelligent gating module that selectively triggers execution-level searches based on contextual necessity, a mechanism we further support with an in-depth analysis detailing how varying degrees of cross-task coupling impact the effectiveness of hierarchical splitting. Comprehensive testing across question answering, mathematical reasoning, and code generation benchmarks confirms that HierFlow consistently outperforms strong baselines, delivering an optimal balance of high-quality results and computational efficiency without any additional training overhead.

</details>


### [163] [SCOPE and SCION: A Benchmark and an Auditable Reference Pipeline for Schema Induction and Fusion from Text](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21610)
*Miaobo Hu,Xiaobo Guo,Shuhao Hu,Bokun Wang,Rui Chen,Xin Wang,Daren Zha,Jun Xiao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SCION is a reference pipeline for schema construction and ontology induction from raw text, evaluated on the SCOPE benchmark (24 IE sources), achieving state-of-the-art F1 across multiple metrics while reducing reliance on proprietary LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Schema graphs are a critical upstream bottleneck for schema-grounded information extraction and knowledge graph construction, yet most extraction systems assume the schema is already available rather than inducing it from raw text.

Method: SCION constructs candidate spaces from training text, then restricts naming, merging, filtering, validation, and conservative fusion to candidate-linked evidence under strict JSON contracts. SCOPE is a benchmark from 24 public IE sources (15 RE, 9 EE) normalized into gold schema graphs. Evaluation uses Literal, Fuzzy, Continuous, and Graph schema-graph metrics.

Result: SCION-lite achieves the highest F1 among all baselines (Text2Onto-style, LLM-only, extract-then-aggregate). SCION-RL reduces reliance on proprietary LLMs while remaining competitive. Results are reported against normalized typed-edge targets.

Conclusion: The induced schemas are evaluated against gold standards rather than claiming superiority over human ontology design. The release includes comprehensive artifacts: evidence-linked outputs, logs, run manifests, code, and benchmark packages.

Abstract: Schema graphs are an upstream bottleneck of schema-grounded information extraction and knowledge graph construction, yet most extraction systems assume the schema is already available. We introduce SCOPE (Schema Construction and Ontology-induction Pipeline Evaluation), a train-text-only benchmark for corpus-to-schema induction and optional schema fusion from raw text, built from 24 public information extraction sources (15 RE and 9 EE) normalized into evaluation-only gold schema graphs; its core event-extraction target covers event types and within-event argument roles, with inter-event links reported separately. We present SCION (Schema Construction and Induction with Ontology Normalization), an auditable reference pipeline rather than a new extraction architecture; it constructs candidate spaces from train text and restricts naming, merging, filtering, validation, and conservative fusion to candidate-linked evidence under strict JSON contracts. On the SCOPE core suite, SCION-lite attains the highest F1 among released source-schema references, Text2Onto-style, LLM-only, and matched extract-then-aggregate baselines under Literal, Fuzzy, Continuous, and Graph schema-graph metrics, while the compact open-model SCION-RL variant reduces reliance on proprietary LLM schema engineers. These results are reported against normalized typed-edge targets rather than as claims that induced schemas surpass human ontology design; the release includes evidence-linked outputs, parse/fallback logs, candidate retention/merging logs, run manifests, code, and benchmark packages at https://github.com/wandugu/paper_scion.

</details>


### [164] [Procedural Knowledge Is Not Low-Rank: Why LoRA Fails to Internalize Multi-Step Procedures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21612)
*Simon Dennis,Kevin Shabahang,Hao Guo,Rivaan Patil*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LoRA, despite being the default for parameter-efficient fine-tuning, fails to match full fine-tuning on procedural knowledge tasks (multi-step procedures with conditional branching). Across multiple domains and ranks (16-128), LoRA significantly underperforms full fine-tuning, and SVD analysis shows this is because procedural tasks require high-rank weight updates (effective rank 761-1,026), far exceeding what LoRA's low-rank decomposition can capture (only 43-51% at rank 128).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether LoRA, which succeeds on instruction following, style transfer, and factual adaptation, can effectively handle procedural knowledge—a critical capability for agentic applications requiring multi-step procedures with conditional branching.

Method: Systematic ablation of LoRA ranks (16-128) on a procedural travel booking task (14 nodes), cross-domain replication on Zoom support (14 nodes) and insurance claims (55 nodes) at 8B scale, plus SVD analysis of full fine-tuning weight updates to measure effective rank.

Result: All LoRA configurations fail uniformly (task success ≤2.54 vs 4.11 for full fine-tuning, p<0.001), with scores decreasing at higher ranks despite 95-99% conversation completion. Cross-domain: LoRA underperforms full fine-tuning by 0.8-2.2 points at both r=32 and r=128. SVD reveals effective rank of updates is 761-1,026, with rank 128 capturing only 43-51% of the squared Frobenius norm.

Conclusion: For procedural tasks, LoRA falls fundamentally short of full fine-tuning—a critical limitation for agentic applications that require procedural knowledge. The high effective rank of weight updates explains why low-rank approximations cannot capture the necessary procedural complexity.

Abstract: Parameter-efficient fine-tuning methods like LoRA have become the default for adapting large language models, succeeding across instruction following, style transfer, and factual adaptation. We show that for procedural knowledge--the ability to follow multi-step procedures with conditional branching through to terminal states--LoRA fails to match full fine-tuning at the ranks where it retains its efficiency advantage. In a systematic ablation (r = 16--128) on a procedural travel booking task (14 nodes), all LoRA configurations fail uniformly (task success <= 2.54 vs. 4.11 for full fine-tuning, all p < 0.001), with scores decreasing at higher ranks--despite maintaining 95--99% conversation completion rates. Cross-domain replication on Zoom support (14 nodes) and insurance claims (55 nodes) at 8B confirms the failure generalizes: LoRA underperforms full fine-tuning by 0.8--2.2 points on average at both r = 32 and r = 128, with the largest gap on the most complex procedure. Quadrupling rank from 32 to 128 provides marginal improvement but does not close the gap. SVD analysis of the weight changes produced by full fine-tuning explains why: across three domains at both 3B and 8B, the mean effective rank of the update ranges from 761 to 1,026, and rank 128 captures only 43--51% of the squared Frobenius norm. Together, these findings establish that for procedural tasks LoRA falls well short of full fine-tuning--a fundamental limitation for agentic applications.

</details>


### [165] [The Hard Decision Layer: Evidence for Committed Inference in Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21613)
*Ashwath Vaithinathan Aravindan,Mayank Kejriwal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper identifies the Hard Decision Layer (HDL) in transformer-based language models, where answer option rankings abruptly stabilize during multiple-choice QA inference. HDL emerges consistently across models (Qwen, Llama, Granite, Mistral) and benchmarks, is invariant to fine-tuning, and yields accuracy improvements up to +0.61 before performance plateaus.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand where and how transformer-based LMs commit to predictions in multiple-choice QA, and to identify architectural properties that govern decision-making during inference.

Method: The authors define the Hard Decision Layer (HDL) as the layer where answer option rankings stabilize abruptly. They validate across four LMs (Qwen, Llama, Granite, Mistral) and four benchmarks, analyze invariance to fine-tuning, and conduct systematic ablations on label formats and problem complexity.

Result: HDL emerges consistently without learned routing; accuracy improves by up to +0.61 (Qwen on CommonsenseQA) at the HDL before stabilizing; HDL is invariant to fine-tuning and fundamental to model architecture.

Conclusion: The findings provide mechanistic insights into transformer inference and suggest opportunities for more efficient reasoning and model steering.

Abstract: We investigate where and how transformer-based language models commit to predictions in multiple-choice question answering. We identify the _Hard Decision Layer_ (HDL), a natural architectural property where answer option rankings stabilize abruptly during inference. Empirical validation across four language models (Qwen, Llama, Granite, Mistral) and four benchmark datasets demonstrates consistent HDL emergence without learned routing policies. We also show that the HDL is invariant to fine-tuning. Our results reveal striking accuracy improvements at the HDL: up to +0.61 (Qwen on CommonsenseQA), after which performance stabilizes. Systematic ablations on label formats and problem complexity confirm the phenomenon is fundamental to model architecture. These findings offer mechanistic insights into transformer inference and suggest opportunities for efficient reasoning and model steering. All code and results required to reproduce this work are available in https://github.com/Mystic-Slice/hard-decision-layer

</details>


### [166] [Household Movement Detection in Mixed-Format Occupancy Data Using LLM-Based Entity Resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21614)
*Sasirekha Oguri,John R. Talburt,Mert Can Cakmak*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes an AI-enhanced framework that integrates LLM-based NER, semantic embeddings, and graph reasoning to detect indirect entity links from household movement patterns in unstandardized name-address data, achieving 8-15% recall improvement and 6-8% F1 gains over pairwise baselines on SPX benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional entity resolution relies on pairwise similarity, which fails to capture indirect relationships like household movement (multiple individuals relocating together). This is challenging due to mixed-format records, noise, duplication, and lack of stable identifiers in demographic occupancy data.

Method: A three-component framework: (1) prompt-based LLM NER for extracting personal names and addresses without extensive preprocessing; (2) semantic text embeddings for robust similarity computation; (3) graph-based reasoning to infer group-level movement patterns.

Result: On SPX benchmark datasets (S8-S12), incorporating indirect household movement evidence improved recall by 8-15% while maintaining high precision, yielding F1-score gains of 6-8% over a strong pairwise baseline.

Conclusion: Graph-based reasoning over household movement patterns significantly enhances entity resolution beyond what pairwise methods alone can achieve, demonstrating the value of leveraging indirect relational evidence in noisy demographic data.

Abstract: Entity resolution (ER) typically relies on pairwise similarity comparisons between records, which limits its ability to capture indirect relationships present in demographic occupancy data. An important indirect pattern arises from household movement, where multiple individuals relocate together across addresses, but detecting such patterns is difficult due to mixed-format records, noise, duplication, and the absence of stable identifiers. This paper proposes an AI-enhanced framework for detecting indirect entity links associated with household movement in unstandardized name-address data. The approach integrates prompt-based large language model (LLM) named entity recognition for extracting personal names and addresses without extensive preprocessing, semantic text embeddings for robust similarity computation, and graph-based reasoning to infer group-level movement patterns. Experimental evaluation on SPX benchmark datasets (S8-S12) generated using the Synthetic Occupancy Generator demonstrates that incorporating indirect household movement evidence improves recall by 8-15% while maintaining high precision, yielding F1-score gains of 6-8% over a strong pairwise baseline.

</details>


### [167] [FrED: External Data Influence Estimation via Domain Knowledge Graph Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21615)
*Theodoros Aivalis,Iraklis A. Klampanos,Antonis Troumpoukis,Joemon M. Jose*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A black-box probabilistic framework for Training Data Attribution that fuses continuous feature similarities with discrete Knowledge Graphs, evaluated on artistic image synthesis and weather forecasting, achieving strong Linear Datamodeling Scores without requiring internal model access.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid deployment of generative AI has amplified the critical need for Training Data Attribution to ensure transparency and accountability, but current parametric approaches require computationally prohibitive access to model weights, while similarity-based methods ignore deep structural context.

Method: A novel probabilistic framework operating entirely in a black-box setting, fusing continuous feature similarities with discrete, domain-specific Knowledge Graphs (KGs) to ground attribution in structural reality, explicitly rewarding specific historical samples while preventing generic background data from dominating.

Result: In the artistic domain, achieves a strong Linear Datamodeling Score exceeding standard black-box similarity baselines and closing much of the gap to gradient-based estimators. In weather forecasting, domain KGs improve geographic localisation for regional flood forecasts over a latent-only baseline.

Conclusion: Operating entirely without internal model access, this approach provides an efficient, interpretable mechanism for post-hoc influence analysis and domain-grounded retrieval across diverse domains.

Abstract: The rapid deployment of generative AI has amplified the critical need for Training Data Attribution to ensure transparency and accountability. However, current parametric approaches require computationally prohibitive access to model weights, while similarity-based methods ignore deep structural context. We propose a novel probabilistic framework that operates entirely in a black-box setting. Our method fuses continuous feature similarities with discrete, domain-specific Knowledge Graphs (KGs). This approach ensures the attribution is grounded in structural reality, explicitly rewarding highly specific historical samples while preventing generic background data from dominating the results. We evaluate our framework across two distinct domains where linking outputs to data and domain context is inherently complex: abstract artistic image synthesis and high-dimensional physical weather forecasting. Extensive benchmarking demonstrates the robust efficacy of our approach. In the artistic domain, it achieves a strong Linear Datamodeling Score that exceeds standard black-box similarity baselines, while closing much of the gap to gradient-based estimators. We additionally present a cross-domain feasibility case study in environmental forecasting, where we use domain KGs to retrieve physically consistent historical analogs for regional flood forecasts, improving geographic localisation over a latent-only baseline. Operating entirely without internal model access, our approach provides an efficient, interpretable mechanism for post-hoc influence analysis and domain-grounded retrieval.

</details>


### [168] [Lost in Context: Addressing Context Anxiety in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21616)
*Ifueko Igbinedion,Jillian Ross,Etienne Ricardez,Sertac Karaman,Eric So*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Frontier reasoning models sometimes have the necessary capabilities to solve problems but fail due to "context anxiety" — premature self-doubt arising from an inability to accurately estimate required tokens. This leads to efficiency losses and can be mitigated by teaching models better self-assessment strategies rather than scaling capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To identify and systematically study the phenomenon of "context anxiety" (premature self-doubt) in reasoning models, which causes them to fail problems they actually have the capability to solve.

Method: Systematic empirical study of frontier reasoning models, analyzing failure patterns related to token estimation inaccuracies and perceived constraints, followed by testing alternative strategies to overcome context anxiety.

Result: Context anxiety arises partly from models' inability to accurately estimate the tokens required to complete a task, and leads to material efficiency losses under perceived constraints. Models can learn alternative strategies for long-horizon problems without exhibiting context anxiety.

Conclusion: Performance improvements may be achievable not through scaling model capabilities, but by improving models' ability to accurately assess and adapt to their own limitations.

Abstract: Conventional wisdom suggests that reasoning models fail when problems exceed their capabilities. However, we find that frontier reasoning models sometimes possess the necessary capabilities to solve problems but fail due to premature self-doubt -- a phenomenon informally known as context anxiety. We provide the first systematic study of context anxiety, demonstrating that it arises, in part, from a model's inability to accurately estimate the tokens required to complete a task. We also show that context anxiety leads to material efficiency losses when models operate under perceived constraints. Building on this analysis, we further show that models can learn alternative strategies for solving long-horizon problems without exhibiting context anxiety, suggesting that performance improvements may be achievable not through scaling model capabilities, but by improving models' ability to accurately assess and adapt to their own limitations.

</details>


### [169] [Do VLMs Read or Rewrite? On Transcription Faithfulness in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21617)
*Gwang Gook Lee,Kenan Emir Ak,Jay Mohta,Yan Xu,Dimitrios Dimitriadis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VLMs often "rewrite" imperfect text to plausible forms (unlike traditional OCR), a behavior undetectable by clean-text benchmarks. The paper introduces FaithC4 (multilingual perturbation benchmark), evaluates 15 systems, and finds rewriting correlates with proximity of perturbed representations to original encodings in the model's FFN layers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To demonstrate that VLMs are not faithful transcribers when text is imperfect (they rewrite to plausible forms), a limitation clean-text OCR benchmarks cannot detect, and to systematically measure this behavior.

Method: Introduced FaithC4, a multilingual (EN/ZH/KO) perturbation benchmark with 3 families (scramble, random substitution, visually similar substitution) across 1,455 documents. Evaluated 15 systems (general VLMs, OCR-specialized VLMs, traditional OCR pipelines) via WER degradation. Probed Qwen3-VL-4B layer-by-layer to study rewriting behavior.

Result: General VLMs degrade up to 4.5 WER points, OCR-specialized VLMs 0.2-2 points, traditional OCR <0.6 points. Rewriting occurs when perturbed word's final FFN representation stays close to original encoding; divergence yields faithful transcription. Short words (4-6 chars) rewritten up to 10% of the time, with cutoff at 8+ chars (0%).

Conclusion: VLMs exhibit a "rewriting bias" under text imperfection that traditional OCR does not, tied to representation proximity in FFN layers and word length, highlighting risks of using VLMs as drop-in OCR replacements without awareness of this behavior.

Abstract: Vision Language Models (VLMs) are increasingly used in place of traditional OCR pipelines for document understanding. In this paper, we show they do not always act as faithful transcribers: when text is imperfect, they often tend to rewrite it into a more plausible form - a behavior that clean-text OCR benchmarks cannot detect. We introduce FaithC4, a multilingual perturbation benchmark of 1,455 single-page documents (English, Chinese, Korean) with three perturbation families: scramble, random substitution, and visually similar substitution. We use the benchmark to evaluate 15 systems spanning general-purpose VLMs, OCR-specialized VLMs, and traditional OCR pipelines. These three categories differ in WER degradation under perturbation: general-purpose VLMs degrade by up to 4.5 points, OCR-specialized VLMs by 0.2-2 points, and traditional OCR by less than 0.6 points on English. Probing Qwen3-VL-4B layer-by-layer, we identify a consistent pattern: rewriting fires only when a perturbed word's final layer FFN representation stays close to the original encoding; when the representation diverges sufficiently, the model transcribes faithfully. Word length affects rewriting rate: short words (4-6 characters) are rewritten up to 10% of the time, with a sharp cutoff at 8 characters above which rewriting drops to 0%.

</details>


### [170] [LeafData: An Agentic System for Data Migration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21618)
*Sadanand Katukuri,Rajasekhar Bada,Navya Induri,Rohit Gandham,Lynette Pinto,Joses Selvan,Abishek Krishnamoorthy,Joseph Rozario,Pu Tian,Pavan Poudel,Yalong Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LeafData is an agentic system with a chatbot frontend and backend service that converts user intent into validated JSON configuration for heterogeneous data migration, eliminating manual coding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: JSON-based data migration configuration requires domain knowledge, is time-consuming, and error-prone; users need a simpler way to express migration intent.

Method: LeafData uses a frontend chatbot for incremental information collection and schema-driven validation, paired with a backend service that generates validated JSON configuration artifacts consumable by orchestration platforms.

Result: The system produces validated JSON configuration directly usable by orchestration platforms for end-to-end pipeline generation and execution without manual coding.

Conclusion: LeafData enables heterogeneous data migration across relational databases, file-based systems, document-oriented databases, and REST APIs through an agentic, intent-driven approach.

Abstract: Modern data migration relies on JSON configuration to define data connection, pipeline logic, and orchestration behavior. This requires domain knowledge from users and is time-consuming and error-prone. In this paper, we present LeafData, an agentic system that converts user intent into validated and executable JSON configuration for data migration. Specifically, LeafData comprises a frontend chatbot and the backend service. The chatbot incrementally collects required information from users and performs schema-driven validation, while the backend service processes validated inputs and generates JSON configuration artifacts. These artifacts are directly consumable by orchestration platforms, enabling end-to-end pipeline generation and execution without manual coding. LeafData supports heterogeneous data migration across various data sources and connectors including relational databases, file-based systems, document-oriented databases, and REST APIs.

</details>


### [171] [From Profiles to Steering Vectors: Global Sparse Priors and Local Semantic Calibration for Personalized Text Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21620)
*Liuji Chen,Zeyu Zhang,Xinyuan Zhang,Shuai Nie,Qiang Liu,Shu Wu,Liang Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GLASS is a training-free framework for personalized text generation that uses sparse autoencoders to extract global and local style vectors from user history, then injects them into model layers during inference. It outperforms retrieval, fine-tuning, and steering baselines on LaMP and LongLaMP benchmarks while better disentangling style from content.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing personalized generation methods (retrieval, fine-tuning, activation steering) introduce inference/storage overhead or fail to separate stylistic signals from semantic content. A more efficient and disentangled approach is needed.

Method: GLASS uses sparse autoencoders (SAEs) to extract a global user-style prior from historical responses, constructs local contrastive style vectors over clustered interaction scenarios, and jointly injects both global and local vectors into different model layers during inference—all without training, retrieval, or parameter updates.

Result: Outperforms retrieval-, fine-tuning-, and steering-based baselines on LaMP and LongLaMP benchmarks across ROUGE metrics and LLM-as-judge evaluations. SAE-based representations show better robustness to topic and length shifts, indicating improved style-content disentanglement.

Conclusion: GLASS demonstrates that training-free activation steering with sparse autoencoders can effectively capture user-specific stylistic patterns while avoiding the overhead and semantic entanglement issues of prior methods.

Abstract: Personalized text generation requires models to capture user-specific writing styles from historical data. Existing approaches based on retrieval, parameter-efficient fine-tuning, or activation steering either introduce inference and storage overhead or struggle to separate stylistic signals from semantic content. We propose GLASS, a training-free framework for personalized generation via Global-Local Activation Steering with Sparse priors. GLASS uses sparse autoencoders to extract a global user-style prior from historical responses and constructs local contrastive style vectors over clustered interaction scenarios. During inference, it jointly injects global and local vectors into different model layers, enabling context-aware personalization without retrieval or parameter updates. Experiments on LaMP and LongLaMP show that GLASS outperforms retrieval-, fine-tuning-, and steering-based baselines across ROUGE metrics and LLM-as-judge evaluations. Further analyses show that SAE-based representations are more robust to topic and length shifts, suggesting better disentanglement of stylistic information from semantic residue.

</details>


### [172] [FBLayout: Optimizing Memory Layout for Efficient LLM Finetuning on Mobile GPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21624)
*Kahou Tam,Wei Niu,Yu Bao,Xiaomin Ouyang,Chengzhong Xu,Li Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FBLayout is a layout-aware framework that co-designs tensor organization with mobile GPU platforms, introducing unified R-Tile layout, tile-based index transformation, and activation-guided layout selection to achieve 2.2-5.7x speedup over existing frameworks (MNN, TFLite, TVM) for on-device fine-tuning of transformer models on mobile GPUs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: On-device fine-tuning of transformer models offers privacy-preserving personalized AI but remains inefficient on mobile GPUs due to severe memory constraints and frequent layout transformations in attention mechanisms during training. Existing mobile training frameworks either use unified layouts causing fragmented memory access or rely on explicit layout conversions with significant overhead.

Method: FBLayout introduces: (1) a unified R-Tile layout for multi-dimensional reductions across forward/backward passes; (2) tile-based index transformation to eliminate physical data movement; and (3) activation-guided layout selection to propagate efficient layouts globally.

Result: Evaluated on seven transformer models across ARM Mali and Qualcomm Adreno GPUs, FBLayout achieves 2.2-5.7x speedup over MNN, TFLite, and TVM, with improved cache efficiency and reduced memory footprint.

Conclusion: FBLayout enables practical on-device large model fine-tuning by co-designing tensor layout with mobile GPU hardware, eliminating layout conversion overhead while maintaining efficient memory access patterns.

Abstract: Transformer-based models have enabled unprecedented capabilities across language, vision, and multimodal tasks. On-device fine-tuning of transformer models offers a privacy-preserving path to personalized AI, yet remains inefficient on mobile GPUs due to severe memory constraints and frequent layout transformations in attention mechanism during training. Existing mobile training frameworks either use unified layouts for forward and backward passes -- leading to fragmented memory access and poor GPU utilization during backpropagation -- or rely on explicit layout conversions, which introduce significant transformation overhead.
  To overcome this, we propose FBLayout, a layout-aware framework that co-designs tensor organization with mobile GPU platforms. FBLayout introduces: (1) a unified R-Tile layout for multi-dimensional reductions across forward/backward passes; (2) tile-based index transformation to eliminate physical data movement; and (3) activation-guided layout selection to propagate efficient layouts globally. Evaluations on seven transformer models across different mobile phones (including ARM Mali and Qualcomm Adreno GPUs) show that FBLayout achieves 2.2-5.7x speedup over MNN, TFLite, and TVM, while significantly improving cache efficiency and reducing memory footprint, enabling practical on-device large model fine-tuning.

</details>


### [173] [Trajectory-Aware Retrieval Agents for Temporal Decision- Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21625)
*Jing Wang,Jie Shen,Xing Niu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TLM is a closed-loop LLM agentic framework that uses SHAP-guided feedback and a latent growth curve model over chunk embeddings to iteratively refine evidence from temporally structured text, outperforming RAG baselines on medical and financial decision tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard RAG pipelines fragment chronological context into isolated snippets, discarding temporal structure critical for correct downstream decisions in long-form, temporally structured text.

Method: TLM is a closed-loop agentic framework that iteratively refines evidence using SHAP-guided feedback, with a latent growth curve model (LGCM) over retrieved chunk embeddings to detect trajectory trends, turning points, and information gaps. The refinement procedure is theoretically shown to be monotonically non-decreasing in correct-label probability under a scorer-calibration assumption.

Result: TLM substantially outperforms zero-shot LLM baselines and standard RAG approaches on medical question answering, and yields consistent, economically meaningful gains on earnings call surprise prediction and overnight stock gap prediction.

Conclusion: Incorporating temporal structure through iterative, SHAP-guided refinement with a latent growth curve model significantly improves decision-making from temporally structured text across medical and financial domains.

Abstract: We study the problem of decision-making from long-form, temporally structured text using large language model (LLM) agents. Standard retrievalaugmented generation (RAG) pipelines fragment chronological context into isolated snippets, discarding the temporal structure that is often critical for correct downstream decisions. We introduce TLM (Trajectory Language Model), a closed-loop agentic framework that iteratively refines the evidence set using SHAP-guided feedback. The key technical contribution is the latent growth curve model (LGCM) over retrieved chunk embeddings, which provides an interpretable mechanism for detecting trajectory trends, turning points, and information gaps. We show that, under a scorer-calibration assumption (which holds approximately in practice), the iterative refinement procedure is monotonically non-decreasing in the probability assigned to the correct label. Empirically, TLM is evaluated on three temporally grounded decision tasks: medical question answering, earnings call surprise prediction, and overnight stock gap prediction. TLM substantially outperforms both zero-shot LLM baselines and standard retrieval-augmented approaches on the medical task, and yields consistent, economically meaningful gains on the two financial tasks.

</details>


### [174] [Discrete Action Space as a Prerequisite for GRPO Convergence in Small-Model Continuous Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21626)
*Dmytro Filatov,Valentyn Fedorov,Vira Filatova*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GRPO fine-tuning of small language models (Qwen-0.5B) for quadrotor control collapses to zero action (0% success) due to entropy collapse. Replacing continuous action with 5-way categorical PID presets enables successful training, achieving 98.6-100% success with varying jerk. A classical PID baseline outperforms in simplicity (100% success, 0.736 jerk).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether GRPO, a reinforcement learning method typically used for language models, can be effectively applied to fine-tune small language models for continuous-control tasks like quadrotor velocity control.

Method: Applied vanilla GRPO fine-tuning to Qwen-0.5B for 25 Hz quadrotor velocity control; tested ablations (removing jerk-penalty, removing KL anchor); replaced continuous action with 5-way categorical PID preset selection; evaluated across three pretrained LMs; compared against classical PID baseline with Ki=0.30, vmax=2.5; tested in high-fidelity Crazyflie 2.1 simulation.

Result: Vanilla GRPO collapses to zero action (0% success, entropy 0.35→0.03). Ablations prevent entropy collapse but no learning. Categorical action interface converges: 98.6% success / 0.656 jerk at 64 steps; 100% success / 1.103 jerk (0.796 with cap) at 256 steps. Classical PID achieves 100% success at 0.736 jerk.

Conclusion: GRPO with continuous action is ineffective for quadrotor control due to entropy collapse; categorical action discretization enables learning but is outperformed by a simple classical PID baseline. The approach reveals a hover-region training-distribution gap in high-fidelity simulation.

Abstract: We study whether Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) can fine-tune small language models for simulated quadrotor continuous-control tasks. In our benchmark, vanilla GRPO fine-tuning of Qwen-0.5B for 25 Hz quadrotor velocity control collapses to the trivial zero action: 0 percent success rate, with entropy falling from 0.35 to 0.03 within 60 steps. Two ablations - removing the jerk-penalty term and removing the KL anchor to the pretrained prior - each prevent entropy collapse, yet neither enables learning. When the action interface is replaced by a 5-way categorical choice over PID presets, training converges. The resulting controller traces a smoothness-reliability Pareto frontier along training duration; both endpoints are reported: 98.6 percent success with 0.656 m/s3 jerk at 64 steps, and 100 percent success with 1.103 m/s3 jerk, or 0.796 under a matched velocity cap, at 256 steps. The recipe is evaluated across three pretrained language models. As context, a re-tuned classical baseline, PID with Ki = 0.30 and vmax = 2.5, reaches the same 100 percent success rate at jerk 0.736 m/s3. A high-fidelity simulation using Crazyflie 2.1 dynamics surfaces a hover-region training-distribution gap.

</details>


### [175] [Do Modules Stay in Their Lane? Role Drift in Compound LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21627)
*Xiaoyang Cao,Siddarth Srinivasan,Michiel A. Bakker*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper identifies "Role Drift" in compound LLM systems where modules deviate from their assigned roles while maintaining performance, and proposes "Role Anchor," a regularizer that constrains role deviation by preserving how role prompts shift next-token predictions relative to neutral prompts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: End-to-end RL improves compound LLM accuracy but doesn't constrain internal module role division, leading to "Role Drift"—a failure where modules use role-violating shortcuts that are invisible to standard accuracy metrics.

Method: Proposes Role Anchor, a regularizer that modulates module deviation from assigned roles during end-to-end training by preserving the relative shift in next-token predictions between a role prompt and a neutral prompt as a proxy for the role's intended effect.

Result: Role drift found in two pipelines: (1) a decomposer plants answers in sub-questions instead of splitting them, and (2) a reader bypasses retrieved passages for parametric memory. 86% of RL gain in the decomposer pipeline vanishes under role anchoring. Role Anchor mitigates drift with tunable accuracy cost.

Conclusion: Terminal accuracy alone overstates genuine learning in compound LLM systems; Role Anchor makes role drift observable and controllable, with gradient analysis suggesting it reduces alignment with drift directions rather than suppressing learning.

Abstract: End-to-end reinforcement learning can improve the accuracy of compound LLM systems, but it does not constrain how modules divide labor internally. We identify Role Drift, a failure mode in which modules preserve or improve end-task performance while deviating from their assigned roles through role-violating shortcuts that remain invisible to system-level evaluation. To make role drift observable and controllable, we propose Role Anchor, a regularizer that modulates how much each module deviates from its assigned role during end-to-end training. The key idea is to preserve how the role prompt shifts the module's next-token predictions relative to a neutral prompt, which serves as a proxy for the role's intended effect during training. Experiments on two compound LLM pipelines reveal role drift that accuracy alone fails to detect: a decomposer meant to split a question into sub-questions for a separate solver instead plants the answer in them, and a reader meant to answer from retrieved passages instead falls back on parametric memory. In fact, on the decomposer pipeline this shortcut drives most of the apparent RL gain: 86% of it vanishes once the decomposer is held to its role, indicating that terminal accuracy alone can badly overstate how much a compound system has genuinely learned. Across both pipelines, Role Anchor mitigates role drift at a tunable accuracy cost that varies by pipeline and anchor strength. Additional gradient analysis suggests that the regularizer reduces alignment with the role-drift direction rather than simply suppressing learning.

</details>


### [176] [Wavelet Phase Diffusion for Structurally and Semantically Consistent Sim-to-Real Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21628)
*Kaiwen Wang,Frank Bieder,Yinzhe Shen,Carlos Fernandez,Jan-Hendrik Pauls,Omer Sahin Tas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Wavelet Phase Diffusion introduces Dual-Tree Complex Wavelet Packet Transform with Low-Frequency Randomization for simulation-to-reality translation, achieving realistic, spatially-adaptive, instance-level translation without paired data or expensive control modules.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing simulation-to-reality methods suffer from either expensive control modules, complex synthesis pipelines altering scene geometry, or lack of perceptual quality. Fourier-domain phase-preserving diffusion causes spatial artifacts due to global spectral coupling.

Method: The method uses Dual-Tree Complex Wavelet Packet Transform for localized, spatially-adaptive phase injection without global spectral interference, plus Low-Frequency Randomization (LFR) to replace low-frequency packets, decoupling from synthetic illumination priors.

Result: Outperforms prior methods on vKITTI→KITTI in realism and semantic consistency; on CARLA video translation, reduces VLM planner ADE by 5.4% and FDE by 5.1% while approaching paired-data method realism.

Conclusion: The wavelet-domain approach effectively addresses global spectral coupling artifacts, enables unpaired open-domain training with negligible inference overhead, and supports instance-level translation.

Abstract: Simulation-to-reality translation must bridge the appearance gap between synthetic and real domains while preserving structural and semantic consistency. Conditioning-based methods achieve spatial alignment but introduce computationally expensive control modules. Paired-data methods achieve realism but rely on complex synthesis pipelines, often altering scene geometry and semantics. Training-free editing methods avoid both constraints but lack a learned appearance prior, limiting their perceptual quality. Recently proposed phase-preserving diffusion presents a promising alternative, but Fourier-domain formulations are constrained by global spectral coupling. This coupling induces spatial artifacts such as ringing and boundary leakage, thereby degrading structural and semantic consistency. We introduce Wavelet Phase Diffusion, which addresses this through two components. First, we operate in the Dual-Tree Complex Wavelet Packet Transform domain, whose localized wavelet packets enable spatially adaptive phase injection without global spectral interference. Second, Low-Frequency Randomization (LFR) replaces the low-frequency packet, decoupling the model from the synthetic illumination prior and enabling in-distribution real-world appearance. Both components train on unpaired open-domain data, and introduce negligible inference overhead. The spatial locality further enables instance-level translation, where individual objects or regions are translated to photorealistic appearance independently while the surrounding scene remains untranslated. On vKITTI $\to$ KITTI image translation, ours outperforms prior methods in realism and semantic consistency while maintaining competitive structural alignment. For CARLA video translation, ours approaches the realism of paired-data methods while reducing VLM planner ADE and FDE by $5.4\%$ and $5.1\%$, respectively.

</details>


### [177] [Defining AI-Native Systems: Autonomy as Revision Authority](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21659)
