*Cheng Tan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper defines "AI-native" systems technically: a system is AI-native when an AI autonomously rewrites the system's own implementations, with requirements for escalation detection, verification, and verified fallback, while humans retain ownership of purpose and correctness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: "AI-native" is widely used as a marketing term without a precise technical definition, hindering clear discourse about systems where AI makes autonomous decisions.

Method: The paper introduces a decision-level model distinguishing occupancy (who executes) from revision authority (who may change), organizes revision authority into a ladder (self-tuning, self-rewriting, self-architecting), and defines AI-native as autonomous self-rewriting with escalation detection, verification, and fallback.

Result: A precise technical definition of AI-native systems based on revision authority over implementations, not model capability.

Conclusion: AI-native is defined along a single axis of decision authority, requiring AI autonomy in rewriting implementations while keeping purpose and correctness human-owned.

Abstract: AI has begun to write systems code: agents now synthesize, verify, and deploy system components. Despite this shift, "AI-native" remains a marketing term with no precise technical definition. This paper gives it one. We define AI-nativeness along a single axis---authority over the system's own decisions rather than by the capability of the underlying AI models. Building on a decision-level model of a system, we distinguish occupancy (who executes a decision) from revision authority (who may change it), organize revision authority into a ladder---self-tuning, self-rewriting, self-architecting and define a system as AI-native when an AI autonomously rewrites the system's own implementations. The definition further requires an escalation detector, a verification procedure, and a verified fallback, while leaving purpose and correctness human-owned.

</details>


### [178] [Persistent Computational State: A Session-Centric Runtime for Generative World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21686)
*Zhen Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that failures of video world models as simulators are not due to model architecture but because request-centric serving discards non-recomputable runtime state (Persistent Computational State, PCS). By preserving and restoring this state (observation+RNG, memory bank, or KV context), they achieve byte-identical continuations after excursions, with minimal overhead (0.012 ms checkpoint/restore vs 1.85s generation) and show world memory eviction should be relevance-based, not recency-based.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current video world models fail as simulators when forking, rolling out futures, and backtracking. Recent work attributes this to model architecture, but the authors suspect the true cause is the serving infrastructure discarding runtime state.

Method: Define Persistent Computational State (PCS) as the minimal non-recomputable state that must survive across requests; measure what constitutes PCS for different architectures; build a session-centric runtime with checkpoint/restore; compare byte-identical continuation fidelity across intact vs corrupted state restoration.

Result: Restoring full PCS (observation+RNG, memory bank, or KV context) yields byte-identical continuations after excursions; corrupting only the RNG degrades output. Checkpoint/restore costs 0.012 ms versus 1.85s generation step. Sessions scale to 1024 and become host-bounded rather than device-bounded. World memory eviction must be relevance-based (not recency-based as in LLMs).

Conclusion: The simulator failure is not a model problem but a serving infrastructure problem. Request-centric serving inherited from LLMs is inappropriate for world models because they carry non-recomputable PCS. A session-centric runtime with PCS checkpoint/restore fixes the issue with negligible overhead.

Abstract: Generative world models are increasingly driven as simulators: a planner forks a state, rolls out futures, backtracks, and returns to a visited viewpoint. Recent benchmarks establish that current video world models fail this usage, and attribute it to the model, prescribing new architectures and training objectives. We show this attribution is incomplete, and for an important class of models simply wrong. Snapshotting the state the runtime already holds -- an observation plus RNG state, a memory bank, or a windowed KV context, by architecture -- and restoring it after a genuine excursion reproduces the never-left continuation byte-identically on all three; corrupting only the RNG degrades it. The capability was never missing: request-centric serving discarded it, inheriting from language-model serving the assumption that runtime state is recomputable -- but world-model state carries a non-recomputable kernel. We define Persistent Computational State (PCS), the minimal non-recomputable state that must survive across requests, show it can be discovered by measurement, and build a session-centric runtime over it. Checkpoint and restore cost 0.012 ms against a 1.85 s generation step; resident sessions become host- rather than device-bounded (measured to 1,024); and world memory must be evicted by relevance to the return, not recency -- the inverse of LLM practice.

</details>


### [179] [What AI Red-Team Evaluations Can and Cannot Prove](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21735)
*Bandana Kaur*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper establishes a mathematical framework for determining the evidential ceiling of AI red-team evaluations, showing that above a calculable harm rate, modest benchmarks can provide strong evidence of safety, while below that rate, no feasible passive benchmark can meet evidentiary standards. The framework covers adaptive and automated red-teaming and reveals that current benchmarks are adequate for common harms but orders of magnitude insufficient for rare, catastrophic ones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve the ambiguity in claims supported by red-team evaluations by providing a formal, calculable boundary that separates what evaluations can and cannot certify about AI safety.

Method: The authors derive a closed-form expression for the evidential ceiling—the maximum factor by which evaluation results can update belief under a fixed testing budget—under a null benchmark result. They extend this to cover adaptive and automated red-teaming via hypothesis-conditioned elicitation rates and audit eight evaluation suites against the derived boundary.

Result: Above a calculable harm rate, a clean sheet in a modest benchmark outweighs a single reproduced failure. Below that rate, no feasible passive benchmark provides specified evidence of safety. Current benchmarks are adequate for high-frequency harms but insufficient by orders of magnitude for rare, catastrophic ones.

Conclusion: Safety benchmarks are not uninformative—they are informative about a specific, computable set of propositions. The discipline required is to state which propositions they support, and the derived boundary provides a formal method for doing so.

Abstract: Red-team evaluations of AI models support some claims and not others, and the boundary between the two is calculable rather than merely a matter of judgment. We define the evidential ceiling of an evaluation as the largest factor by which one result can move belief under a fixed testing budget, derive it in closed form for the benchmark null result, and use it to locate that boundary exactly. We find that above a calculable harm rate, a benchmark of modest size certifies a category to a stated evidentiary standard, and a clean sheet is then the stronger of the two possible observations, outweighing a single reproduced failure. Below that rate, no passive benchmark of feasible size provides the specified evidence of safety under the fixed scoring rule and approximately independent trial structure. The crossing between the two regimes has a closed form. The bound is not specific to benchmarks: written in terms of a procedure's hypothesis conditioned elicitation rates, it covers adaptive and automated red teaming as well, and shows that discrimination between the hypotheses rather than attack success is what determines evidential worth. Auditing eight evaluation suites against the boundary, we find that current benchmarks are adequate for high-frequency harm categories and several orders of magnitude short for rare, catastrophic ones. Safety benchmarks are not uninformative. They are informative about a specific and computable set of propositions, and the discipline they need is to state which.

</details>


### [180] [From Seasonality to Semantics: Benchmarking a Hybrid Probabilistic Forecasting System for Roadblocks in Bolivia](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21785)
*Rodrigo Vargas Sainz,Christian Berón Curti*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A hybrid probabilistic forecasting system combining Prophet time series decomposition with NLP embeddings and zero-shot classification on Bolivian news data predicts roadblocks up to 7 days ahead, outperforming SARIMA and LightGBM baselines with AUC-ROC of 0.677 and 10.9% Brier Score reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Roadblocks in Bolivia cause economic losses equivalent to 4% of GDP, yet no local predictive systems exist for anticipating these events to support logistical decision-making.

Method: Integrates Prophet time series decomposition with NLP vector semantic embeddings and zero-shot classification on a six-year Bolivian news corpus, evaluated via expanding walk-forward validation over 1,762 days across seven forecasting horizons (H+1 to H+7), compared against SARIMA and LightGBM baselines.

Result: Hybrid configuration (C6) achieved AUC-ROC of 0.677 at H+1 and reduced Brier Score by 10.9% (0.220 vs. 0.247) relative to baseline, with statistically significant error reduction across all horizons (p < 0.02).

Conclusion: Integrating semantic news signals enables detection of social tension peaks not captured by historical inertia, providing a technical tool for risk management in critical transport corridors.

Abstract: Roadblocks in Bolivia are a social conflict phenomenon with devastating economic impacts, estimated at losses equivalent to 4% of the national Gross Domestic Product. Despite their recurrence and impact, there is a lack of local predictive systems to anticipate these events for logistical decision-making. This paper presents a hybrid probabilistic forecasting system that integrates time series decomposition (Prophet) with natural language processing (NLP) techniques applied to a six-year corpus of Bolivian news coverage. The methodology employs vector semantic embeddings and zero-shot classification models to capture signals of discursive escalation prior to the materialization of the roadblocks. Using an expanding walk-forward validation scheme applied over 1,762 days and seven forecasting horizons (H+1 to H+7), seven internal configurations and four external benchmarks were compared, including SARIMA and LightGBM. The results demonstrate that the hybrid configuration (Prophet + NLP, C6) consistently outperforms purely statistical models, achieving an AUC-ROC of 0.677 at H+1 and reducing the Brier Score by 10.9% relative to the baseline temporal model (0.220 vs. 0.247), maintaining a statistically significant error reduction across all evaluated horizons ($p < 0.02$). This research validates that the integration of semantic news signals allows for the detection of social tension peaks not captured by historical inertia, providing a technical tool for risk management in critical transport corridors.

</details>


### [181] [QLPO: Quadrant-weighted Sampling for Length-aware Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21793)
*Siwei Chen,Siqi Chen,Xupeng Miao,Bin Cui*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: QLPO is a resampling-based variant of GRPO that achieves implicit length control in large reasoning models by over-generating responses and resampling training groups to favor short correct and long incorrect responses, reducing response length by 30-70% while preserving reasoning performance across 1.5B to 32B models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large reasoning models develop long chain-of-thought responses during RL, causing high inference latency and deployment cost. Existing length control methods rely on explicit penalties or additional modules that require careful tuning and may harm reasoning quality.

Method: QLPO over-generates candidate responses, then resamples the training group by preserving the empirical correct/incorrect ratio while selecting short correct responses and long incorrect responses, reshaping the training distribution for implicit length control without reward function modification.

Result: Across models from 1.5B to 32B parameters (including base and strong reasoning models), QLPO consistently improves the accuracy-length trade-off, reducing response length by 30% to 70% while maintaining reasoning performance.

Conclusion: Structured resampling provides an effective and robust approach to efficient reasoning, enabling significant length reduction without compromising quality.

Abstract: Recent large reasoning models often develop long chain-of-thought responses during reinforcement learning (RL), resulting in high inference latency and deployment cost. Existing methods for response length control typically rely on explicit length penalties or additional control modules, which require careful tuning and may compromise reasoning quality. We propose Quadrant-weighted Sampling for Length-aware Policy Optimization (QLPO), a simple resampling-based variant of GRPO that introduces implicit length control without modifying the reward function. QLPO first over-generates candidate responses and then resamples the training group by preserving the empirical correct/incorrect ratio while favoring short correct responses and long incorrect responses. This reshapes the training distribution and implicitly encourages shorter model outputs. Across models ranging from 1.5B to 32B parameters, including both base models and strong reasoning models, QLPO consistently improves the accuracy-length trade-off. It reduces response length by 30% to 70% while preserving reasoning performance. These results suggest that structured resampling provides an effective and robust approach to efficient reasoning.

</details>


### [182] [DAGForge: Auditable Causal DAG Authoring with Biomedical Literature](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21859)
*Yi-han Sheu,Michael R. Steigman,Yu Zhou,Bo Wang,Fan-Yu Yen,Jordan W. Smoller*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DAGForge is a browser-based system that uses LLMs and evidence-linked reasoning to construct causal DAGs from free-text study descriptions, producing auditable graphs with provenance, confidence estimates, and constraint checking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Constructing causal DAGs is essential for biomedical causal analysis but remains a largely manual, labor-intensive process lacking systematic audit trails and evidence provenance for expert review.

Method: DAGForge combines: (1) a reproducible literature snapshot from free-text descriptions, (2) an LLM-based reasoning module generating structured pairwise causal judgments grounded in verbatim evidence excerpts, and (3) a constraint-checked graph assembly with confidence estimates, provenance, and reviewable rationales.

Result: Evaluated against compact benchmark DAGs and reference DAGs from published literature, DAGForge achieves high edge recall on the literature-based cohort while retaining verifiable evidence trails absent from LLM-only baselines.

Conclusion: DAGForge reduces the burden of causal DAG curation while making resulting assumptions auditable, supporting design, analysis, and interpretation of biomedical studies.

Abstract: Constructing causal directed acyclic graphs (DAGs) is a core step in biomedical causal analysis, yet it remains a largely manual process. Analysts must connect study variables to prior literature, evaluate uncertain causal claims, and preserve sufficient provenance for expert review. We present DAGForge, a browser-based system for authoring causal DAGs as auditable, evidence-linked artifacts. Given free-text descriptions of study concepts, DAGForge creates a reproducible literature snapshot, uses an LLM-based reasoning module to generate structured pairwise causal judgments grounded in verbatim evidence excerpts, and assembles those judgments into a constraint-checked graph. Each proposed edge includes confidence estimates, provenance, and a reviewable rationale. The interface supports study specification, progress monitoring, evidence review, graph comparison, adjustment-set computation, and export. In evaluations against both compact benchmark DAGs and reference DAGs derived from published literature, DAGForge achieves high edge recall on the literature-based cohort while retaining verifiable evidence trails absent from LLM-only baselines. DAGForge thus reduces the burden of causal DAG curation while making the resulting assumptions auditable, supporting the design, analysis, and interpretation of biomedical studies.

</details>


### [183] [When Is a Learned Command Adapter Worth It? Closed-Loop Identification and Counterfactual Auditing of Frozen Locomotion Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21867)
*Zongtan Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces an adapter necessity audit to determine whether adding a learned adapter to a frozen locomotion policy is worthwhile, evaluating gains across multiple metrics. Results show minimal recoverable gains (e.g., 5.2% same-state headroom but only 0.55% recovered allocation gain on Go2), leading to NO-GO or ABSTAIN decisions for most query distributions, demonstrating that adapter value should be empirically tested rather than assumed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adding adapters to frozen locomotion policies is common, but their value may be illusory if deployment-time observations cannot recover the gains seen in controlled settings. A systematic necessity audit is needed to separate real, recoverable improvements from artifacts.

Method: The audit decomposes gains into global operating-point gain, same-state counterfactual headroom, deployment gain over a cross-fitted fixed action, and state-allocation gain over a frequency-matched randomized policy. Source-cluster learner refits map these to a GO/NO-GO/ABSTAIN decision. Closed-loop command-response identification optionally provides features. Evaluations use 200 full learner refits on 20 independent clusters per query distribution (direct, VGCC, MPC).

Result: On Go2, scale-prefix diagnostic finds 5.2% same-state headroom but only 0.55% recovered allocation gain. At 1% deployment/allocation thresholds and 5% violation tolerance: direct queries → NO-GO, VGCC and MPC → ABSTAIN. VGCC has largest mean deployment gain (1.34%) but allocation lower bound 0.09% and violation upper bound 6.25%. H1 audit returns NO-GO; learner-level synthetic control returns GO.

Conclusion: The audit tests whether observable deployment-time signal justifies state-dependent adaptation, rather than presuming adapter value. Most settings fail to demonstrate sufficient recoverable gain, suggesting cautious deployment decisions are warranted.

Abstract: Adding a learned adapter to a frozen, command-conditioned locomotion policy is worthwhile only if the interface exposes improvements that are both real and recoverable from deployment-time observations. We introduce an adapter necessity audit that separates global operating-point gain,same-state counterfactual headroom, deployment gain over a cross-fitted fixed action, and state-allocation gain over a frequency-matched randomized policy. Source-cluster learner refits map these quantities and constraint violations to a GO/NO-GO/ABSTAIN decision. Closed-loop command- response identification provides optional decision features. On Go2, an archived scale-prefix diagnostic finds 5.2% same-state headroom but only 0.55% recovered allocation gain. Our confirmatory audit evaluates direct, scale, heading, and yaw interventions on twenty independent clusters for each of three query distributions induced by direct control, VGCC, and MPC, using 200 full learner refits. At 1% deployment and allocation thresholds and a 5% violation tolerance, direct queries return NO-GO, while VGCC and MPC queries ABSTAIN. VGCC has the largest mean deployment gain (1.34%), but its allocation lower bound is 0.09% and its violation upper bound is 6.25%. A deployment-representative twenty-cluster H1 audit also returns NO-GO, whereas a learner-level synthetic control returns GO. The audit therefore tests whether observable signal justifies state-dependent adaptation rather than presuming that an adapter is valuable.

</details>


### [184] [Multi-Agent System-driven Digital Twins for predictive maintenance: architectures, technologies and open research challenges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21873)
*Korota Arsène Coulibaly,Mohamed Hamlich*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This systematic review of 547+ papers examines the integration of Multi-Agent Systems (MAS) and Digital Twins (DT) for predictive maintenance in Industry 4.0/5.0. It proposes a taxonomy of hybrid architectures, identifies key bottlenecks, and formulates three open research questions regarding AI deployment on resource-constrained microcontrollers, distributed multi-node coordination via lightweight protocols, and hierarchical DT orchestration for smart factory control with residual life estimation and explainable AI. The analysis concludes that no current system provides an integrated embedded-distributed-hierarchical solution meeting Industry 5.0 requirements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Digital Twins are foundational for Industry 4.0 but suffer from growing complexity in distributed environments. Intelligent architectures enabling autonomous decision-making, dynamic adaptability, and inter-agent coordination are needed but lacking.

Method: Systematic review of 547+ papers from high-impact journals (IEEE Transactions, Nature, Elsevier, MDPI), with critical analysis establishing a taxonomy of existing hybrid MAS-DT architectures and identifying technological bottlenecks.

Result: No existing system provides an integrated embedded-distributed-hierarchical solution simultaneously meeting Industry 5.0 requirements. Three open research questions are identified concerning: (i) AI on resource-constrained microcontrollers, (ii) distributed multi-node coordination via lightweight communication protocols, and (iii) hierarchical DT orchestration with residual life estimation and explainable AI.

Conclusion: Significant progress has been made in MAS-DT integration for predictive maintenance, but a unified embedded-distributed-hierarchical architecture remains an open challenge for Industry 5.0.

Abstract: Digital twins have emerged as a foundational technology within the context of Industry 4.0, offering a paradigm for the real-time virtual representation of physical systems. However, managing their growing complexity, particularly in distributed industrial environments, requires intelligent architectures capable of autonomous decision-making, dynamic adaptability, and inter-agent coordination. This systematic review explores the intersection between Multi-Agent Systems and Digital Twins, with a particular focus on predictive maintenance applications in resource-constrained contexts. Through a critical analysis of over 547 papers published in high-impact journals (IEEE Transactions, Nature, Elsevier, MDPI), we establish a taxonomy of existing hybrid architectures, identify persistent technological bottlenecks, and formulate three open research questions concerning: (i) the deployment of artificial intelligence on resource-constrained microcontrollers, (ii) distributed multi-node coordination via lightweight communication protocols, and (iii) the hierarchical orchestration of Digital Twins toward smart factory control integrating residual life estimation and explainable Artificial Intelligence. The results of this analysis reveal that, despite significant progress, no existing system offers an integrated embedded-distributed hierarchical solution that simultaneously meets the requirements of Industry 5.0.

</details>


### [185] [TRW: TRACE-RealWorld---An Auditable Consistency Contract for World Models as Materialized Views](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21910)
*Edward Y. Chang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TRACE-RealWorld presents a data-management framework for maintaining actionable materialized views over a continuously changing physical world, addressing challenges of priced, delayed, heterogeneous, and fallible base-state reads through commitment-level validity, adaptive view maintenance, dependency-scoped compensation, and append-only provenance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To solve the core problem of maintaining accurate and actionable materialized views over a dynamic physical world where sensing (data acquisition) is costly, delayed, inconsistent in quality, and unreliable.

Method: Commitment-level validity abstraction for predictions; consequence-conditioned adaptive view maintenance; transaction-style dependency-scoped compensation for invalidated commitments; append-only provenance for exact replay.

Result: Evaluated via Flood-SAR end-to-end system treating sensing as physical data acquisition, measuring freshness, verification cost, stale reads, recovery scope, restoration failure, and replayability through six pre-registered questions with held-out seeds.

Conclusion: The contribution is a consistency, recovery, and accountability contract for deploying learned world representations as operational data systems, not a new predictive model.

Abstract: TRACE-RealWorld addresses a core data-management problem: maintaining an actionable materialized view over a continuously changing physical world when reads of the base state are priced, delayed, heterogeneous, and fallible. Its data-management contributions are a commitment-level validity abstraction for materialized predictions; consequence-conditioned adaptive view maintenance; transaction-style, dependency-scoped compensation for commitments invalidated after authorization; and append-only provenance supporting exact replay. The work builds directly on materialized-view maintenance, adaptive stream synchronization, transaction recovery, sagas, data freshness, and provenance. The end-to-end Flood-SAR evaluation treats sensing as physical data acquisition and measures freshness, verification cost, stale reads, recovery scope, restoration failure, and replayability through six pre-registered questions with held-out seeds. The contribution is therefore not a new predictive model, but a consistency, recovery, and accountability contract for deploying learned world representations as operational data systems.

</details>


### [186] [Semiotic logical hexagon theory for LLM logical reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21933)
*Yunyao Zhang,Xinglang Zhang,Zeliang Chen,Junqing Yu,Zikai Song*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HexLogicAgent is a framework that organizes semantic structure using logical hexagon theory before reasoning, showing that incomplete semantic representations (not deductive inference) are the main cause of logical reasoning failures in LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs still make mistakes in problems requiring both meaning understanding and logical reasoning because natural-language statements carry implicit semantic relations that, if not properly organized, lead to incorrect conclusions even when reasoning appears valid. Existing methods overlook the semantic structure on which reasoning depends.

Method: HexLogicAgent: a framework that first organizes the meaning of natural-language statements via logical hexagon theory (modeling complete structure of semantic opposition), then guides logical reasoning through structured verification.

Result: Experiments on challenging logical reasoning benchmarks show consistent improvement in reasoning reliability across multiple LLMs. Explicitly modeling complete semantic opposition delays degradation of reasoning performance as logical complexity increases.

Conclusion: Incomplete semantic representations are a major source of logical reasoning failures in LLMs, not deductive inference itself. A complete structure of opposing meanings (logical hexagon) is necessary for reliable reasoning.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have become powerful tools for language understanding and logical reasoning. However, they still make mistakes when a problem requires both understanding meaning and following logic. A key reason is that natural-language statements often carry implicit semantic relations before any formal reasoning begins. If these hidden meanings are not properly organized, the model may reach incorrect conclusions even when the subsequent reasoning process appears logically valid. Existing methods improve reasoning through decomposition, symbolic translation, external solvers, or self-verification, but pay comparatively less attention to the semantic structure on which reasoning depends. In this paper, we further investigate how semantic organization influences logical reasoning in LLMs. To this end, we propose HexLogicAgent, a framework that first organizes the meaning of natural-language statements and then guides logical reasoning through structured verification. In our investigation, we also make two observations. First, incomplete semantic representations, rather than deductive inference itself, are a major source of logical reasoning failures in LLMs. Second, explicitly modeling the complete structure of semantic opposition substantially delays the degradation of reasoning performance as logical complexity increases. Experiments on challenging logical reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that HexLogicAgent consistently improves reasoning reliability across multiple LLMs. The core idea is supported by a logical hexagon theory, which explains why a complete structure of opposing meanings is necessary for reliable reasoning.

</details>


### [187] [Learning as Reasoning Unfolds: Progressive Rollout Allocation for Efficient Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22002)
*Heyang Jiang,Henry Liu,Baharan Mirzasoleiman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VIGOR is a variance-guided rollout allocation method for RLVR that dynamically assigns more rollouts to high-variance examples, achieving up to 2.3× rollout reduction in math tasks and 1.49× in coding compared to GRPO, with theoretical guarantees on speedup.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GRPO is computationally expensive and unstable due to generating many uninformative rollouts with fixed per-example budgets; existing filtering methods offer modest gains but slow down training.

Method: VIGOR begins with a small fixed number of rollouts per example in a batch, then iteratively allocates additional rollouts to examples with the highest group reward variance until a total rollout budget is reached.

Result: VIGOR reaches target accuracy with up to 2.3× fewer rollouts on math tasks, reaches GRPO's final coding full pass rate with 1.49× fewer rollouts, and improves coding average test pass rate by 3.4 points.

Conclusion: Variance-guided dynamic rollout allocation significantly improves the efficiency and stability of RLVR for LLM reasoning, with provable speedup ratios over fixed-budget GRPO under Pareto-distributed reward variance.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has emerged as a highly effective framework for improving LLM reasoning, with methods such as GRPO among its most successful instantiations. However, GRPO relies on repeated generation of long chain-of-thought rollouts. Training time scales with the number of rollouts, a large fraction of which are uninformative. Thus, GRPO is computationally expensive and unstable. To mitigate this, existing approaches either generate a larger pool of rollouts and filter the most informative prompts, or leverage historical signals for filtering at later stages of training. These strategies offer modest performance gains, but slow down the overall process. To address this, we propose VarIance Guided Online Rollout allocation (VIGOR) which instead of allocating a fixed rollout budget per example, begins with a small number of rollouts for all examples in a batch and iteratively allocates additional rollouts to those with the highest group reward variance until a fixed total rollout budget is reached. Theoretically, we show that under RLVR, reward variance controls the gradient magnitude, and derive VIGOR's closed-form speedup ratio over GRPO, which grows with refinement rounds under Pareto-distributed reward variance. Experiments on mathematical reasoning and coding tasks show that VIGOR reaches target accuracy with up to 2.3$\times$ fewer rollouts on math, reaches GRPO's final coding full pass rate with 1.49$\times$ fewer rollouts, and improves the coding average test pass rate by 3.4 points.

</details>


### [188] [Zero-Shot Mission-Level Evaluation for Aerial MLLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22014)
*Suman Navaratnarajah,Taehyoung Kim,Jona Ruthardt,Ishaan Bhimwal,Ryousuke Yamada,Yannik Blei,Wolfram Burgard,Yuki M Asano*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MissionBench is a benchmark evaluating 22 MLLMs on 120 long-horizon embodied missions in aerial 3D environments. The best model achieves <35% success vs. 84.4% human performance, showing scaling improves zero-shot capabilities but multi-step planning and adaptive reasoning remain major challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To assess how well general-purpose Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) can solve long-horizon embodied tasks from a single high-level instruction, without domain-specific fine-tuning, as they become core reasoning modules for embodied agents.

Method: Introduced MissionBench, a benchmark with 120 missions across five simulated 3D environments and four task families. Agents use egocentric observations and action history, performing autonomous planning, navigation, and reporting without aerial-specific fine-tuning. Evaluated 22 open- and closed-source MLLMs.

Result: The strongest MLLM succeeded on <35% of missions vs. 84.4% human performance. Scaling improves zero-shot embodied capabilities, but large variations exist across model families. Success requires coordinating spatial perception, multi-step planning, and adaptive reasoning.

Conclusion: Mission-level embodied competence remains a significant challenge for current MLLMs, requiring closed-loop evaluation. Scaling-driven improvements show promise but also risk if evaluated only on isolated sub-tasks, motivating comprehensive benchmarks like MissionBench.

Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) are emerging as core reasoning modules for embodied agents, yet it remains unclear how well general-purpose models can solve long-horizon embodied tasks from a single high-level instruction. We introduce MissionBench, a benchmark for mission-level evaluation of MLLMs in aerial 3D environments. It comprises 120 missions across five simulated 3D environments and four task families. Agents must autonomously plan, navigate, and report outcomes using only egocentric observations and its action history, without aerial-specific fine-tuning. Across 22 open- and closed-source MLLMs, the strongest model succeeds on fewer than 35% of missions compared to 84.4% human performance, highlighting the difficulty of multi-step embodied tasks. Despite large variations between model families, we observe gains from scaling, indicating that larger general-purpose models possess stronger zero-shot embodied capabilities. Our analysis shows that mission-level competence requires coordinating multiple capabilities beyond spatial perception, including multi-step planning and adaptive reasoning. This motivates closed-loop evaluation and highlights both the promise and risk of scaling-driven improvements for embodied AI.

</details>


### [189] [Nanbeige4.2-3B: Unlocking Agentic Capabilities in a Compact Mode](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22083)
*Nanbeige Lab,:,Chen Yang,Chengrui Huang,Fufeng Lan,Hanhui Chen,Hao Zhou,Huatong Song,Jiaqi Cao,Jiaying Zhu,Jinlin Niu,Kai Wang,Lisheng Huang,Qiliang Liang,Ran Le,Ruixiang Feng,Shuang Sun,Tao Gu,Tao Zhang,Tianyu Luo,Yang Song,Yun Xing,Yuntao Wen,Ziyao Xu,Zongchao Chen,Zongqiang Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Nanbeige4.2-3B is a compact 3B-parameter agentic model pretrained from scratch on 28T tokens using a Looped Transformer. It excels in code-agent, office-agent, and tool-use tasks while maintaining strong reasoning. It outperforms larger models like Qwen3.5-9B and Gemma4-12B on agentic benchmarks via mixed-mode RLHF, length-controlled reasoning RL, and agentic RL with outcome/process rewards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To build a compact general agentic model (3B parameters) that achieves strong performance across diverse agentic and reasoning tasks, outperforming larger models while being suitable for local deployment.

Method: Pretrained from scratch on 28T tokens using a Looped Transformer (reuses layer stack to increase capacity without adding parameters). Uses diverse SFT data and trajectory construction via real-world deployment and large-scale synthesis. RL pipeline includes: mixed-mode RLHF over Think/Non-Think responses, length-controlled reasoning RL for accuracy-efficiency balance, and agentic RL with outcome and process rewards for long-horizon training stability.

Result: Outperforms larger models (Qwen3.5-9B, Gemma4-12B) on diverse agentic benchmarks, remains competitive on reasoning and alignment tasks. Supports use as a compact local personal assistant with OpenClaw.

Conclusion: A compact 3B agentic model can outperform much larger models through careful pretraining (Looped Transformer), diverse data construction, and a multi-stage RL pipeline combining reasoning, alignment, and agentic reward signals.

Abstract: We present Nanbeige4.2-3B, a compact general agentic model with 3B non-embedding parameters. It delivers strong performance across code-agent, office-agent, and complex tool-use tasks while maintaining highly competitive reasoning capabilities in mathematics, coding, and science. Nanbeige4.2-3B is pretrained from scratch on 28T tokens with a Looped Transformer that reuses the layer stack to increase capacity without adding parameters. For SFT data and trajectory construction, we expand the diversity of executable environments, task assets, and agentic scaffolds through real-world deployment and large-scale synthesis. Our RL pipeline applies mixed-mode RLHF over Think and Non-Think responses to improve overall model quality and reduce failure cases, length-controlled reasoning RL to balance accuracy and reasoning efficiency, and agentic RL with outcome and process rewards to stabilize long-horizon training. Extensive evaluations show that Nanbeige4.2-3B outperforms larger models, including Qwen3.5-9B and Gemma4-12B, across diverse agentic benchmarks while remaining competitive on reasoning and alignment tasks. Performance with OpenClaw further supports its use as a compact local personal assistant.

</details>


### [190] [Reasoning Denoiser: Denoising Reasoning Traces for Hallucination Detection in Large Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22098)
*Junlin Fang,Do Nguyen-Thanh,Xiaogang Xu,Zhen Fang,Sean Du*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: REDE is a learning framework that denoises reasoning traces from large reasoning models by leveraging final-answer attention to filter out irrelevant and repetitive steps, improving hallucination detection performance across multiple benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long reasoning traces from LRMs contain useful signals for hallucination detection, but they are often contaminated by noisy steps (irrelevant and repetitive steps) that obscure truthfulness assessment. Existing confidence-based scores and naive embedding-based filtering fail to reliably separate noisy from informative steps.

Method: REDE (Retrieval-based Denoising) leverages final-answer attention as an automatic supervision signal to shape step-level representation space, producing refined embeddings that reliably identify and filter noisy steps. It operates on filtered reasoning trajectories after removing noisy steps and can be plugged into diverse hallucination detectors.

Result: Extensive experiments on multiple reasoning benchmarks show that REDE consistently improves detection performance over competitive baselines.

Conclusion: REDE effectively addresses the challenge of reasoning noise in LRMs by providing a learnable denoising mechanism that enhances hallucination detection, demonstrating consistent improvements over existing methods.

Abstract: Large reasoning models (LRMs) generate long reasoning traces before producing final answers. While these traces may contain useful signals for hallucination detection, harnessing them is non-trivial because long trajectories often include noisy steps that obscure the cues relevant to truthfulness assessment. In this paper, we identify two prevalent forms of reasoning noises, i.e., irrelevant steps and repetitive steps, and show that both substantially degrade hallucination detection performance. Existing confidence-based scores and naive embedding-based filtering fail to reliably separate noisy from informative steps. To address this challenge, we propose REDE, a novel learning framework for denoising reasoning traces for hallucination detection. Specifically, REDE leverages final-answer attention as an automatic supervision signal to shape the step-level representation space, yielding refined embeddings in which noisy steps can be reliably identified and filtered. REDE can be readily plugged into diverse hallucination detectors by operating on the filtered reasoning trajectory after removing noisy steps. Extensive experiments on multiple reasoning benchmarks show that REDE consistently improves detection performance over competitive baselines.

</details>


### [191] [Industrial Tokenization for LLM-Based Health Intelligence: A Federated Architecture for Industrial Evidence Integration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22153)
*Deshui Li,Xiao-Ming Yuan,Zishun Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces Industrial Tokenization, a conceptual interface that transforms heterogeneous industrial diagnostic outputs into structured, machine-interpretable "Industrial Tokens" containing source, temporal scope, context, analytical meaning, confidence, and provenance. A federated architecture is proposed where autonomous subsystems expose these tokens to a central reasoning layer, with an initial DiagnosisToken pathway using vibration diagnostics and LLM-based interpretation demonstrated as proof of concept.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Industrial health management relies on heterogeneous data sources (condition monitoring, SCADA, maintenance records, prognostics) that differ in structure, resolution, physical meaning, and reliability. Directly integrating them into monolithic LLMs reduces interpretability, traceability, and adaptability. A standardized semantic interface is needed.

Method: Industrial Tokenization: transform source-specific analytical outputs into structured units (Industrial Tokens) with domain-grounded evidence, source, temporal scope, operating context, analytical meaning, confidence, and provenance. A federated architecture where heterogeneous analytical subsystems retain autonomy while exposing standardized tokens to a central LLM/agent reasoning layer. Initial implementation: end-to-end DiagnosisToken pathway via vibration diagnostics, rule-based event aggregation, structured textual token generation, and LLM-based interpretation.

Result: An initial DiagnosisToken pathway demonstrated as proof of concept. Other token types (SCADA condition-monitoring, maintenance, prognostic) are reserved as future extensions. The framework serves as a semantic interface rather than another raw data encoding method.

Conclusion: Industrial Tokenization provides a standardized semantic interface between domain-specific industrial intelligence and LLM/agent-based reasoning, preserving subsystem autonomy while enabling cross-source reasoning with improved interpretability, traceability, and adaptability to equipment/data changes.

Abstract: Industrial health management increasingly relies on heterogeneous information sources, including condition monitoring systems, supervisory control and data acquisition systems, maintenance records, inspection results, and prognostic models. Although large language models provide new opportunities for cross-source reasoning, industrial data and analytical outputs differ substantially in structure, temporal resolution, physical meaning, and reliability. Directly integrating such heterogeneous information into a monolithic model may reduce interpretability, traceability, and adaptability to equipment and data changes. This paper introduces Industrial Tokenization, a conceptual interface for transforming source-specific analytical outputs into structured and machine-interpretable units of industrial evidence, termed Industrial Tokens. Unlike numerical tokens used to encode raw time-series data, Industrial Tokens represent domain-grounded evidence together with source, temporal scope, operating context, analytical meaning, quality or confidence information, and provenance. Based on this concept, a federated industrial architecture is proposed, where heterogeneous analytical subsystems retain autonomy while exposing standardized Industrial Tokens to a central reasoning layer. As an initial implementation, this study presents an end-to-end DiagnosisToken pathway based on vibration-diagnostic outputs, rule-based event aggregation, structured textual token generation, and LLM-based interpretation. Other Industrial Tokens, including SCADA-based condition-monitoring tokens, maintenance tokens, and prognostic tokens, are reserved as future extensions. The proposed framework positions Industrial Tokenization as a semantic interface between domain-specific industrial intelligence and LLM- or agent-based reasoning, rather than another method for encoding raw industrial data.

</details>


### [192] [Learning on the Job: Continual Learning from Deployment Feedback for Frozen-Weights Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22157)
*Valentin Tablan,Scott Taylor,Kristoffer Bernhem*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that frozen AI agents can continually learn from operational feedback (outcome verdicts and corrections) by using an external memory that distills episodes into retrievable natural-language rules, achieving 1.6x (verdicts) to 2.6x (corrections) improvement over static RAG on τ-bench banking tasks, with transferable memory across models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI agents discard nearly all learning opportunities because their underlying models are frozen at deployment, causing them to start from zero on recurring tasks despite receiving feedback through outcome verdicts and after-the-fact corrections during ordinary operation.

Method: Pair a frozen model with an external memory that distills each episode into retrievable natural-language rules; use operational feedback (one-bit outcome verdicts or corrections) as the learning signal; evaluated on τ-bench banking domain against a static-RAG baseline.

Result: Learning from one-bit outcome verdicts achieves 1.6x baseline success; learning from corrections achieves 2.6x baseline; converts 22 of 84 tasks the baseline never solves; results hold on both Mistral Large (open-weights) and Claude Sonnet 5 (frontier model); memory transfers between models.

Conclusion: Operational feedback is sufficient for continual learning when paired with an external natural-language rule memory, spanning the deployment spectrum and enabling cross-model memory transfer.

Abstract: AI agents encounter learning opportunities in every episode they run, and discard nearly all of them: the underlying models are frozen at deployment, so an agent that resolves a difficult request today starts from zero when it recurs tomorrow. Yet ordinary operation already produces feedback, in the form of outcome verdicts and after-the-fact corrections. We show that this feedback is a sufficient signal for continual learning when the frozen model is paired with an external memory that distils each episode into retrievable natural-language rules. On the banking domain of $τ$-bench, against a static-RAG control retrieving over the complete policy corpus, learning from the one-bit outcome verdict lifts single-trial success to 1.6$\times$ the baseline, and learning from corrections to 2.6$\times$, converting 22 of the 84 tasks the baseline never solves. The result spans the deployment spectrum, measured on Mistral Large, an open-weights model that organisations with data sovereignty requirements can self-host, and replicated on a frontier model, Claude Sonnet 5. The accumulated memory also transfers: each model, reading the store built by the other, rises above its own no-memory baseline. The harness, protocol, and data are released.

</details>


### [193] [Deconstructing Off-Policy Ratios: Entropy-Scaled Trust Regions for Asynchronous Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22186)
*Guanqun Zhao,Zijun Xie,Binbin Zheng,Enlei Gong,Jiafeng Lu,Yehan Yang,Aoqi Hu,Zeyu Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Paper identifies that importance ratio variability depends on token entropy in asynchronous RL for LLM post-training, and proposes Entropy-Scaled Trust Region (ESTR) to adjust off-policy correction per-token based on local entropy, outperforming existing asynchronous methods and achieving 2.6x speedup over synchronous GRPO with comparable accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Asynchronous RL accelerates LLM post-training but suffers from policy collapse due to stale, off-policy data. Existing methods apply uniform importance ratio thresholds across token positions, failing to account for the fact that the natural scale of importance ratios varies systematically with token entropy, leading to either amplified noise or suppressed exploration.

Method: Proposes Entropy-Scaled Trust Region (ESTR), which scales each token's off-policy deviation by its local entropy. This avoids magnitude-only correction that admits amplified noise at low entropy and masks exploration at high entropy. ESTR requires no auxiliary forward passes or explicit version-switch detection.

Result: Consistently outperforms existing asynchronous methods across long-horizon agentic tasks and mathematical reasoning benchmarks. Achieves best train-inference consistency. Compared with synchronous GRPO, ESTR attains comparable accuracy while improving training speed by 2.6x.

Conclusion: Entropy-scaled trust region correction is an effective solution for stabilizing asynchronous RL-based LLM post-training, addressing the fundamental limitation of magnitude-only off-policy correction methods.

Abstract: Asynchronous reinforcement learning (RL) accelerates large language model (LLM) post-training by overlapping rollout generation with policy optimization, but the resulting stale, off-policy data can destabilize optimization and ultimately cause policy collapse. Existing methods typically retain or discard tokens based solely on the magnitude of their importance ratios, applying the same threshold uniformly across token positions. In this work, we reveal that the natural scale of the importance ratio varies systematically with token entropy. Under asynchronous dynamics, this entropy-ratio scaling dictates two distinct phenomena: at low entropy, the inherent train-inference discrepancy is drastically amplified into substantial sampling noise; at high entropy, in-flight weight updates naturally induce pronounced, legitimate exploratory deviations. Consequently, magnitude-only correction inadvertently admits the amplified noise while strictly masking out the essential exploration triggered by in-flight updates. To address this, we propose the Entropy-Scaled Trust Region (ESTR), which scales each token's off-policy deviation by its local entropy, requiring no auxiliary forward passes or explicit version-switch detection. Across long-horizon agentic tasks and mathematical reasoning benchmarks, ESTR consistently outperforms existing asynchronous methods and achieves the best train-inference consistency. Compared with synchronous GRPO, ESTR attains comparable accuracy while improving training speed by $2.6\times$.

</details>


### [194] [AI4PLE: A Methodology for Integrating AI into Product Line Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22260)
*Bedir Tekinerdogan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a methodological framework for systematically integrating AI into Product Line Engineering (PLE) and evaluates it through industrial multi-case studies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite the promises of reuse-based development and PLE, the systematic integration of AI into PLE processes has not yet been comprehensively explored.

Method: The authors propose a methodological framework to support systematic AI integration into PLE and evaluate it through a multi-case study conducted in an industrial context.

Result: The framework's effectiveness was evaluated through industrial multi-case studies, demonstrating practical applicability.

Conclusion: The proposed framework enables systematic integration of AI into PLE, addressing key challenges in feature selection, variability management, and configuration optimization.

Abstract: Reuse-based development has become increasingly important in the creation of complex systems, offering significant opportunities to reduce costs, improve quality, and accelerate time-to-market. Product Line Engineering (PLE) provides a systematic approach to realizing this potential by enabling the efficient creation, management, and customization of product families by reusing shared assets and capabilities. PLE involves addressing numerous complex decisions, including feature selection, variability management, and configuration optimization, which are critical to the success of a product line. Despite its promise, the systematic integration of Artificial Intelligence (AI) into PLE processes has not yet been comprehensively explored. In this paper, we propose a methodological framework to support the systematic integration of AI into PLE and evaluate its effectiveness through a multi-case study conducted in an industrial context.

</details>


### [195] [A Roadmap to Impactful Pluralistic Alignment Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22305)
*Elinor Poole-Dayan,Jillian Fisher,Atoosa Kasirzadeh,Jacob Andreas,Mitchell Gordon,Michiel A. Bakker*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Despite the active research agenda on pluralistic value alignment, there is no evidence it has been adopted in real-world AI systems. The authors audit frontier labs and find no mention of pluralism as a goal, arguing the research community must shift toward empirical justification, concrete definitions of pluralistic behavior, and production-ready methods and evaluations to drive adoption.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The gap between the normative/speculative justifications for pluralistic alignment and the lack of adoption in deployed AI systems motivates this paper. The authors aim to highlight this disconnect and push the research community toward impact-driven work.

Method: The authors audit the public behavior documents and evaluations of frontier AI labs to check for evidence of pluralistic alignment goals or practices. They identify and analyze three main reasons for the adoption gap and propose corresponding future research directions.

Result: No frontier lab names pluralism as a goal; there is no clear indication that production models are explicitly trained or tested for pluralistic alignment. Three core problems are identified: (1) lack of empirical evidence for benefits, (2) unsettled definitions of ideal pluralistic behavior, and (3) unmeasured trade-offs against other LLM desiderata with non-hill-climbable metrics.

Conclusion: The pluralistic alignment research community needs to move from normative justification to empirical foundations, establish a concrete operationalizable goal for developers, and develop trade-off-aware evaluations and methods suitable for production systems. This is a call to action for adoption-focused research.

Abstract: Pluralistic value alignment---the goal of building AI systems that represent and serve diverse human values and perspectives---has emerged as an active research agenda. Yet, there's no public evidence that it has shaped the training or evaluation of the AI systems people actually use. We audit the public behavior documents and evaluations of frontier labs, finding none name pluralism as a goal, and as of this writing, no clear indication that production models are explicitly trained or tested for it. This goes against the primary motivations and goals of pluralistic alignment, which revolve around making a positive difference in the models serving billions of users worldwide. We argue that the pluralistic alignment research community should focus on supporting impact and adoption in deployed, widely-used AI systems. We provide evidence for the adoption problem, present three main reasons behind it, and discuss three corresponding areas for future research to address it: 1. The primary justifications for pluralistic alignment so far have been normative or speculative. We need studies showing empirically how pluralistic AI benefits users or society. 2. The pluralistic alignment research community has not settled when pluralistic behavior is warranted or what pluralism ideally looks like in practice. We need to establish a concrete goal for developers to operationalize. 3. Current methods trade off against other desiderata of LLMs in ways that are largely unmeasured, and existing metrics are not "hill-climbable." We need trade-off-aware evaluations and methods that meet the requirements of production systems. This paper serves as a collective call to action for the pluralistic alignment researchers: progress requires moving beyond normative justification toward empirical foundations, a concrete account of ideal pluralistic behavior, and practical methods and evaluations built for adoption.

</details>


### [196] [Learning Structural Convergence: A Neuro-Symbolic Benchmark for Temporal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22365)
*Michael Romei De Socio,Gian Luca Pozzato,Alessio Merlo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TRACTA is a controlled synthetic benchmark for temporal structural reasoning in high-complexity event-driven systems, showing that semantically grounded trajectories outperform raw-event models, especially on temporal tasks, with complementary contributions from capability dynamics, contextual impacts, and temporal structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-complexity operational environments require methods that detect and anticipate temporally distributed patterns rather than classify isolated events, motivating the need for benchmarks and approaches that support temporal structural reasoning.

Method: The paper introduces TRACTA, a synthetic benchmark with three tasks (early_warning, pattern_detection, run_classification), and compares raw-event neural models, a contract-lite semantic baseline, and a neuro-symbolic configuration operating on semantically grounded trajectories using ablation and shortcut diagnostics.

Result: Neuro-symbolic models using semantically grounded trajectories achieve the highest aggregate point estimates, with largest margins on temporal tasks; ablation shows complementary contributions from capability dynamics, contextual impacts, and temporal structure; the primary neural input controls global-identifier shortcuts, though residual shallow signals remain.

Conclusion: In controlled synthetic settings, semantically grounded trajectories provide an effective representation for temporal structural reasoning, supporting further investigation of semantic interfaces between event data, structured representations, and temporal learning.

Abstract: High-complexity operational environments require methods that detect and anticipate temporally distributed patterns rather than classify isolated events. This paper introduces TRACTA (Temporal Reasoning and Capability-Trajectory Analysis), a controlled synthetic benchmark for temporal structural reasoning in high-complexity event-driven systems, instantiated through Multi-Domain Operations (MDO)-like scenarios. The benchmark includes three tasks: early_warning, pattern_detection, and run_classification, and compares raw-event neural models, a contract-lite semantic baseline, and a neuro-symbolic configuration operating on semantically grounded trajectories. Results show that raw event-level learning remains informative, but learned temporal modeling over semantic capability and contextual direct-impact trajectories achieves the highest aggregate point estimates, with the largest margins on the temporal tasks. Ablation analysis indicates that capability dynamics, contextual impacts, and temporal structure contribute complementary information. Shortcut diagnostics indicate that the most direct cross-run global-identifier shortcut is controlled in the primary neural input view, while residual shallow signals remain. Overall, the findings support a bounded methodological conclusion: in controlled synthetic settings, semantically grounded trajectories provide an effective representation for temporal structural reasoning, supporting further investigation of semantic interfaces between event data, structured representations, and temporal learning.

</details>


### [197] [Do Agent Benchmarks Measure Capability? Protocol Validity in the Age of Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22368)
*Jiaqi Shao,Hanck Chen,Wei Zhang,Maxm Pan,Bing Luo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces HackDetect, a post-hoc audit method for identifying exposure-based shortcuts and reward hacking in agent benchmarks. Analyzing 2,385 traces across 15 benchmarks, they find 66-67% of traces show evidence of such issues, with score inflation of 0.45-1.00, undermining the validity of capability claims.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agent benchmarks increasingly evaluate complex capabilities, but reward hacking and shortcut behaviors are widespread and no common procedure exists to systematically attribute these shortcuts and quantify their effect across benchmarks, threatening the validity of capability claims.

Method: The authors formulate protocol validity and develop HackDetect, a post-hoc audit that identifies exposures, determines how the agent used them, and assesses whether the resulting score is misleading. They quantify score inflation via the Mislead gap (exploit score minus intended score) and audit 2,385 traces across 15 agent benchmarks.

Result: Evidence of exposures and reward hacking found in 67.0% of Frontier Science traces and 66.7% of AutoLab tasks. Across paired comparisons, score inflation measured 0.45-1.00.

Conclusion: Benchmark reports should provide evidence that scores reflect the intended capability, as current scores are substantially inflated due to shortcuts and reward hacking.

Abstract: Agent benchmarks increasingly evaluate repository editing, web research, terminal use, and long-horizon interaction. Their scores support capability claims only when the evaluation protocol keeps the intended capability necessary for success. Recent reward-hacking benchmarks and system reports show that agents can instead recover public solutions, read evaluation artifacts, infer generator structure, manipulate feedback, or benefit from invalid scoring paths; existing responses do not provide a common procedure for attributing these shortcuts and quantifying their effect across benchmarks. We formulate protocol validity and introduce HackDetect, a post-hoc audit that identifies an exposure, determines how the agent used it, and assesses whether the resulting score is misleading. We quantify score inflation with the Mislead gap, defined as the exploit score minus the intended score. We audit 2,385 traces across 15 agent benchmarks and find evidence of exposures and reward hacking in 67.0% of Frontier Science traces and 66.7% of AutoLab tasks. Across paired comparisons, we measure score inflation of 0.45-1.00, showing that benchmark reports should provide evidence that scores reflect the intended capability.

</details>


### [198] [IDEAgent: Agentic Quality-Diversity Search for Research Idea Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22375)
*Varun Gumma,Navonil Majumder,Soumitra Sinhahajari,Soujanya Poria*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: IDEAgent is a multi-agent framework that treats research ideation as a Quality-Diversity (QD) search, outperforming baselines by 3.89x on the joint Yield metric across 32 CS topics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based scientific discovery systems optimize for either idea Quality or Diversity independently, leading to either clustered or trivial/unsound ideas. The paper argues research ideation requires a conjunction of both objectives.

Method: IDEAgent is a multi-agent framework managing idea evolution through lineages. Quality is driven by multi-objective feedback for repair and refinement; Diversity is achieved via lightweight sequential memory and explicit comparison against completed ideas, ancestors, and rejected proposals. Yield is introduced as a joint metric measuring the largest set of mutually diverse ideas above a quality threshold.

Result: Across 32 topics in 8 CS domains, IDEAgent outperforms the best baseline by 3.89x on Yield and achieves non-zero Yield on 8x more topics. Repair and refinement improve logical rigor, clarity, and non-obviousness.

Conclusion: QD-search-based ideation with joint optimization of Quality and Diversity is effective for automated scientific ideation. The framework and metric are open-sourced to encourage future research.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have significantly automated the process of scientific discovery over the past few years. However, existing systems share one core limitation: they generate and optimize ideas independently for either Quality or Diversity. This often leads to the generation of ideas in close proximity to one another or to a large set of trivial, unsound, or unclear concepts. In this work, we instead argue that research ideation should be treated as a conjunction of both objectives and framed as a Quality-Diversity (QD) search. In line with this perspective, we introduce IDEAgent, a multi-agent framework that manages the evolution of ideas through lineages. We jointly drive Quality using multi-objective feedback for dedicated repair and refinement, while Diversity is achieved through lightweight sequential memory and explicit comparison against completed ideas, their historical ancestors, and rejected proposals. To systematically evaluate this QD conjunction, we develop Yield, a joint metric that computes the largest set of mutually diverse ideas that satisfy a predetermined quality threshold. Finally, through evaluations across 32 topics spanning 8 domains of Computer Science, we show that IDEAgent outperforms the best baseline by 3.89x on Yield, while achieving non-zero Yield on 8x more topics. We further corroborate these findings through an analysis of quality improvements, showing that repair and refinement are crucial for building logical rigor and clarity while preserving non-obviousness. To encourage future research on QD-search-based ideation, we open-source IDEAgent at https://github.com/declare-lab/IDEAgent.

</details>


### [199] [Agentic Root Cause Analysis through Evidence-Grounded Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22385)
*Amaury Wei,Olga Fink*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentRCA is a zero-shot agentic framework for root cause analysis that uses a digital twin and LLM to perform evidence-grounded reasoning without fault-specific training, achieving supervised-level performance on real-world industrial systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing data-driven RCA approaches are limited by black-box operation (no justification) and reliance on scarce labeled fault examples, creating a major operational bottleneck in industrial anomaly diagnosis.

Method: AgentRCA combines a data-driven digital twin (modeling normal system dynamics) with a tool-augmented LLM agent that iteratively gathers statistical evidence, evaluates competing hypotheses, and identifies the most plausible physical fault through inference-time reasoning rather than learning fault-specific mappings.

Result: On a real-world multiphase-flow facility and a large-scale chemical plant, AgentRCA achieved diagnostic performance competitive with fully supervised baselines without any fault-specific training data.

Conclusion: Autonomous hypothesis-driven reasoning serves as a practical foundation for scalable industrial RCA, producing transparent reasoning traces that link symptoms to physical causes.

Abstract: Diagnosing the root cause of anomalies is essential for safe industrial operation. Despite extensive sensor instrumentation, formulating hypotheses and gathering evidence remains a manual process, creating a major operational bottleneck. While existing data-driven approaches aim to automate this, two critical limitations restrict their deployment: their operate as black boxes unable to justify their diagnosis, and they require scarce labeled examples of faulty operation. To address this gap, we introduce AgentRCA, a zero-shot agentic framework for evidence-grounded root cause analysis. Rather than learning fault-specific mappings, AgentRCA performs inference-time reasoning by combining a data-driven digital twin (modeling normal system dynamics) with a tool-augmented large language model. The agent iteratively gathers statistical evidence, evaluates competing hypotheses, and identifies the physical fault that best explains the observed behavior. Evaluated on a real-world multiphase-flow facility and a large-scale chemical plant, AgentRCA achieves diagnostic performance competitive with fully supervised baselines without relying on fault-specific training. Crucially, it produces transparent reasoning traces that explicitly link observed symptoms to their underlying physical causes. These results establish autonomous hypothesis-driven reasoning as a practical foundation for scalable industrial root cause analysis.

</details>


### [200] [SceneActBench: Can Agents Act on the 3D Scenes They See?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22393)
*Yifei Zhao,Xiangxin Zhou,Wenhao Yang,Jiaqi Tang,Pu Jian,Huanjin Yao,Jiarui Yao,Haowei Lin,Chunchao Guo,Zhuo Chen,Wenkai Lyu,Jianzhu Ma,Xueqian Wang,Wenxi Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SceneActBench is a benchmark for evaluating VLM agents on multi-object 3D action tasks, revealing that current models score 38.6-50.2 overall and none perform consistently well across tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing 3D benchmarks only score textual responses or single-object operations, leaving agent action on complete multi-object 3D scenes under-evaluated.

Method: The benchmark includes five 3D tasks under a unified agent-environment loop, built from 210 source instances yielding 520 task cases. Agents act on 3D environments given PNG images or video frames, evaluated against hidden ground truth with task-specific geometric metrics.

Result: Across eleven proprietary VLM configurations, overall scores range from 38.6 to 50.2, with no model performing consistently well across all tasks.

Conclusion: Current VLM agents show limited and uneven performance on multi-object 3D action tasks, highlighting the need for further development in this area.

Abstract: Vision-language model (VLM) agents increasingly use tools to act on 3D scenes rather than only describe them. Existing 3D benchmarks score textual responses or single-object operations, leaving agent action on complete multi-object 3D scenes under evaluated. We present SceneActBench, a benchmark for visually conditioned action across five 3D tasks under a unified agent-environment loop. Given PNG images or sampled video frames and, where applicable, supplied 3D assets, an agent acts on a 3D environment. We evaluate each final output against hidden ground truth with task-specific geometric metrics. SceneActBench comprises five tasks built from 210 source instances, yielding 520 task cases including paired input conditions. Every task runs through one fixed agent loop to keep the comparison fair. Across eleven proprietary VLM configurations, Overall scores span 38.6-50.2, and none performs consistently well across tasks. We further analyse where and how failures manifest.

</details>


### [201] [Dynamic Capability Scoping for Enterprise AI Agents: A Synthetic Dataset and Three-Source Permission Architecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22445)
*Halil Burak Noyan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a dynamic least-privilege architecture for enterprise AI agents that scopes credentials based on task context rather than static role-based assignments, and contributes a synthetic dataset of 600 enterprise task prompts with permission labels to evaluate this approach.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise AI agents are configured with static, over-privileged credential sets that expand the attack surface, as agents hold every tool they might ever need regardless of the specific task. The authors argue that capability scoping should follow a dynamic least-privilege principle as a prevention mechanism rather than detection.

Method: The architecture combines three sources: role-based ceilings, a task-context classifier, and policy-derived combination prohibitions. A synthetic dataset of 600 enterprise task prompts was constructed using a two-pass pipeline separating prompt generation from permission labeling to avoid circularity, validated against a human-reviewed sample (Cohen's κ=0.917 pre-review, 0.967 post-review).

Result: Iterating between dataset and policy reduced ceiling violations from 46 to 3 (93% reduction). Human review achieved high inter-annotator agreement (κ=0.967 post-review). The architecture supports both enforcing and observe-only deployment modes, where the latter produces behavioral signals for misalignment research.

Conclusion: Synthetic prompt generation can drive policy refinement when developed together. The released dataset, environment specification, and pipeline support evaluation of dynamic scoping mechanisms. The key insight is that credentials not present in an agent's context cannot be misused regardless of agent reasoning sophistication.

Abstract: Enterprise AI agents are typically granted static credential sets at configuration time, holding every tool the role might need for every task they perform. This persistent over-privilege expands the attack surface. We argue that capability scoping must follow a dynamic least-privilege principle and be treated as a prevention mechanism before a detection one. A credential that does not exist in an agent's context cannot be misused regardless of the agent's reasoning or evasion sophistication. We outline a three-source architecture instantiating this principle: role-based ceilings, a task-context classifier, and policy-derived combination prohibitions creating a layered proactive defense against LLM agent misalignment and misuse cases. The architecture supports both enforcing and observe-only deployment; the latter records agent permission requests inconsistent with task context, producing a behavioral signal usable in misalignment research.
  As a first step toward evaluating this architecture, we contribute a synthetic dataset of 600 enterprise task prompts grounded in a multi-department company policy, labeled with minimum required permissions across a 15-permission tool-based taxonomy that maps directly to deployable credentials or enforceable guardrails. The dataset is constructed via a two-pass pipeline that separates prompt generation from permission labeling to avoid circularity, and is validated against a 60-record/688 decisions human-reviewed sample (Cohen's $κ= 0.917$ pre-review and $κ= 0.967$ post-review). Iterating between dataset and policy reduced ceiling violations from 46 to 3, a 93% reduction. This shows that synthetic prompt generation can drive policy refinement when the two are developed together. The dataset, environment specification, and generation pipeline are released to support evaluation of dynamic scoping mechanisms.

</details>


### [202] [TRACE-ROUTER: Task-Consistent and Adaptive Online Routing for Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22465)
*Ritik Raj,Souvik Kundu,Sarbartha Banerjee,Dheemanth Joshi,Ishita Vohra,Tushar Krishna*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TRACE-Router is a task-level routing framework that assigns each agentic task to a single LLM using a contextual bandit, leveraging delayed task-level feedback to optimize accuracy-latency trade-offs, outperforming per-call routers and single-model baselines on agentic benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing per-call routers make independent routing decisions for each LLM call, which misaligns with agentic applications where quality is determined by delayed, task-level outcomes. This prevents correct attribution of feedback to individual routing decisions.

Method: TRACE-Router assigns each task to a model once at admission using a contextual bandit, pins all subsequent LLM calls to that backend, and updates its policy using the task's terminal reward (jointly accounting for accuracy and latency) to leverage delayed task feedback without explicit task-complexity estimation.

Result: Across three agentic benchmarks, TRACE-Router achieves non-dominated Pareto frontier points. On tau2-Bench, it outperforms latency-matched interpolation by 7-8 accuracy points. On Terminal-Bench, it achieves 7.1 higher accuracy points than the strongest single model baseline with 36% lower latency.

Conclusion: TRACE-Router effectively aligns routing decisions with task-level supervision, learning policies that adapt to workload and consistently improve the accuracy-latency trade-off in agentic applications.

Abstract: Routing to select large language models (LLMs) with different cost-quality trade-offs has become a fundamental deployment feature of enterprise AI. Existing routers, primarily make independent routing decisions for each LLM call. However, agentic applications execute as long-horizon workflows whose quality is determined only by a delayed, task-level outcome. This mismatch prevents per-call routers from correctly attributing feedback to individual routing decisions.
