Towards mitigating this, we present TRACE-Router, a task-level routing framework that aligns routing with the unit of supervision. TRACE-Router assigns each task to a model once at admission using a contextual bandit, pins all subsequent LLM calls to the selected backend, and updates its policy using the task's terminal reward, jointly accounting for accuracy and latency. By leveraging delayed task feedback, TRACE-Router learns routing policies that adapt to the workload while avoiding explicit task-complexity estimation. Across three agentic benchmarks, TRACE-Router consistently improves the accuracy-latency trade-off, achieving non-dominated Pareto frontier points. On tau2-Bench, it outperforms latency-matched interpolation between individual models by 7-8 accuracy points, while on Terminal-Bench it achieves 7.1 higher accuracy points than the strongest single model baseline with 36% lower latency.

</details>


### [203] [The Regression Tax: Decomposing Why Skills Help and Hurt LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22520)
*Darshan Tank,Baran Nama*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Adding procedural skills to LLM agents can cause regressions (tasks that succeed without skills but fail with them), not just improvements. Analyzing nearly 6,000 runs, the paper finds top skills outperform others by regressing less, identifies three regression causes (skill description osmosis, grounding displacement, verification displacement), and shows reliability depends more on grounding and verification than procedural skill choice.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation of procedural skills in LLM agents focuses on average improvement in task success, which hides the cost of regressions—tasks that agents solve without skills but fail after skills are added. The paper aims to measure and decompose these negative effects.

Method: Comparative experiments across ~6,000 runs on two office automation benchmarks and three model stacks, comparing agents with and without skills. Failure traces were analyzed to categorize regressions and identify root causes.

Result: Regressions are substantial; best skills outperform others by regressing less, not gaining more. Three regression causes identified: skill description osmosis, grounding displacement, verification displacement. Persistent failures stem from insufficient grounding/verification support, not procedural guidance.

Conclusion: Procedural skills should be evaluated by decomposing net effect into gains and regressions. Reliability depends more on grounding and verification than on procedural skill choice. Many regressions and persistent failures are recoverable through better grounding and verification.

Abstract: Adding procedural skills to an LLM agent is typically evaluated by average improvement in task success. However, this metric hides an important cost: skills can also make agents worse. We measure both sides by comparing agents with and without skills across nearly 6,000 runs spanning two office automation benchmarks and three model harness stacks. This allows us to distinguish two outcomes. A regression is a task solved without skills but failed after skills are added. A residual failure is a task that fails both with and without skills. We find that regressions are substantial enough that the best performing skills outperform others primarily by regressing less, not by gaining more. We identify three causes of regression: (i) skill description osmosis, a skill changes an agent's behavior simply by being present in context, even when it is never invoked; (ii) grounding displacement, a skill's prescribed procedure overrides how the agent interprets its inputs; and (iii) verification displacement, where the procedure suppresses checks the agent would otherwise perform on its outputs. Analysing persistent failures reveals the same underlying pattern. Existing skills overemphasize procedural guidance the stage least often responsible for failure while under supporting grounding and verification, the dominant sources of remaining errors. After correcting evaluation artifacts and studying traces, we find many regressions and persistent failures recoverable through better grounding and verification. Procedural skills should be evaluated by decomposing their net effect into gains and regressions, not by aggregate improvement alone. We identify three regression modes skills should avoid, and find that reliability depends more on grounding and verification than on procedural skill choice.

</details>


### [204] [Explainable Reinforcement Learning for assisting Air Traffic Controllers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22525)
*Anduel Mehmeti,Gabriella Gigante,Salvatore Venticinque*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper explores explainability techniques for Reinforcement Learning in Air Traffic Control by training an RL agent to avoid no-fly zones and using saliency maps to identify key input features influencing decisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Building trust in AI for high-stakes environments (healthcare, autonomous driving, aviation) requires explainability, which remains challenging for deep learning and RL systems.

Method: An RL agent is trained in a simplified ATC environment to make route decisions avoiding no-fly zones; saliency maps are used as a preliminary explainability technique.

Result: Saliency maps provide insights into which input features most significantly influence the agent's decision-making process in the ATC scenario.

Conclusion: Explainability for RL in safety-critical domains like ATC is feasible and can support trust-building; saliency maps offer a useful starting point.

Abstract: To effectively integrate AI into high-stakes, critical environments such as healthcare, autonomous driving, and aviation--and to advance toward higher levels of automation and seamless human-AI collaboration--building trust in AI-driven solutions is essential. Trust, in turn, is closely linked to the explainability of AI systems. The rapid advancements in AI across various domains have underscored the challenges of establishing trust, raising increasing interest in AI explainability even more when applied to deep learning. In this context, the present work aims to explore the application of explainability techniques to Reinforcement Learning (RL) algorithms, specifically within the safety-critical domain of Air Traffic Control (ATC). Using a simplified ATC environment as an initial testbed, an intelligent agent is trained with a reinforcement learning algorithm to make decisions on alternative flight routes that avoid no-fly zones. As a preliminary explainability approach, a saliency map is employed, providing insights into the input features that most significantly influence the agent's decision-making process.

</details>


<div id='cs.GR'></div>

# cs.GR [[Back]](#toc)

### [205] [Curly Hair Simulation using Curly Finite Elements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22103)
*Xinming Pei,Zhendong Wang,Lei Lan,Weiwei Xu,Yin Yang,Huamin Wang*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: A curly hair simulation model that decomposes strands into rod-base configurations with analytical high-frequency wrinkles (waves/helices), using curvature-energy splitting and hybrid collision handling for efficient and visually faithful simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Realistic simulation of curly hair is challenging due to the tight coupling between macroscopic strand deformation and high-frequency geometric details such as waves and helices.

Method: Decomposes each strand into curly elements with a rod-base configuration and analytically defined high-frequency wrinkles; uses curvature-energy splitting to separate stretching, buckling, and bending contributions; introduces hybrid collision handling combining coarse proxies with analytical detail treatment; adapts guide-strand interpolation to the curly finite-element representation.

Result: Stable, efficient, and visually faithful simulation of curly hair across diverse scenarios, with improved numerical robustness and reduced computational cost without degrading visual fidelity.

Conclusion: The proposed curly hair model effectively decouples macroscopic deformation from high-frequency geometric details, enabling practical and visually convincing curly hair simulation.

Abstract: Realistic simulation of curly hair is challenging due to the tight coupling between macroscopic strand deformation and high-frequency geometric details such as waves and helices. In this paper, we propose a curly hair model that decomposes each strand into curly elements, consisting of a rod-base configuration and an analytically defined high-frequency wrinkles represented by planar waves or volumetric helices, with deformation governed primarily by bending for wavy hair and twisting for spiral hair. A curvature-energy splitting scheme separates stretching, buckling, and bending contributions, and efficient energy approximations improve numerical robustness and reduce computational cost without degrading visual fidelity. We further introduce a hybrid collision handling strategy that combines coarse collision proxies for the base configuration with analytical treatment of high-frequency details, and adapt guide-strand interpolation to our curly finite-element representation to generate dense hair while preserving fine structure. Experiments demonstrate stable, efficient, and visually faithful simulation of curly hair across diverse scenarios.

</details>


### [206] [Loom: Multi-Region Analysis of Spatial Transcriptomics with Local Neighborhoods and Global Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22505)
*Siyuan Zhao,Nafiul Nipu,Hossein Fathollahian,Olga Karginova,Hao Chen,Ameen Salahudeen,G. Elisabeta Marai*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: Loom is a visual computing system for spatial transcriptomics that enables analysis of pseudo-temporal trajectories, cross-sample comparisons, and local microenvironment investigation through a novel glyph design and computational backbone, validated via case studies and usability testing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spatial transcriptomics data analysis is challenged by multi-modal registration issues, complex pseudo-temporal patterns, spatial enrichment data, and gene expression dynamics, requiring integration with cell reference datasets and temporal simulations for practical applications.

Method: Loom employs a novel glyph coupled with a computational backbone to facilitate detailed pseudo-temporal exploration, cross-sample comparisons, and investigation of spatiotemporal biological mechanisms in local microenvironments.

Result: Two case studies with tissue pathology experts and oncologists, plus an external usability study, demonstrated that Loom effectively supports discovery of cellular transitions and spatiotemporal expression dynamics.

Conclusion: Loom successfully addresses spatial transcriptomics analysis challenges by providing an integrated visual computing approach for pseudo-temporal and spatial exploration of microenvironments.

Abstract: We present Loom, a spatial transcriptomics (ST) visual computing system to support the analysis of pseudo-temporal trajectories, comparative investigation across samples and regions of interest, and the examination of spatially structured processes within local microenvironments. ST is a molecular profiling technology that measures gene expression directly within a thin tissue section while preserving its spatial organization. For practical application-driven analyses, the ST local microenvironment data needs to be integrated with cell reference datasets and temporal simulations of cell behavior. This integration is challenging due to multi-modal registration issues and the complexity of the pseudo-temporal patterns, spatial enrichment data, and gene expression dynamics. Loom leverages a novel glyph coupled with a computational backbone to facilitate the detailed pseudo-temporal exploration of local microenvironments, cross-sample comparisons, and investigation of spatiotemporal biological mechanisms. We evaluate Loom through two case studies developed with experts in tissue pathology and oncologists and through an external usability study. The results demonstrate that Loom supports effectively the discovery of cellular transitions and spatiotemporal expression dynamics.

</details>


<div id='cs.SD'></div>

# cs.SD [[Back]](#toc)

### [207] [Probing Speaker Identity Sensitivity in Audio Deepfake Detectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21820)
*Daniyal Kabir Dar,Arun Ross*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: The paper identifies speaker-identity reliance as a key factor in audio deepfake detector failures across datasets and proposes the Identity Sensitivity Score (ISS), an inference-time diagnostic that measures detector output changes across speaker identity contexts, achieving high predictive accuracy for misclassification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Audio deepfake detectors show dramatic performance degradation (e.g., error rate increasing twentyfold) when evaluated on different datasets. The authors hypothesize this is partly due to speaker-identity reliance, where detectors exploit spurious correlations between speaker identity and genuine/synthetic labels in training data rather than focusing solely on synthesis artifacts.

Method: The authors propose the Identity Sensitivity Score (ISS), a per-utterance metric computed from the detector score and a pool of reference speaker examples, requiring no ground-truth labels at inference time. They validate ISS across two detectors and two datasets, and further test whether ISS captures identity sensitivity (vs. being a confidence proxy) by applying voice conversion to 500 utterances and measuring detector-score shifts.

Result: Incorrectly classified utterances have ISS scores 29-52 times higher than correctly classified ones. ISS predicts misclassification with AUC up to 0.954. Utterances flagged as identity-sensitive by ISS respond 19-30 times more strongly to voice conversion manipulation than stable utterances.

Conclusion: ISS is a practical, inference-time diagnostic for speaker-dependent failure analysis in audio deepfake detection, effectively identifying utterances where detectors rely on speaker-related cues rather than genuine synthetic artifacts.

Abstract: Audio deepfake detectors are trained to distinguish genuine speech from synthetic speech and often perform well on standard benchmarks. Yet the same detector that achieves less than 1% error on one dataset can see its error rate increase twentyfold when evaluated on a different dataset. We argue that one contributing factor is speaker-identity reliance: standard training corpora correlate speaker identity with the genuine/synthetic label, allowing detectors to partially rely on speaker-related cues rather than synthesis artifacts alone. We propose the Identity Sensitivity Score (ISS), a per-utterance diagnostic that quantifies how much a detector's output changes across different speaker identity contexts. ISS requires no ground-truth labels at inference time and can be computed from the detector score and a pool of reference speaker examples. Across two detectors and two datasets, incorrectly classified utterances have ISS scores 29 to 52 times higher than correctly classified utterances, and ISS alone predicts misclassification with area-under-curve (AUC) up to 0.954. To test whether ISS actually captures identity-sensitive behavior rather than serving only as a proxy for prediction confidence, we apply voice conversion to 500 utterances and measure the resulting detector-score shift. Utterances flagged as identity-sensitive by ISS respond 19 to 30 times more strongly to this manipulation than utterances flagged as stable. These results position ISS as a practical inference-time diagnostic for speaker-dependent failure analysis in audio deepfake detection.

</details>


### [208] [SoundscapeAgent: Agentic Soundscape Construction for Controllable Synthesis and Scalable Audio-Language Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21857)
*Hao Zhang,Yiwen Zhao,Yixuan Zhang,Yiwen Shao,Steve Yves*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper introduces an agentic soundscape construction framework that decomposes controllable audio generation into explicit planning, source selection, temporal layout, and rendering steps using an LLM-based agent, achieving competitive performance against text-to-audio baselines and demonstrating improved downstream audio reasoning with agent-generated data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional text-to-audio models handle soundscape generation implicitly as a single-shot process, lacking controllability, inspectability, and reusability. This work aims to provide a structured, modular framework that makes each pipeline step explicit and supports human-in-the-loop interaction.

Method: An LLM-based agent converts user intent into an executable scene plan, then retrieves or generates audio assets on demand, renders controllable multi-event mixtures with temporal layout, and exports aligned metadata. The framework also supports user-guided tool selection and editable scene plans for human-in-the-loop interaction.

Result: Listener studies and objective metrics show competitive generation performance compared to text-to-audio baselines. Models trained with agent-generated data consistently outperform models trained on real-only data in downstream audio reasoning tasks.

Conclusion: The proposed agentic framework provides an inspectable and reusable approach to controllable soundscape synthesis and scalable audio-language data construction, with demonstrated advantages in both generation quality and downstream task performance.

Abstract: We present an agentic soundscape construction framework for controllable compositional audio generation that makes explicit the scene planning, source selection, temporal layout, and rendering steps typically handled implicitly by single-shot text-to-audio models. An LLM-based agent converts user intent into an executable scene plan, acquires assets through retrieval and on-demand generation, renders controllable multi-event mixtures, and exports aligned scene metadata. The framework also supports human-in-the-loop interaction through user-guided tool selection and editable scene plans. Together, these components provide an inspectable and reusable approach to controllable soundscape synthesis and scalable audio-language data construction. Listener studies and objective metrics demonstrate competitive generation performance against text-to-audio baselines, while models trained with agent-generated data consistently outperform real-only baselines in downstream audio reasoning. Code, demos, and listening-test results are available at https://haozhang6720.github.io/SoundscapeAgentDemoPage/.

</details>


### [209] [CODA: Cascaded Online Discontinuity-Aware Alignment for Real-Time Image-Based Score Following](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21899)
*Yining Yang,Ruogu Chen,Jie Han*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: CODA is the first real-time score following system that enforces cascaded prediction consistency (system→bar→note) across musical notation levels and introduces a silence-driven break mode to recover from score discontinuities (repeats, D.C., coda jumps), achieving SOTA accuracy on MSMD piano benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing image-based score following systems suffer from unstable multi-resolution predictions due to independent predictions across notation levels, and lack mechanisms to recover from score discontinuities like repeats, D.C., or coda jumps.

Method: CODA enforces cascaded structure prediction: first selects the active system, then the bar within it, then the note within the bar, ensuring consistency across resolutions. A silence-driven break mode enables recovery from arbitrary score discontinuities without prior knowledge of repeat structures.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art tracking accuracy and discontinuity-recovery performance on MSMD piano benchmarks under real-time throughput.

Conclusion: The cascaded prediction approach and silence-driven break mode effectively address stability and discontinuity recovery challenges in real-time score following.

Abstract: Real-time score following from sheet images remains chal- lenging because the model must process streaming au- dio while resolving highly repetitive visual patterns un- der strict latency constraints. Recent image-based meth- ods have attempted to use multi-resolution prediction by simultaneously predicting the positions of the active sys- tem, bar, and note. However, their predictions across these different levels of notation are independent, which makes the predictions unstable and introduces unnecessary ex- tra search space for bar- and note-level predictions. Most existing methods also lack mechanisms to recover from score discontinuities, such as repeats, da capo (D.C.), or coda jumps. This paper proposes CODA, to the best of our knowledge, the first real-time score following system that addresses both gaps. CODA explicitly exploits the cascaded structure of music scores: it first selects the ac- tive system, then the active bar within it, and finally the active note within the selected bar. This enforces pre- diction consistency across resolutions. A silence-driven break mode enables recovery from arbitrary score discon- tinuities without requiring knowledge of the repeat struc- ture. Evaluated on the Multimodal Sheet Music Dataset (MSMD) piano benchmarks, CODA achieves state-of-the- art tracking accuracy and discontinuity-recovery perfor- mance under real-time throughput. Code is available at https://github.com/ValleyC/CODA.

</details>


### [210] [Listen, Do Not Copy: Internalizing Audio-Grounded Scaffold Context for Robust Omni-Model Speech Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21943)
*Pengfei Zhang,Biao Tian,Tianxin Xie,Minghao Yang,Xiangang Li,Li Liu*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: Omni models fail on overlapping noisy speech. Using scene descriptions as clues causes "perception bypass" (models copy text instead of listening). The authors propose Audio-Grounded Scaffold Context (AGSC): clues derived from audio that guide training but vanish at test time, plus a GDPO task for streaming control. Results: mpWER drops from 25%-71% to 9%-15% across three models with minimal inference overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Omni models perform poorly on overlapping, noisy multi-speaker speech where speaker attribution matters most. A naive fix (providing scene description text) introduces a shortcut: models copy answer-bearing text instead of actually listening, inflating scores.

Method: (1) AGSC: audio-derived clues that guide listening without revealing answers; (2) Answer-overlap and silence tests detect leakage/audio dependence; (3) Clues used during training but removed at test time (no-clue capability); (4) Joint GDPO task for streaming control: learns when to use a clue and how to produce speaker-attributed transcripts from format, gate, and transcript rewards.

Result: Across three heterogeneous Omni models, AGSC reduces no-clue capped mpWER on overlapping noisy speech from 25%-71% down to 9%-15%. Near-zero inference overhead after internalization.

Conclusion: AGSC effectively prevents perception bypass by decoupling training clues from test-time input, enabling robust multi-speaker transcription without the shortcut risk of text-based scene descriptions.

Abstract: Omni models transcribe clean, single-speaker speech well, but their accuracy drops sharply when speakers overlap and the scene is noisy, exactly where knowing who said what matters most. A natural fix is a short scene description. We show why this is risky: answer-bearing text lets the model copy instead of listen, so the score rises although nothing has been heard; a silent test exposes this shortcut at once. We call this failure mode perception bypass and address it with Audio-Grounded Scaffold Context (AGSC). AGSC links three steps: first, we build clues from audio to guide listening without giving the answer; second, answer-overlap and silence tests probe them for leakage and audio dependence; finally, those clues scaffold training but vanish at test time, yielding no-clue capability. Across three heterogeneous Omni models, training on AGSC lowers no-clue capped mean permutation word error rate (mpWER) on overlapping, noisy speech from 25%-71% to 9%-15%. For streaming control, we formulate a joint GDPO task in which the model learns when to use a clue and how to produce a speaker-attributed transcript from separately normalized format, gate, and transcript rewards. After internalization, AGSC adds almost no inference overhead.

</details>


### [211] [Music-JEPA: Learning a World Model of Sound from Action](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22000)
*Ziyu Wang,Kun Fang,Yann LeCun*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper proposes learning a world model for piano sound using Joint Embedding Predictive Architecture (JEPA) by treating music as an action-conditioned system (audio as state, pianoroll as action), enabling prediction of future audio states from current audio and actions. The model is trained offline on paired data and supports downstream tasks including beat tracking, composer identification, key estimation, and piano transcription via planning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While JEPA has shown promise for learning world models via latent prediction, it remains unclear how such frameworks can naturally support world model formation for the music domain. The authors aim to address this gap.

Method: The authors frame music as an action-conditioned system where audio is the state and pianoroll is the instrument action. Using JEPA, the model learns to predict future audio states in latent space given a current audio state and an action. Training is fully offline using paired audio-pianoroll data with no environment interaction.

Result: The learned model captures relationships between musical actions and their resulting sounds. Representations support beat tracking, composer identification, and key estimation. Additionally, the model enables piano transcription via planning by searching for actions that best explain a target sound.

Conclusion: JEPA can be effectively adapted to learn a world model for piano sound by treating music as an action-conditioned system, where the model learns causal relationships between actions (pianoroll) and audio outcomes, supporting both representation learning and planning-based downstream tasks.

Abstract: Joint Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPA) have recently emerged as a paradigm for learning world models by predicting latent representations, offering a promising direction for self-supervised learning. While initial attempts have applied JEPA to the music domain, it remains unclear how such frameworks can naturally support the formation of a world model for music. In this work, we propose to learn a world model of piano sound using JEPA by framing music as an action-conditioned system: the audio is treated as the state, and the pianoroll as the instrument action. Given a current audio state and an action, the model predicts the resulting future audio state, mirroring how humans learn musical sound through interaction. The model is trained in a fully offline setting using paired audio-pianoroll data, without environment interaction. Experiments show that the learned model captures the relationships between musical actions and their resulting sound. The resulting representations support downstream tasks, including beat tracking, composer identification, and key estimation, and enable piano transcription via planning, by searching for actions that best explain a target sound.

</details>


### [212] [MemNMF: Memory-Augmented NMF on LPC Spectra for Anomalous Sound Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22086)
*Phurich Saengthong,Takahiro Shinozaki*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: MemNMF proposes using Linear Predictive Coding (LPC) spectra combined with a memory-augmented NMF-based reconstruction method for robust anomalous sound detection in machinery, showing improved performance and noise robustness over standard spectrogram autoencoders.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard spectrogram autoencoders for anomalous sound detection are sensitive to noise and transients, and can reconstruct anomalous inputs well, weakening normal-anomaly separation.

Method: MemNMF operates on LPC spectra (compact spectral envelope estimates) instead of full spectrograms. It initializes a memory module from an NMF dictionary learned on normal LPC spectra and reconstructs each input as an attention-weighted combination of prototypical normal spectral patterns.

Result: Experiments on MIMII and DCASE 2020 Task 2 across multiple machine types and operating conditions show LPC-spectrum inputs improve a standard autoencoder baseline, and MemNMF yields further gains with especially strong robustness under noisy, non-stationary settings.

Conclusion: MemNMF with LPC spectra is more robust and effective for anomalous sound detection than standard spectrogram autoencoder approaches, particularly in challenging noisy environments.

Abstract: Autoencoder-based anomalous sound detection is attractive for machine condition monitoring because it can be trained using only normal recordings and yields an interpretable anomaly score from reconstruction error. Most prior work uses spectrogram autoencoders, but reconstructing detailed time--frequency patterns is sensitive to noise and transients, and models can reconstruct some anomalous inputs well, weakening normal--anomaly separation. We propose MemNMF, a constrained reconstruction method that operates on the Linear Predictive Coding spectrum, a compact estimate of the spectral envelope. MemNMF initializes a memory module from an NMF dictionary learned on normal LPC spectra and reconstructs each input as an attention-weighted combination of prototypical normal spectral patterns. Experiments on MIMII and DCASE 2020 Task 2 across multiple machine types and operating conditions show that LPC-spectrum inputs improve a standard autoencoder baseline and that MemNMF yields further gains, with especially strong robustness under noisy, non-stationary settings.

</details>


### [213] [Reflector: Arrangement-Aware Harmonic Retrieval for Sample-Based Composition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22413)
*Austin Rockman*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: Reflector is an interactive audio workstation that tracks harmonic context as arrangements evolve, using a fixed interval-class oracle approximated by a learned embedding for adaptive sample retrieval.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing sample retrieval tools query from fixed reference samples, which become less informative as arrangements evolve and harmonic context shifts with each musical decision.

Method: A hand-designed interval-class oracle table scores pitch-class compatibility; an encoder trained on synthetic audio approximates this oracle in a 128-D embedding space. A sweep-line analysis on the multi-track timeline computes oracle-weighted centroids for adaptive retrieval. Session centroids are projected into a navigable 3-D space.

Result: The learned embedding preserves the kernel's pairwise judgments while covering the entire library, overcoming the kernel's degenerate solutions when used directly. The system runs locally with no copyrighted training data.

Conclusion: Reflector effectively adapts sample retrieval to evolving harmonic contexts, with the embedding providing a normalized geometry that avoids degenerate solutions of direct scoring. The system is free and open-source.

Abstract: Sample retrieval tools can help composers find harmonically compatible material, but querying from a fixed reference sample becomes less informative as arrangements evolve and the harmonic context shifts with each musical decision. We present Reflector, an interactive audio workstation that tracks harmonic combinations as they accumulate on the composer's timeline and adapts retrieval as the arrangement develops. The system is organized around a fixed interval-class oracle: a hand-designed table of weights that scores how pitch-class content combines between sources. An encoder trained entirely on synthetic audio learns to approximate the oracle in a 128-dimensional embedding space, where dot products stand in for compatibility scores at interactive speed. As the composer arranges material on a multi-track timeline, a sweep-line analysis discovers co-sounding regions, computes oracle-weighted centroids, and retrieves against the composite harmonic identity of the session as it evolves. Session centroids projected into a navigable 3-D space reveal structural harmonic relations across the composer's body of work. This paper is a systems account: we give the design rationale for each architectural decision, characterize Reflector's behavior through intrinsic measurements on a working sample library, and describe the implementation. The characterization yields a central finding: the learned embedding preserves the kernel's pairwise judgments while covering the whole library, something the kernel cannot do when used directly as a retrieval rule, because the embedding's normalized geometry cannot express the degenerate solutions that direct scoring favors. The entire pipeline runs locally with no copyrighted training data. Reflector is free, and the training pipeline is open source.

</details>
